# Preface.

This book is dedicated to the memory of my esteemed teacher **H. BROCKHAUS** <note>Hermann Brockhaus (1806–1877) was a renowned German scholar of Oriental languages and Sanskrit; here, he is credited with sparking the author's interest in Celtic history.</note>, to whom I owe my initial inspiration for **Celtic studies** <term>Celtic studies: The academic discipline concerned with the languages, literature, and cultural history of Celtic-speaking peoples.</term>. When I departed in 1870 for a lengthy stay in London, which was primarily arranged by him, he strongly urged me to also direct my attention toward the **Ossian question** <note>The "Ossian question" refers to the intense 18th and 19th-century debate over whether the "Ossian" poems, published by James Macpherson, were authentic ancient Gaelic works or modern inventions.</note>. The second person to whom I am personally deeply indebted in this field of my studies is my friend **STANDISH HAYES O’GRADY** <note>Standish Hayes O'Grady (1832–1915) was an influential Irish scholar and civil engineer known for his monumental work in translating medieval Irish manuscripts, such as 'Silva Gadelica'.</note>. He assisted me in the most gracious and selfless manner through the first difficulties of the language and the manuscripts, introduced me to the world of ancient sagas, and supported me—even to his own deprivation—with books and his own valuable transcripts. I have not forgotten the Ossian question. What is scattered in various places throughout this book, in the introductory remarks to individual texts, I have sought to unite into a comprehensive overview in my lecture **"On the Old Irish Saga and the Ossian Question"** <note>original: "Ueber die altirische Sage und die Ossianfrage"</note>, which was delivered at the 1878 Assembly of Philologists in **Gera** and printed in their proceedings (published by B. G. Teubner). If I place a particular value on the demonstration attempted there regarding how the poetic figure of Ossian originated, it is because this is intended as a response, so to speak, to the request with which **BROCKHAUS** once sent me on my way.

<summary>In this preface, the author credits Hermann Brockhaus and Standish Hayes O'Grady for their mentorship and assistance in his Celtic research. He highlights his work on the "Ossian question," connecting his findings to a lecture delivered in 1078.</summary>

<keywords>Hermann Brockhaus, Standish Hayes O'Grady, Ossian question, Celtic studies, Old Irish sagas, Philologists' Assembly, Gera, manuscript research</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the preface by Ernst Windisch, following his acknowledgement of his teachers and his interest in the "Ossian question." It transitions into a discussion of the technical preparation and editorial principles of the current volume of Irish texts.</meta>

# Preface.

The first part of the present work was already fully printed three years ago. In that section, I gathered such texts as I was able to provide at that time. Today, now that the **Book of Leinster** <note>The *Book of Leinster* (Lebor Laignech) is a massive 12th-century Irish manuscript; a facsimile edition had recently been published when Windisch wrote this.</note> is also available in facsimile, the selection might have turned out differently, though I do not wish this to be seen as a criticism of my compilation from that time. One can rely upon the wording of the texts. I have supervised the printing with the greatest possible care; the errors that occur—aside from certain inconsistencies in the separation of words—consist of incorrect expansions of abbreviations in the manuscripts. I do not believe that anything substantial has remained uncorrected, nor do I believe that a further **collation** <term>collation: the process of comparing a printed text against the original manuscript to ensure accuracy</term> of the originals would yield significant results. Admittedly, in the interest of the book, it was necessary to reprint two and a half sheets (pages 257–280 and 321–336), because I fortunately discovered at the last minute that the collation of **H.** <note>Windisch refers to manuscript H. 2. 16, known as the *Yellow Book of Lecan*.</note> for the ***The Wasting Sickness of Cú Chulainn*** <note>original: "Serglige Conculaind," a famous tale from the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology.</note> and the collation of **Eg.** <note>Egerton 93, a manuscript held in the British Library.</note> for the ***The Feast of Bricriu*** <note>original: "Fled Bricrend," a Middle Irish story concerning a competition among heroes.</note> did not reflect the manuscript readings with the desired accuracy.

In the texts, it was my aim to separate the combinations usually written together in the **manuscripts** <note>original: "Handschriften"</note>, such as the article and noun, preposition and noun, verbal particle and verbal form, and so on—and to do so without overloading the print with small dashes and other symbols. In the later written language, it is customary to print all formally independent elements of the sentence separately. In the older language as well, I recommend separation as a principle wherever possible, without breaking apart visible **phonetic fusions** <term>phonetic fusions: instances where the sounds of two words merge into one (sandhi), which Windisch argues should sometimes be preserved in spelling.</term> in such a way that forms are created which are otherwise unusual. Accordingly, one should write *na mac* (of the sons) separately, for example, but not the identical *nammac*, because *nam* is not an independent form; one should write separately...

<summary>The author explains the editorial history of the volume, noting that new manuscript facsimiles became available during production and detailing his methods for word separation in Old Irish.</summary>
<keywords>Book of Leinster, manuscript collation, The Wasting Sickness of Cú Chulainn, The Feast of Bricriu, Old Irish grammar, word separation</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues Ernst Windisch's preface, moving from his editorial methods regarding manuscript collation to specific linguistic choices, his treatment of previous scholarship, and the nature of Middle Irish.</meta>

VII

-># Preface.<-

is *at the feats* <note>original: "oc na clessaib"</note>, but not *at the pig* <note>original: "ocon muicc"</note>, because *on* is not an independent form of the article. The "eclipsis" <term>eclipsis: a linguistic feature in Celtic languages where the initial consonant of a word is suppressed or "eclipsed" by a preceding nasal sound</term> is indicated just as in the modern language: for example, *na n-dáine* (of the people), *na m-ban* (of the women). In such cases, the manuscripts often have a dot—and not only over the guttural *n*—but I have not retained these dots. On the other hand, I have still employed the small dash before certain, often untranslatable, enclitically attached particles—the so-called *emphasizing particles* <note>original Latin: "Particulae augentes"</note>—in order to distinguish them from the full-weighted demonstrative pronoun. Admittedly, however, it is actually an inconsistency to write *ind fir sea* (these men).

For some texts, I have included the translations of native scholars for difficult passages. My intention was to show the extent of understanding within these circles, so that one might not imagine they could extract much more from those rare and difficult-to-obtain books than what I provide here. These native scholars translated everything without hesitation, as if no difficulties existed at all. <note>Windisch is likely referring to 19th-century Irish antiquarians whose enthusiasm sometimes outpaced their philological precision compared to the burgeoning "scientific" German school of Celtic studies.</note>

The language of the manuscripts from which the present texts are taken is called **Middle Irish**. This is not a clearly definable, internally uniform language. The fact that one can perhaps detect traces of different dialects here and there is far less important than the fact that numerous ancient forms and words have been preserved in the middle of the language of a later period. In general, every scribe rewrote his original into the language of his own time, but did so quite inconsistently; thus, even in late texts, we can encounter fragments of an ancient vocabulary. For example, I recently found in the **Egerton version** of the ***The Feast of Bricriu*** <note>original: "Fled Bricrend"</note> (see p. 336, regarding p. 292, 3) the previously unknown *fathar* (of you/your) <note>original: "fathar vestrum"</note>, which stands nicely alongside the long-known *nathar* (of us/our) <note>original: "nathar nostrum"</note>.

<summary>Windisch explains his orthographic choices for Irish grammar, his critical view of earlier "native" translations, and defines Middle Irish as a transitional language containing layers of both ancient and contemporary forms.</summary>
<keywords>Middle Irish, orthography, eclipsis, emphasizing particles, philology, Fled Bricrend, Egerton manuscript, linguistic evolution</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues Ernst Windisch's preface to his "Irische Texte." Having discussed the linguistic peculiarities of Middle Irish on the previous page, he now shifts to the cultural importance of the Irish sagas and the practical difficulties of compiling the accompanying dictionary.</meta>

VIII

-># Preface.<-

Regarding the high value of the Irish saga, I have spoken more extensively in the aforementioned lecture. I stand by my position that the Irish saga is the only abundantly flowing source of unbroken Celticism <note>German: "Celtenthum". In the 19th century, this referred to the study of the ancient Celtic peoples, their spirit, and their cultural continuity.</note>. What a wealth of interesting features O’CURRY <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862) was a foundational Irish scholar whose "Lectures on the Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History" were essential to the 19th-century Celtic revival.</note> has drawn from this source in his *Lectures*, without exhausting it! In Germany, circles of anthropologists and antiquarians have always been particularly interested in Celtic culture. I refer them, for example, to the information shared in the dictionary under *carn* <note>original: "carn" — a cairn or mound of stones, often marking a burial site or boundary.</note>. Where else can such details be found?

The dictionary was a very laborious task. Even during the proofreading stage, I sought to reduce the number of question marks. I have placed such a mark not only where I had no solution, but also wherever I was not certain of my case; and I wish that criticism would focus on these question marks, instead of perhaps quarreling with me over the arrangement or other subordinate points. Aside from the unresolved words, the dictionary contains nearly 7,000 articles or distinct words whose meanings are essentially secured. I have not limited myself solely to my own texts, particularly as the work progressed; rather, I was compelled by the numerous parallel passages I brought in to expand the scope of my sources. This was a never-ending task <note>original German: "Schraube ohne Ende," literally "a screw without end."</note>, and the printing process finally had to determine the point of conclusion. Particularly for the first letters of the alphabet, the number of addenda and new words is very large. Much of this I owe to my esteemed friend WHITLEY STOKES <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909) was a prominent Irish lawyer and philologist who worked in colonial India while producing massive volumes of edited Celtic texts.</note>. He had the kindness to read the galley proofs and to send me his valuable remarks as quickly as was possible from *Calcutta* or *Simla*. Had I not been forced to have two and a half sheets reprinted, as mentioned above, I would have shared the most important of these addenda even now.

<sig>2</sig>

<summary>Windisch emphasizes the unique cultural value of Irish sagas for understanding ancient Celtic society and describes the immense labor of compiling his 7,000-entry Irish dictionary with help from Whitley Stokes.</summary>
<keywords>Irish sagas, Eugene O'Curry, Celtic culture, lexicography, Whitley Stokes, Middle Irish dictionary, antiquarianism</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues Ernst Windisch's preface to his "Irische Texte." On the previous page, he discussed the cultural value of Irish sagas and his methodology for compiling the dictionary. Here, he addresses the practicalities of publication and his focus on Middle Irish.</meta>

IX

-># Preface.<-

Since, however, it appeared desirable for various reasons to publish the book still within this year, I must save the supplements for a future publication, which will then provide an even richer contribution to a complete collection of the Irish vocabulary <note>original: "Wortschatz". Windisch is referring to the ongoing effort to document the entirety of the Irish language, which was still in its early stages of modern scholarship.</note>. The present book forms a self-contained whole in itself, as its texts have been fully indexed for the dictionary. We possess such excellent dictionaries in various linguistic fields that in a new work of this kind, every merit is easily taken for granted, while every deficiency is felt to be unpardonable. I ask that in judging my achievement, one takes not an absolute, but a historical perspective <note>Windisch is asking readers to judge his work based on the limited tools available in 1880, rather than comparing it to the perfected dictionaries of established languages like Latin or Greek.</note>. My resources can be seen in the individual articles. O'REILLY's *Irish-English Dictionary* with O'DONOVAN's *Supplement* (Dublin 1864) <note>Edward O'Reilly (d. 1830) and John O'Donovan (d. 1861) were pioneers in Irish lexicography, though their work was often based on later manuscripts.</note> was very useful to me, even if O'REILLY alone, because of his many errors and inaccuracies, must never be regarded as a reliable source of instruction. O'CLERY's small glossary <note>Míchél Ó Cléirigh's 1643 glossary of difficult Irish words.</note> was unfortunately not at my disposal; I am pleased to learn that the next issue of the *Revue Celtique* <note>The "Celtic Review," a prominent scholarly journal founded in 1870.</note> will bring a new reprint of it.

To make my book easily accessible to foreign scholars, I have provided the meaning of the word in English or Latin <note>original: "englisch oder lateinisch". By using Latin and English alongside German, Windisch ensured that scholars across Europe could use his dictionary regardless of their native tongue.</note> for almost every entry. The *Grammatica Celtica* <note>The foundational 1853 work by Johann Kaspar Zeuss that established the scientific study of Celtic languages.</note> and the Old Irish vocabulary of the glosses are not fully utilized; least of all have all forms of the same word been included. My main focus is directed toward the language of Middle Irish literature; Old Irish has been for me only a starting point and an aid. It is my view that the entire Old Irish vocabulary from the glosses should be combined into a lexical work of its own. While I leave this beautiful task to others, I shall continue to work on the vocabulary of Middle Irish literature...

<summary>Windisch explains the limitations and focus of his dictionary, noting that while he utilized existing resources like O'Reilly's dictionary, his primary goal is documenting the language of Middle Irish sagas rather than Old Irish glosses.</summary>
<keywords>Ernst Windisch, Irish lexicography, Edward O'Reilly, John O'Donovan, Míchél Ó Cléirigh, Middle Irish, Old Irish, Grammatica Celtica, Revue Celtique</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page concludes Ernst Windisch's preface. The previous page discussed his lexicographical methods and sources; here, he outlines his future research plans and expresses a desire for collaboration with fellow scholars like Professor Hennessy.</meta>

X

# Preface.

...to work, and to make these themselves more accessible. During my stay in London and Dublin this year, I have had the opportunity to collect much material, and will gradually publish—partly from this material and partly from the beautiful facsimiles of the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre"; an 11th-century manuscript, the oldest extant in Irish.</note> and the *Book of Leinster* <note>original: "Buch von Leinster"; a major 12th-century Irish manuscript.</note>—the *Cattle Raid of Cooley* <note>original: "Táin Bó Cúailnge"; the central epic of the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology.</note> and the smaller *Raids* <note>original: "Táins"</note>, along with other texts related to the *Cúchulainn cycle* <note>original: "Cúchulinnsage"; the body of medieval Irish heroic legends focused on the hero Cúchulainn.</note>. How wonderful it would be if Professor Hennessy’s <note>William Maunsell Hennessy (1829–1889), a prominent Irish scholar and editor of ancient texts.</note> long-promised edition of *The Destruction of Da Derga's Hostel* <note>original: "Togail Bruidne Dá Derga"</note> were available to me for this purpose!

->Leipzig, November 3, 1880.<-

**E. Windisch.**

<summary>Ernst Windisch concludes his preface by detailing his upcoming projects involving the Cúchulainn sagas and emphasizing the importance of manuscript facsimiles and scholarly collaboration.</summary>
<keywords>Ernst Windisch, Táin Bó Cúailnge, Book of the Dun Cow, Book of Leinster, Cúchulainn, William Hennessy, Irish mythology, Leipzig</keywords>

# Abbreviations.

*AD., Aid. Chonch.* *The Death of Conchobar* <note>original: "Aided Chonchobair"</note> (from the *Book of Leinster*), edited by O'Curry, *Manuscript Materials*, page 637.
*Ad. Pr.* *Adamnán's Prayer*, *Goidelica* (2nd edition), page 173.
*AE., Aid. Ech.* *The Death of Eochaid* <note>original: "Aided Echach"</note> (from the *Book of the Dun Cow*), edited by Crowe, *Journal* 1870, page 94.
*Aen. Carm.* *The Fair of Carman* <note>original: "Aenach Carmain"</note>, see *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume III, page 526.
*Amr.* *The Eulogy of Saint Columba* <note>original: "Amra Choluimb Chille"</note>, *Irish Liber Hymnorum* edition by Stokes, *Goidelica* (2nd edition), page 156; *Book of the Dun Cow* edition by Crowe, Dublin 1871.
*Beitr.* *Contributions to Comparative Language Research* <note>original: "Beiträge zur Vergleichenden Sprachforschung"</note>.
*Cam.* *The Cambrai Codex* <note>original: "Codex Camaracensis"</note>, see *Zeuss's Grammatica Celtica* (2nd edition), page 1004.
*CC.* see Text V <note>Referring to the fifth text in this volume.</note>.
*CCn.* *The Cause of the Battle of Cnucha* <note>original: "Fotha Catha Cnucha"</note> (from the *Book of the Dun Cow*), edited by Windisch, *Irish Grammar*, page 121, and edited by Hennessy, *Revue Celtique* Volume II, page 86.
*Chron. Scot.* *Chronicle of the Scots* <note>original: "Chronicum Scotorum"</note>, edited by Hennessy, London 1866.
*Cl. Fiachr.* *The Genealogies, Tribes, and Customs of Hy-Fiachrach*, edited by O'Donovan, Dublin 1844.
*Corm.* *Cormac's Glossary* <note>original: "Sanas Cormaic"</note>, in *Three Irish Glossaries* by Whitley Stokes, London 1862.
*Corm. Transl.* *Cormac's Glossary*, translated by J. O'Donovan, edited by Whitley Stokes, Calcutta 1868.
*Cr.* *Bede's Karlsruhe Codex* <note>original: "Bedae Codex Carolisruhensis"</note> (see *Zeuss's Grammatica Celtica*, 2nd edition).
*Crith Gabl.* *The Branched Purchase* <note>original: "Crith Gablach"; an 8th-century Irish law tract concerning social status and property.</note>, see *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume III, page 465.
*EC.* *The Adventure of Connla the Fair* <note>original: "Echtra Condla Chaim"</note>, edited by Windisch, *Irish Grammar*, page 118; edited by Crowe, *Journal of the Royal Historical and Archaeological Association of Ireland* 1874, page 118.
*Eg.* Egerton Manuscript in the British Museum.
*FA.* see Text VII.
*FB.* see Text IX.
*FC., Fís Cath.* *The Vision of Cathair Mór* <note>original: "Fís Cathair Móir"</note>, edited by Crowe, *Journal of the Royal Historical and Archaeological Association of Ireland* 1872, page 26.
*Fél.* *The Calendar of Oengus* <note>original: "Félire Oengusso"</note>, used by me initially from the facsimile of the *Speckled Book* <note>original: "Leabhar Breac"</note>, and later from the edition by Stokes: *On the Calendar of Oengus*, Dublin 1880.
*FF.* *The Fight of Ferdiad* (from the *Book of Leinster*), see *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume III, page 413.

<meta>This page serves as the formal introduction to the first major section of the work, moving from the physical description of the manuscript to the academic analysis of the Old Irish hymns themselves.</meta>

# I.
## The Old Irish Hymns
### of the
## Liber Hymnorum.

The manuscript known by the name *Liber Hymnorum* <note>Latin for "Book of Hymns"</note> is located in the library of Trinity College, Dublin (Trin. Coll. Dub. E. 4. 2.). Most of the Irish pieces within its contents were shared by Stokes in his *Goidelica* (1st ed. Calcutta 1866, 2nd ed. London 1872); a short report on the physical appearance of the manuscript and its entire contents can be found in *Goidelica*, 2nd edition, page 61.

Almost all the hymns are preceded by an Irish preface, which reports on the author, and the place and time of origin, as much as was known in tradition. The reports partially contradict one another, which may be interpreted as an indication of the relatively great age of the hymns. See Ebel, *Grammatica Celtica*, 2nd edition, page 957, annotation.

Based on the Middle Irish forms <note>Middle Irish refers to the Gaelic language as written and spoken between roughly 900–1200 AD</note> occurring in the text of the hymns, in the prefaces, and in the glosses <note>explanatory notes written in the margins or between lines</note>, Stokes suggests (*Goidelica* 2nd ed., p. 61) that the manuscript was written at the end of the 11th or the beginning of the 12th century*. However, the hymns themselves are without question much older, and based on their language, they may be placed roughly alongside the Old Irish <note>the earliest written form of the Gaelic language, roughly 600–900 AD</note> glosses of the codices <note>handwritten books</note> in Milan, Würzburg, and elsewhere. Above all, the verb forms correspond perfectly to the Old Irish verbal system.

---

* For the dating of the manuscript, it would be important if the author of Hymn 8 were really Maelísu hua Brolcháin, who is said to have died in the year 1086, as Stokes suggests in *Goidelica* 2nd ed., p. 174. <note>Maelísu hua Brolcháin was a famous Irish cleric and writer known for his high-quality Middle Irish poetry.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the Liber Hymnorum manuscript, discussing its preservation at Trinity College Dublin and its scholarly publication history. It debates the dating of the hymns, distinguishing between the 11th-century physical manuscript and the much older linguistic roots of the poems themselves.</summary>
<keywords>Liber Hymnorum, Trinity College Dublin, Whitley Stokes, Old Irish, Middle Irish, Maelísu hua Brolcháin, manuscript dating, glosses</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the linguistic age of the hymns, focusing on specific archaic verb forms and the editorial principles used to reconstruct the original poetic meter of the poems.</meta>

# I. The Old Irish Hymns.

In this regard, the numerous forms of the reduplicated **perfect** <term>reduplicated perfect: an archaic past-tense verb form where the first sound of the word is repeated, common in very old Irish but later lost</term> are of great importance. The glossator <note>the scholar who wrote the explanatory notes</note> always replaces them with the corresponding form of the **s-preterite** <term>s-preterite: a later, simpler way of forming the past tense that became standard as the language evolved</term>; in the glossator’s time, these older forms belonged to an outdated way of speaking. Everything that Stokes, in the work cited <note>original Latin: "a. a. O." (am angeführten Ort), referring to Goidelica</note>, identifies as Middle Irish forms and Middle Irish orthography <note>spelling</note> is, as far as the text of the hymns is concerned, merely a result of the copying tradition. Whether the glosses and the prefaces originate from the scribe of the manuscript itself, I do not dare to decide with certainty. Naturally, these parts are in any case younger than the main text of the hymns.

I compared the text of the hymns shared by Stokes in the 1st edition of *Goidelica* with the manuscript during my stay in Dublin (1871); my treatment of the hymns is based on this collation <note>a critical, word-for-word comparison of different versions of a text</note> as well as on the text of the 2nd edition of *Goidelica*. In doubtful cases, Professor Atkinson in Dublin had the kindness to consult the manuscript once more on my behalf. In the 2nd edition, Stokes limited himself more than in the 1st to providing an exact reproduction of the manuscript. I have included the corrections from the 1st edition in my text on several occasions. The external reasons for this were partly due to an "impure" rhyme and partly due to a deviation from the regular number of syllables in the verse. I was guided by my own observations regarding the regularity of Irish verse, as well as the chapter "Irish Poetic Construction" <note>original Latin: *Constructio Poetica Hibernica*</note> in the *Celtic Grammar* <note>original: *Grammatica Celtica*</note>, and the chapter "Of Versification" in O’Donovan’s *Grammar of the Irish Language*. Accordingly, I saw an error* wherever isolated half-verses of six or eight syllables appeared in the middle of half-verses of seven syllables. It should be noted, however, that eight syllables in the first

---

* The author of the *Preface* <note>original: *Praefatio*</note> to the *Félire* <note>The Félire Óengusso, or Martyrology of Oengus, a famous 9th-century calendar of saints</note> expresses himself in exactly the same way when reporting on its metrical form: "six syllables in its quarters, and twelve in its half-stanzas, and twenty-four in its complete stanzas. And if it be more or less, it is a **blemish**" <note>original Irish/Latin: "sé sillaeba ina cethramnaib ocus a dó déc ina lethrannaib ocus a cethair XX ina rannaib chomlana. et si sit plus minusve is pudar"</note> — "six syllables in

<summary>The author explains how archaic verb forms prove the hymns are older than the notes surrounding them and describes his methodology for correcting the poetic meter based on traditional Irish rules.</summary>
<keywords>reduplicated perfect, s-preterite, Whitley Stokes, Robert Atkinson, Goidelica, Irish metrics, versification, Félire Óengusso</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the editorial process regarding the Old Irish hymns, specifically focusing on the treatment of glosses and previous scholarship by Whitley Stokes.</meta>

# I. The Old Irish Hymns.

...and seven syllables in the second half of the verse forms a regular pattern of Irish verse.

Regarding the glosses <note>explanatory notes written between lines or in margins of a manuscript</note>, I have only shared those below the text that provide a factual explanation or otherwise clarify the meaning of the sentence. The lexical glosses—that is, those which replace a word from the text with a synonym or provide an etymology <note>the history and origin of a word</note>—I have usually only listed in the dictionary. Likewise, the rarer words and forms from the prefaces are included there, as I have chosen not to reprint the text of the prefaces again following Stokes’ edition. I have not personally collated <note>the process of comparing the printed text against the original manuscript to ensure accuracy</note> the prefaces or the glosses with great precision, with the exception of those already printed in the 1st edition of *Goidelica* <note>a seminal collection of Old Irish texts edited by Whitley Stokes</note>. In some places, the glosses are no longer legible in the manuscript.

Stokes provided translations for the hymns, which on the whole must be regarded as an excellent achievement. He recently added several improvements in the second edition of the work *Remarks on the Celtic Additions to Curtius’ Greek Etymology etc.*, Calcutta 1875, pages 67–72. Regarding the numerous difficult passages, I have held back my own uncertain conjectures as much as possible and have primarily limited myself to highlighting the difficulty of the text or, where necessary, the uncertainty of Stokes’ interpretation. If I have succeeded in advancing the understanding of individual passages, it was only possible upon the foundation laid by Stokes.

---

### 1. Colman’s Hymn.

The preface contains the following noteworthy information: *Colmán mac húi Chluasaig* <note>an 11th-century Irish scholar and poet</note> is identified as the author, a

> its quarters, and twelve in its half-quatrains, and twenty-four in its complete quatrain, and if it be more or less <note>original Latin: "et si sit plus minusve"</note> there is an error“ (Stokes in the unfortunately not yet published edition of the *Félire* <note>The *Félire Óengusso*, or Martyrology of Oengus, an important Old Irish calendar of saints</note> pages VII, IX).

<summary>The author explains his editorial choices, specifically why he excluded certain linguistic notes (lexical glosses) in favor of factual ones, while acknowledging the foundational work of scholar Whitley Stokes. The section begins to introduce Colman's Hymn, citing rules for Irish poetic meter.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page begins the presentation of the Old Irish hymns, starting with "Colmán’s Hymn." It follows the introductory remarks about editorial methods and Whitley Stokes' previous translations, transitioning from the technical preface to the specific historical and poetic analysis of the first hymn in the collection.</meta>

### I 1. Colmán’s Hymn.

A <term>fer legind</term> <note>a "man of learning"—specifically a scholar or lecturer in theology</note> at Cork, who lived during the time of Kings Blaithmac and Diarmait, the sons of King Aed Sláne. Colmán composed this hymn to protect himself and his school against the plague called the *Bude Connaill* <note>The "Yellow Plague," a devastating epidemic that swept through Ireland in the mid-7th century</note>, from which those two sons of King Aed died, among others. To escape this plague, Colmán fled with his school to an island. According to one tradition, Colmán composed the entire hymn; according to another, he wrote only the first four long-lines, while the remaining verses were composed by his students, with each student contributing one long-line.

This latter account aligns well with the hymn's rather unpoetic character and should be kept in mind when interpreting the individual verses. The *Chronicum Scotorum* <note>The "Chronicle of the Scots," a medieval Irish chronicle</note> (edited by Hennessy, page 99) reports that the two sons of Aed died of the aforementioned plague in the year 661 AD.

Each half-verse consists of seven syllables.

->May God’s blessing lead us, may it come upon us, may Mary’s Son protect us!<-
<note>original: "Sén De don fe, for don te, Mac Maire ron feladar!"</note>
->Under His protection may we be tonight; wherever we go, may He guard us well!<-
<note>original: "for a foessam dún innocht, cia tiasam, cain temadar!"</note>

->Whether at rest or in motion, whether sitting or standing,<-
<margin>4</margin> ->The Lord of Heaven against every battle—this is the prayer we offer.<-

->The prayer of Abel, son of Adam; Elijah and Enoch to our aid!<-
->May they save us from the swift disease, wherever throughout the world it rages!<-

->Noah and Abraham, Isaac the wondrous son,<-
<margin>8</margin> ->May they surround us against the pestilence; let no famine reach us!<-

---

* Individual verses call to mind those beginning with "May Jesus save us" in the Epilogue to the *Félire* <note>The *Félire Óengusso*, an 8th-century calendar of Irish saints. The quoted passage translates to: "May Jesus save us from every evil on earth, as he saved Noah, son of Lamech, from the flood," etc.</note>.
2a *under his protection* in the Manuscript; the same construction appears in Hymn 6, 24.
2b *may he guard* in the Manuscript.
5a <term>itge</term> <note>prayer</note>: that is, "we pray."

<meta>This page continues the translation of Colmán’s Hymn, moving from the introduction into the poetic text and detailed linguistic commentary. The previous page established that the hymn was composed as a protective prayer during a plague in the 7th century.</meta>

### I 1. Colmán’s Hymn.

I pray to the Father of the twelve <note>original: "tri cethrur," literally "three fours," referring to the twelve patriarchs or apostles</note> and Joseph from above,
may their prayers save us to the noble King of many angels!
May Moses, the good leader, protect us <note>original: "Snaidsium"</note>; he protected us <note>original: "ron snaid"</note> through the Red Sea <note>original: "rubrum mare"</note>,
<margin>12</margin> Joshua <note>original: "Jesu," the Greek/Latin form of Joshua</note>, Aaron son of Amra, and David the bold lad.

---

**9^b** *i. anuasalfer.* Later, Whitley Stokes <note>A prominent 19th-century Celtic scholar</note> in *Goidelica* (2nd edition, page 184) supplemented this as *anuas[er]*. However, the rhyme requires an *a* in the last syllable, or the word *uasel* (noble) in verse 10.

**11^a** *snaidsium*: compare the forms *snaidsiunn* in verses 27 and 38, *soersum* in verse 28, and *ainsiunn* in Hymn 6, verse 14. Stokes separates an affixed personal pronoun from these forms (translating it as "May Moses protect me"); see *Beiträge VII*, pages 41–42. The easiest form to explain is *ainsium*, "may he protect me" (*Félire Óengusso* <note>The "Martyrology of Oengus," an early 9th-century calendar of saints</note>, Jan. 30). According to Stokes, this equals *ainis-um*, the 3rd person singular Future of the verb *aingim* (to protect) used with a conjunctive meaning. However, this form usually appears as *ain* without the affix, for example: *ronn ain*, "may he protect us." Furthermore, in *soersum* and *snaidsiunn* (derived from verbs of the 2nd and 3rd conjugation), the *s* can hardly belong to the verbal form itself. The scholar Ebel claims this is the case, categorizing *snaidsiunn* (*Grammatica Celtica*, page 467) as a Future or Conjunctive formed with *s*. However, this tense is never otherwise formed from derived verbs, and *snaidsiunn* would violate the rule (as Ebel himself acknowledges) that the final root consonant is always assimilated to the *s* of this formation. It is also significant that only the forms belonging to *ainsiunn* are formed with *s* even when they lack an affix (e.g., *ron anset*, verse 14), while the forms related in meaning to *snaidsium* and *soersum*—such as *ron snada* (13), *ron soera* (56), and *ron soerat* (6, 10, 24)—never contain an *s*. In any case, the view of the glossators <note>Medieval scribes who added explanatory notes between lines or in margins</note> deserves consideration; they attach the *s* to the affix and interpret the affix differently. In our passage, *snaidsiunn* is explained as *ron snade sund*, "may he protect us here." Likewise, in the *Leabhar Breac* <note>The "Speckled Book," a 14th-century Irish manuscript collection</note>, the glossator on *snaidsium* in the *Félire Óengusso* (July 18) explains: "i.e., may he effect our protection, i.e., our defense from here, i.e., from this present world" <note>original: "ab h[oc] s[ae]cli presenti"</note>. For September 21: "i.e., may he effect our protection, may he protect us from here, i.e., from the present world" <note>original: "a presenti s[ae]clo"</note>. Stokes cites *snaidsiunn* from both these places, likely from a different manuscript. Regarding *ainsium* (Jan. 30): "i.e., may those protect me" (sic) or "may they protect us." Regarding *soersum* in this hymn (verse 28): "may he save us" <note>original: "ro soera sinn"</note>. See also: "Our powerful Lord, our leader of a host of many, our sage, our savior, may he protect us [against] death" <note>original: "snaidsium ar cel"</note> from the *Lebor na hUidre* <note>The "Book of the Dun Cow," the oldest surviving manuscript in Irish</note>, page 15a, line 21. I cannot reach a definitive decision: while the context everywhere supports the idea of a conjunctive verb form with an affixed pronoun, these forms do not seem to have been familiar to the medieval glossators.

**11^b** *ron snaid*: i.e., the people of Israel. Stokes translates: "(he) who protected." However, the *n* in *ron* cannot be a relative marker here. According to the glossator, it should be translated: "who protected it (i.e., the people of Israel) through the Red Sea." As noted, we are dealing with "student verses" <note>Referring to the tradition that Colmán's students finished the poem, resulting in less polished grammar</note>. The manuscript reads *maire* <note>sea</note>.

**12^a** *Jesu*: i.e., Joshua son of Nun <note>original: "mac Nún"</note>. **12^b** The manuscript reads *dana* <note>bold/skilled</note>.

<meta>This page continues the translation of Colmán’s Hymn, moving from the earlier verses into a list of biblical figures whose protection is invoked. The previous page discussed the linguistic complexities of the verb "to protect" (snaidsium) and began the petition to the patriarchs.</meta>

### I 1. Colmán’s Hymn.

Job with his trials, beyond the poisons may he protect us <note>original: "ron snada"</note>,
the Lord's prophets, may they protect us along with the seven sons of the Maccabees!

John the Baptist we appeal to, may he be a shelter for us, may he be protection!
<margin>16</margin> Jesus with his apostles <note>original: "conapstalaib," the manuscript's contracted form</note> may he be for our help against danger!

Mary and Joseph, may they summon us, and the spirit of Stephen;
from every hardship may it release us, the remembrance of the soul of Ignatius!

Every martyr, every hermit, every saint who lived in chastity,
<margin>20</margin> may they be a shield for us for our defense, may they be an arrow from us against demons!

We pray to the King of Kings <note>original: "Regem regum rogamus"</note> in our prayers <note>original: "in nostris sermonibus"</note>,
he who protected Noah and his household <note>original: "a luchtlach"</note> in the times of the flood <note>original: "diluvi temporibus"</note>.

Melchizedek, King of Salem, of uncertain lineage <note>original: "incerto de semine"</note>,
<margin>24</margin> may his prayers save us from all fear <note>original: "ab omni formidine"</note>!

The Savior <note>original: "Soter"</note> who saved Lot from the fire, who is held through the ages,
as we all pray, may he deign to liberate us <note>original: "liberare dignetur"</note>.

Abram from Ur of the Chaldees, may the Great King protect us <note>original: "snáidsiunn"</note> who protects us;
<margin>28</margin> may he free us <note>original: "soersum"</note> who freed the people by the water of the spring in danger <note>original: "i n-gaba"</note>.

The Great King who protected the three youths from the furnace of fierce fire,
may he protect us, as he protected David from the hand of Goliath <note>original: "de manu Golai"</note>.

Ruler of the luminous heaven, may he pity us <note>original: "ar don roigse"</note> in our misery,
<margin>32</margin> he who did not leave his prophet to the mouth of any lions!

---

<note>16^a *conapstalaib* in the Manuscript.</note>
<note>22^a *protected*: i.e., the king who protected. *his household*: i.e., his "lake-folk" or his "dark folk," referring to Noah with his three sons and their four wives. The scholar Stokes translates this as "Noah [with] his people," while Ebel (in *Grammatica Celtica*) translates it as "he saved Noah [and] his family."</note>
<note>27. 28 *may he free us*: may he free us. *in danger*: i.e., in the danger in which they found themselves without water when they came out of Egypt. Stokes translates: "Abram . . ., let the Prince who protected him protect us, let him free me" etc. However, should *may he protect us* <note>original: "ron snada"</note> here not be understood in the same way as in verse 13? Compare the note on verse 11 regarding the gloss for *may he free us*. The transition from the 1st Person Plural ("protect us") to the 1st Person Singular ("free me") is striking.</note>
<note>29^b *fierce* <note>original: "co ruadi"</note> in the Manuscript. 31^b i.e., he had compassion <note>original: "arroairchíse"</note>.</note>

<meta>This page concludes Colmán’s Hymn (Sén Dé). Having previously invoked Old Testament figures like Noah and Melchizedek, the poet now moves through New Testament miracles—specifically Peter and Jonah—before transitioning into a powerful litany of the primary patron saints of Ireland: Patrick, Brigid, and Columcille.</meta>

### I 1. Colmán’s Hymn.

As he sent the angel <note>original: "in n-aingel," manuscript reads "inaingel"</note> who released Peter from his chains,
may he be sent <note>original: "dorfoiter," manuscript reads "doroiter"</note> to us for our help; may every rough way be smooth before us!

To our Lord <note>original: "fiadait," manuscript "fiadat"</note> may we be pleasing through our worthy work <note>original Latin: "nostro opere digno"</note>;
36 may we be with him in life <note>original: "i m-bethaid"</note> in the kingdom of paradise <note>original Latin: "in paradisí regno"</note>!

As he saved Jonah the prophet from the belly of the great whale — a bright deed —,
may the strong, threatening, good King protect us! God’s blessing lead us, may it be over us!

Truly, O Lord, truly, may this prayer <note>original: "gude"</note> be granted,
40 may the little sons <note>original: "maccain"</note> of God’s kingdom be in the company of this school!
<gloss>little children who die immediately in holiness after baptism</gloss>

Truly, O Lord, let it be true,
may we all reach the peace of the King!

Moreover, may we come where they have come,
44 in the kingdom of heaven may we meet him!

May we be without age <note>original: "cen aes"</note> in the broad land,
with angels in eternal life!

Patriarchs, prophets without end, angels, apostles — a noble sight <note>original: "ard fegad"</note> —
<gloss>that is, a noble sight of angels and apostles</gloss>
48 may they come with our heavenly Father <note>original: "liar n-athair"</note> before the host of demons to bless us!
<note>The manuscript notes: "Up to this point Colmán sang."</note>

**God’s Blessing.**

A blessing on patron Patrick <note>original: "Patraic"</note> with the saints of Ireland around him <note>original: "immi"</note>,
a blessing on this city and on everyone who is in it!

A blessing on patron Brigid with the virgins of Ireland around her,
52 give all — a fair testimony — a blessing on the dignity of Brigid!

A blessing <note>original: "Bendacht," manuscript "benedacht"</note> on Columcille with the saints of Scotland yonder,
on the soul of noble Adamnán <note>original: "Adamnain"</note>, who laid a law upon the clans!
<note>This refers to the *Cáin Adomnáin* or the "Law of Innocents," a historical legal text protecting non-combatants in war.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the final blessing of Colmán’s Hymn into the introduction and scholarly analysis of the second hymn in the collection: Fiacc’s Hymn. The previous page concluded the poetic litany of Colmán, and we now begin the historical-critical commentary on the life and works attributed to Fiacc of Sletty.</meta>

Under the protection of the King of the Elements <note>original: "na n-dúla"</note>—a guardianship that will not be taken from us— <margin>56</margin> may the Holy Spirit bedew us, may Christ save us, may he bless us!

### 2. Fiacc’s Hymnus.

Here we should likely be cautious about seeking historical truth in the claims of the preface: Fiacc of Sleibte (Sletty) is said to have been a contemporary of St. Patrick, who supposedly baptized him personally. Previously, he was said to have been a pupil of an <term>ardfile</term> <note>a "high-poet" or chief poet, the highest rank of the filid in early Irish society</note> of Ireland named Dubthach mac hui Lugair. He became the Archbishop of Leinster. According to one tradition, he is said to have composed the hymn during the reign of Lugaid († 507 in the *Chronicon Scotorum* <note>a medieval Irish chronicle</note>), the son and successor of Loegaire, following Patrick’s death (489 in the *Chronicon Scotorum*).

Leo treated this hymn in a publication from Halle in the year 1845: *Commentary on the Ancient Irish Song written in praise of St. Patrick* <note>original Latin title: "Commentatio de Carmine Vetusto Hibernico in S. Patricii laudem scripta"</note>. Leo remarks: The hymn cannot have originated before the year 540 and no later than the 8th century. For the glory of Tara <note>the ancient seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note> faded in the 6th century, and the prophecy in verses 20 and 44 must surely be based on that event; whereas, on the other hand, the flowering of early Christian literature declined in the 8th century, the period when foreign invasions <note>referring to the Viking raids</note> began. Furthermore, the hymn cannot stem from an immediate pupil of Patrick, as written records concerning Patrick are already mentioned in verse 12.

> “Truly, that this song is of great antiquity we argue from the fact that not one of the most absurd fables regarding St. Patrick appears in it, many of which are found in all other narratives about Patrick. This opinion is further proved by the ignorance of later writers, which resulted in the names of the mountainous regions of Scotland and of the city of Auxerre (<gloss>read Autes., for Altes.</gloss>), which are found beyond doubt in our song, not only not in—”

---

<margin>55</margin> Stokes translates: “Upon (the) protection of the king of the Elements, a guardianship he will not take from us.”

<summary>This page introduces Fiacc’s Hymn, providing historical context on Fiacc of Sletty and summarizing 19th-century scholarship regarding the poem's dating and authenticity. It argues the hymn is ancient but likely not written by a direct contemporary of St. Patrick.</summary>

<keywords>Fiacc of Sletty, St. Patrick, Dubthach mac hui Lugair, Leinster, Chronicon Scotorum, Tara, Irish hagiography, Auxerre</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly introduction to Fiacc's Hymn. The previous page discussed the legendary origins of the hymn and the life of Fiacc of Sletty, as well as early 19th-century attempts to date the text based on historical references to Tara.</meta>

### 2. Fiacc’s Hymn.

"...not so that they understood, but so that they might invent fables wandering greatly from the truth to interpret these names concerning Patrick’s journey through the Alps to Rome and to the islands of the Tyrrhenian Sea." <note>original Latin: "telligereat, sed ut ad haec nomina interpretanda fabulas maxime a vero aberrantes fingerent de Patricio per Alpes Romam et ad insulas maris Tyrrheni profecto."</note> For the rest, Leo’s work is only of value today insofar as it allows us to feel what tremendous progress <term>Celtic Studies</term> <note>original: "Celtologie"</note> has made in the last thirty years. The Irish text, taken from older editions, is modernized and frequently corrupt; the translation and commentary, however, originate from the era before C. Zeuss <note>Johann Caspar Zeuss, whose 1853 *Grammatica Celtica* founded modern Celtic philology</note>, and they rarely contain the correct interpretation.

According to its poetic form, this hymn is treated in Chapter VI, 2 of the *Grammatica Celtica* (p. 957 ff.), with valuable notes on many individual verses.

Patrick was born in Nemthur <note>an unidentified location, often associated with Dumbarton in Scotland</note>, this is what he tells in stories,<br/>
a boy of sixteen years when he was brought under tears.*

Succat his name, it was said*; even his father’s was worth knowing:*<br/>
son of Calpurn, son of Otide, grandson of the deacon Odisse. <margin>4</margin>

He was six years * in servitude, the finery of men * he did not consume,<br/>
many were the masters of Cothraige <note>a name for Patrick, interpreted as "four-servant"</note>, of the four tribes whom he served.*

2<sup>b</sup> **under tears:** <gloss>at the end, (in the margin) i.e., under the sorrow of captivity.</gloss>

3<sup>a</sup> **Succat:** <gloss>i.e., that is British and "god of war" in Latin <note>original Latin: "deus belli"</note>; i.e., this is what was said by the learned.</gloss> *Stokes <note>Whitley Stokes, a prominent 19th-century Celtic scholar</note> translates: "Succat his name it was said," by relating the second gloss to "itubrad" (perhaps it-ru-brad? "hi trubh rad" according to Leo).*

3<sup>b</sup> **worth knowing:** <gloss>i.e., it was proper to know it.</gloss>

5<sup>a</sup> <gloss>i.e., after the likeness of the little jubilee of the Hebrews.</gloss> <note>A reference to the biblical law where Hebrew slaves were to be freed after six years of service.</note>

5<sup>b</sup> **finery of men:** <gloss>i.e., good food and clothing.</gloss>

6 <gloss>i.e., the name Cothraige stuck to him i.e., "four-servant" <note>Old Irish: "cetharaige"</note> i.e., because he used to serve (Stokes suggests reading "fogníth" instead of "dogníth") four tribes.</gloss> *Stokes translates: "Many were they — four tribes, which Cothraige served." However, "cethartrebe" <note>four tribes</note> cannot be the nominative, as this compound usually follows singular inflection; the word order also weighs against Stokes’ interpretation. I would prefer to view it as a genitive singular dependent on Cothraige: "there were many to whom Cothraige of the four tribes served." The rhyme between "toimled" and "fognad" certainly did not originate in this form from the poet.*

### I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

Victor <note>The angel who traditionally served as St. Patrick's guardian and guide</note> spoke to the servant,*
he placed his foot upon the stone;
He sent him across all the Alps,*
so that he remained with Germanus. <note>St. Germanus of Auxerre, a 5th-century bishop who was Patrick's mentor</note>
In the islands of the Tyrrhenian Sea
he studied the Canon with Germanus;
Toward Ireland he directed them <note>likely referring to his steps or intentions</note>,
often was he seen* in visions.
It was a help to Ireland,
far was heard the sound of their calling;
They prayed that the saint might come,
that he might turn them from error.*

<column-break/>

...that he might walk upon the waves,*
his fame remains,* it has not decayed. <margin>8</margin>
From the Great God, it was a wondrous journey—*
southward in the southern part of Letha. <note>Usually refers to Armorica (Brittany) or Latium (Italy); here the commentator identifies it as Italy.</note>*
He stayed, he numbers the inhabitants,
it is this that the lines relate. <margin>12</margin>
God’s angels in their path*,
would come to him again.
The coming of Patrick was heard,
by the children of the forest of Fochlad. <note>A forest in County Mayo associated with Patrick's visions of the Irish people calling him back</note>* <margin>16</margin>
...that he might travel with them,
bringing the tribes of Ireland to life.

---

7 **servant** <note>original Irish: *gniad*</note> *is two syllables, as in 'serves' in verse 30.*
7^b **upon the waves:** <gloss>i.e., eastwards upon the sea for study.</gloss>
8^b **remains** <note>original: *maraith*</note> *in the Manuscript.*
9^a <gloss>i.e., Victor sent Patrick across the mountain of the Alps.</gloss> Nevertheless, <term>Whitley Stokes</term> <note>A prominent 19th-century Irish scholar</note> translates *do faid* as “he went” and *farggaib* (that is, *fo-ro-ath-gaib*) as “he stayed.” Compare “he sent” <note>Latin: *misit*</note> in *Grammatica Celtica* p. 463, and “he left” <note>Latin: *reliquit*</note> p. 881.
9^b **Great God:** “great God” according to Stokes. Compare “O great God” in the *Félire Oengusso* (Epilogue 78, 79). O’Curry, in his *Lectures* (p. 503), offers a different view on **wondrous journey**: <gloss>i.e., this journey.</gloss> Compare “wondrous story” in *Hymn* 5, 18. In both cases, the word for “wondrous” should probably be read as *amra*.
10 <gloss>i.e., in Italy where Germanus was.</gloss>
13^b **in their path:** “in (his) course” according to Stokes. However, the possessive pronoun would likely not be missing in Irish.
14^a **was seen:** <gloss>i.e., by the saints.</gloss> The first half of the verse has eight syllables; perhaps it should be read as *atchith*.
15 Stokes translates: “It was a help to Ireland (the) coming of Patrick, who was called.” I consider “coming” <note>Irish: *tichtu*</note> to be the subject of “was heard,” regarding the gloss on 16^b.
16^a **was heard:** <gloss>i.e., throughout Ireland.</gloss>
16^b **children:** <gloss>i.e., Crebriu and Lesru, the two daughters of Gleran, son of the grandson of Enne, saying: “The Irish cry out to you, come holy Patrick to save us.”</gloss> <note>original Latin: "Hibernenses ad te clamant, veni sancte Patrici salvos nos facere."</note>
17^a *There is one syllable too many here.*
17^b **with them** <note>original: *lethu*</note> according to Stokes; “late” according to *Grammatica Celtica* p. 469.
18^a **from error:** <gloss>i.e., from the worship of idols.</gloss> 18^b **to life:** <gloss>i.e., to the faith of Christ.</gloss>

<meta>This page continues the text of Fiacc's Hymn, moving from Patrick's preparation for his mission into the period of his arrival in Ireland, his prophecies, and his ascetic practices. ### I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

The tribes of Ireland were prophesying that a new kingdom of peace would come to them,
it will remain until the day of posterity, and the land of silent Tara will be waste. 20

His druids did not hide Patrick’s coming from Loegaire <note>The High King of Ireland at the time of Patrick’s arrival</note>,
the prophecy of the kingdom they used to speak was verified.

Patrick was diligent until he died, he was a pillar for driving out wickedness,
it is this that raised his fame upward beyond the tribes of men. 24

Hymns and the Apocalypse, and the three fifties <note>The 150 Psalms</note> he used to sing,
he preached, baptized, and prayed; from the praise of God he did not cease.

The cold of the weather did not prevent him from sleeping at night in pools of water,
in heaven he will win his kingdom; he preached by day on the hills. 28

In the spring of Slán, north of the Peaks of Mourne <note>Benna Bairche</note>, neither thirst nor hunger seized him,
he sings a hundred psalms every night, serving the King of Angels.

---

20 **until the day of posterity**: i.e., until Judgment <note>original Irish: *co brath*</note>, compare "it will remain until the day of Judgment" in the prologue of the *Félire Oengusso* (Martyrology of Oengus), line 174. **silent** <note>original: *tua*</note>: i.e., without glory. Stokes translates: "*that his successors would abide to (the) day of (Doom), that Tara's land would be waste (and) silent*," in which he—without paying attention to the meter—writes *co aiartaige*, mistakenly applies the gloss "until Judgment" only to *co de*, gives the word *iartaige* an unproven meaning, and considers the two-syllable *tua* to be a Nominative Singular (it is actually the Genitive Singular Feminine belonging to *Temrach* [Tara]).

22<sup>a</sup> *prophecy* <note>original: *indatsine*</note> in the Manuscript. See the interesting marginal note to these verses in the Appendix.

23<sup>a</sup> **he was diligent**: i.e., in devotion. **until he died**: i.e., until his death. *wickedness* <note>original: *cloeni*</note> in the Manuscript. Compare "a pillar for driving out demons" in the *Félire Oengusso*, October 16.

24<sup>b</sup> **upward**: i.e., to heaven <note>original Latin: *ad coelum*</note>. But how is *de* to be understood? Even Stokes is at a loss.

25<sup>a</sup> **hymns**: i.e., the Hymn of Saint Ambrose or the *Audite Omnes* <note>The first Latin hymn written in Ireland, attributed to Sechnall in praise of Patrick</note>.

25<sup>b</sup> *he used to sing* <note>original: *nascanad*</note> with an 'o' over the 'a' of the first syllable in the Manuscript.

26<sup>a</sup> **he prayed**: i.e., he performed prayers and penance.

28<sup>a</sup> has eight syllables; perhaps the 'a' should be deleted.

28<sup>b</sup> *ininib* in the Manuscript. Stokes's conjecture *i n-dinnib* (on the hills) becomes evident through the gloss *i telchaib* (on the heights). Likewise in the text shared by Leo: *i ndindib*.

29<sup>a</sup> **In Slán**: i.e., the name of a spring <margin>it was called "healthy" <note>original: *slana*</note> because every sick person who went there became healthy, and it is in Saul. ### I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

He used to sleep on a bare stone then, with a wet cloak around him;
a pillar-stone was his pillow, he did not allow his body to rest in luxury. 32

He preached the gospel to everyone; he performed great miracles throughout the world <note>original: *illethu*</note>;
he heals the lame and the lepers; the dead he would restore to life.

Patrick preached to the Irish <note>original: *Scotaib*</note>; he suffered great hardships throughout the world,
so that they might come with him to Judgment—everyone whom he brought to life <note>meaning: brought to the faith</note>. 36

The sons of Eber and the sons of Eremon <note>The legendary ancestral lineages of the Irish people</note> all followed the devil <note>original: *cisal*</note>;
their transgression cast them into the great deep pit <note>meaning: into hell</note>.

Until the Apostle came to them; he traveled with the speed of a swift wind;
he preached for sixty years the Cross of Christ to the tribes of the Feni <note>A traditional name for the Irish</note>. 40

Darkness lay over the people of Ireland; the tribes worshipped spirits <note>original: *síde*, referring to the pagan deities of the mounds</note>;
they did not believe in the true divinity of the true Trinity.

In Armagh is the kingship; it is long since Emain Macha was abandoned;
Downpatrick is a great church; it is not pleasing to me that Tara should be a desert. 44

### I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

Patrick, when he was in weakness <note>meaning: his final illness</note>, desired to go to Armagh;
an angel <note>Victor</note> came to meet him on the road in the middle of the day.

He went southwards to Victor—it was he who directed him <note>meaning: he guided Patrick to remain at Saul instead of going to Armagh</note>;
the bush in which he was blazed, and from the fire He <note>God</note> spoke to him. 48

He said: "Primacy <note>original: *orddan*</note> to Armagh; to Christ give thanks!
To heaven you shall soon go; your prayers have been granted to you."

"The hymn you chose during your life shall be a breastplate of protection for everyone;
on the Day of Judgment, the men of Ireland will go with you to be judged." 52

Tassach remained after him <note>referring to Saint Tassach of Raholp</note> when he gave Communion to him;
he said that Patrick would return; Tassach’s word was not a lie.

He <note>Patrick</note> set a boundary against the night, so that time would not be consumed by it;
until the end of a year there was light—that was a long, peaceful day. 56

In the battle fought <note>original: *fechta*</note> in Beth-horon against the people of Canaan by the son of Nun <note>Joshua</note>,
the sun turned back toward Gibeon, as the scriptures tell us.

Since the sun turned back for Joshua at the death of the wicked,
though it be a matter of many rays, how much more fitting was light at the passing of the saint. 60

<meta>This page continues the account of Saint Patrick's passing. ### I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

The clergy of Ireland came from every path to keep watch over Patrick;
the sound of the chanting overwhelmed them <note>original: *fos ro laich*, meaning the music lulled or covered them</note>, so that each of them fell asleep on the way.

Patrick’s soul was separated from his body after his labors;
God’s angels on the first night were singing to him <note>original: *arid fetis*, referring to playing music or piping</note> without ceasing. <margin>64</margin>

When Patrick departed <note>original: *co n-hualai*</note>, he visited the other Patrick <note>Senphatraic, or "Old Patrick," a figure in early Irish tradition often distinguished from the Apostle of Ireland</note>;
together they ascended <note>original: *connuccabsat*</note> to Jesus, son of Mary.

Patrick, without a trace of pride <note>original: *airde n-úabair*</note>, meditated on much that was good;
to be in the service of Mary's son—that was the happy fate <note>original: *sén gaire*, literally "luck of laughter"</note> to which he was born. <margin>68</margin>

66^a <note>Technical note: The manuscript reads *connubcabsat*. Neither the scholars Stokes nor Ebel seemed to object to the first *b*. Leo suggests the form *connucceibhset*, which is linguistically more accurate if separated correctly. It contains the root *gab* (to take/go) with the prefixes *con-ud-*. Between these is an "infixed pronoun," which explains the double *n* and gives the verb a reflexive meaning: "they raised themselves up" or "they ascended."</note>

# Appendix.

In this appendix, those passages from the Latin *Life* written by *Jocelin, Monk of Furness* (*Acts of the Saints, March, Vol. II, page 541 following*), as well as from the Irish *Life* in the *Speckled Book* <note>original: *Lebar Brecc*</note>, are shared, which seemed to me to be useful for the understanding of the *Hymn*.

### Regarding Verses 2–6:

Jocelin, Chapter II, 10. . . . For when the illustrious boy himself had passed through three five-year periods <note>original Latin: *tria lustra*, totaling fifteen years</note>, reaching his sixteenth year, he was snatched away with many other fellow-countrymen by pirates plundering those borders; he was taken captive and led into Ireland. He was then sold into slavery to a certain most pagan petty king <note>Latin: *regulo*</note> named Miliuc, who ruled in the northern part of the same island . . .

. . . he prayed for some time while fasting deeply, sustaining himself occasionally with the roots of herbs or the lightest of foods; he mortified his limbs, which were upon the earth . . .

*Speckled Book, page 25b:* This then is the account of Patrick’s coming to Ireland: the seven sons of Sechtmad, that is, the seven sons of the King of the Britons, who were in exile, committed a plunder in the land of the Britons. The men of Ulster were together with them, and they brought Patrick with them into captivity to Ireland, along with his two sisters, namely Tigris and Lupait. They sold Patrick to Miliuc, son of Húi Buain, that is, to the King of Dál Araide, and to his three brothers; and they sold his two sisters in Conaille Muirthemni. And they did not know about each other <note>i.e., where the others were sold</note>, nor did anyone among them know into which land the other had been sold. Patrick then served the king and his three brothers, and for that reason the name Cothraige was given to him, that is, "servant of four" <note>Irish: *mog cethrair*</note>. He had four names upon him: Sucait, his name from his parents; Cothraige, while he was serving the four; Magonius (<gloss>that is, acting more</gloss>), his name with Germanus; Patricius (<gloss>that is, father of citizens, or father of the city-folk</gloss>), his name with the successor <note>Irish: *comorba*</note> of Peter (<gloss>that is, Pope Celestine</gloss>).

Regarding the names of Patrick, Stokes shares the following in *Remarks^2, page 36, Note*: “*In the Tripartite Life, Rawlinson B. 512, folio 6b. 2, we find:* Four persons indeed bought him; one of them was Miliuc. From him he received the name Cothraige because he served four households <note>Irish: *cethartreb*</note>. He then had four names upon him: Sucait, his name from his parents; Cothraige while he was serving four persons; Magonius from [his teacher] Germanus; Patricius, that is first (?) of the citizens, from Pope Celestine. *Now four persons bought him: one of them was Miliuc. From him Patrick received the name Cothraige, because he served four houses. So he had four names, to wit, Sucait his name from his parents: Cothraige while he was serving four persons: Magonius [compare the Sanskrit word *Maghavan*] from [his teacher] Ger-*

### Regarding Verses 7, 8:
->Jocelin, Chapter II, 12:<- After six years had passed... while he was praying one day, the Angel of the Lord appeared to him, standing upon the ledge of a certain prominent rock... He added that soon the yoke of slavery would be shaken from his neck... And although it is not necessary to judge heavenly spirits by human names, the Angel, beautifully dressed in a human form assumed from the air, called himself Victor... He also added that a ship, prepared to carry him to Britain, was standing in the harbor... The vision and the speech of the Angel vanished; and his footprints, impressed into the rock even to the present day, are remembered in the territory of Dalaradia <note>An ancient kingdom in north-east Ireland</note>, on Mount Mis <note>Slemish Mountain in County Antrim</note>.

### Regarding Verses 9—18:
->Jocelin, Chapter III, 17:<- . . . After some time had passed, while settled in his father's house, he saw in a vision of the night a man of pleasant face and habit, as if bringing many letters from Ireland, and handing one to him to read. Looking at it, he read and found written at the beginning of the letter: *This is the voice of the Irish.* For having read the beginning, when he wished to proceed in reading, it seemed to him in spirit that he heard the Irish infants enclosed in their mothers' wombs, crying out with a clear voice: "We beg you, holy boy Patrick, that you come and walk among us, and deliver us" . . . He consulted the Angel of great counsel on this matter; and that he should leave his country and parents and seek the Gauls <note>Modern-day France</note>, for the sake of learning the doctrine and discipline of the Christian faith, he received a divine oracle through the Angel Victor.

18. Taught and led by heaven . . . passing through his native soil of Britain, he reached the borders of the Gauls. And lest he should run in vain, or teach what he had not learned, he joined Saint Germanus, the Bishop of Auxerre <note>original: *Autisidorensi*</note>; and so that he might progress more fully in Christian religion and learning, he stayed with him for a period of eighteen years, reading and fulfilling the sacred Scriptures . . . Both had received a divine oracle: Patrick, namely, that he should stay with Saint Germanus; and the Bishop, that he should retain the same holy youth to be educated with him. This Bishop was most famous for his lineage, dignity, life, teaching, office, and signs; from him Patrick received each grade of the holy Orders, and even the priestly summit <note>the rank of priest or bishop</note> according to the institutes of the Canons . . .

21. It settled in the mind of Saint Patrick to seek the See of Saint Peter <note>The Papacy in Rome</note> founded upon the rock, and to be more richly imbued with the canonical institutes of the holy Roman Church; desiring that his journey and acts be strengthened by Apostolic authority. When he had explained what he had conceived in his heart to Blessed Germanus, Saint Germanus approved his holy purpose, joining to him the servant of Christ, Segetius <note>A companion and witness to Patrick's character</note>...

<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Fiacc’s Hymn, specifically analyzing verses 9 through 18. It follows the previous page's discussion of Patrick’s escape from slavery and addresses the geographic locations mentioned in the text, such as "Letha" and "Elpa," debating whether they refer to Italy and the Alps or Gaul and Scotland.</meta>

# I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

[...A priest, a companion on the journey, a solace in labor, a suitable witness of holy conversation. Indeed, having set out by divine instinct or angelic revelation to a certain person living on a certain island of the Tyrrhenian Sea, he withdrew as a solitary . . .] <note>This Latin passage describes Patrick's early clerical training and his retreat to an island, likely Lérins or a similar monastic site.</note>

The Scholiast <note>An ancient commentator on the text</note> and likewise *Stokes* relate *Verses 9* and *10* to *Patrick's* journey to Rome. The correctness of this interpretation has frequently been doubted, such as by the *editor* of the *Life of Saint Patrick* in the *Acts of the Saints* <note>original: *Act. Sanct.* (March II, p. 528)</note> and by *Leo* (see above, page 10). Both believe that *across Elpa* <note>original Irish: *tar Elpa*</note> does not refer to the Alps, but to *Alba*, Scotland (compare the word *Alpu* in the Accusative Singular in *Zeuss’s Grammatica Celtica* page 266), and that *Letha* is not Latium <note>The region of Italy containing Rome</note>, but *Letavia*, which is *Armorica* <note>The coastal region of northwest France, modern Brittany</note>. Thus, *Verse 9* would follow directly after *Verse 8*; since *Milchu* or *Miliucc* was a chieftain in northern Ireland, *Patrick* would necessarily have to come to Scotland first during his escape. However, the main goal was for *Patrick* to be prepared for his mission. Nowhere do we find that he first met *Germanus* in Rome; even the Latin *Life*, which reports on *Patrick's* stay in Rome, has him seek out *Germanus* in Gaul. For this reason alone, it is likely that the geographical information in *Verse 10* does not refer to Rome. Added to this is the fact that the words *in the south of Letha* <note>original Irish: *i n-deisciurt Letha*</note> would be a very strange expression for Rome, quite apart from the fact that Rome does not lie in the southern part of Latium. By what right *Autisiodorum* (now *Auxerre*), *Germanus's residence* in Gaul, could be described as being located *in the south of Letha* is another question. However, a geographical inaccuracy would not be so striking in the case of a less well-known city. If one can assume that *Letavia* was meant in a broader sense to include the entire northern half of Gaul, then *everything would be in order.*\* That *Letha* has been used in the sense of *Latium* can be doubted by no one (for example, *from Rome of Letha*, in the *Great Book of the Speckled* <note>original: *o Róim Letha, Leb. Br. 66a, 55*</note>). Compare *O'Curry’s Lectures* page 502. A remarkable passage is found in the *Life of Patrick* in the *Great Book of the Speckled* <note>original: *Lebar Brecc*</note> page 26a line 36: [afterward the angel brought him into *Armorica of Letha* to the city named Capua on Mount Armon . . .] Here *Latium* is even designated by *Arboric Letha*, that is, *Armorica Letavia*.

The information communicated from Chapter III, 17 of the *Life* refers to verses 15—18. Similarly in the Irish *Life* in the *Great Book of the Speckled*, page 25b line 65: [. . . the angel came to him in his sleep with many letters with him in the Irish language <note>original: *tria Goedeilg*</note>, and while he was reading them, he heard a great cry of infants in the wombs of their mothers in the regions of Connacht. Those children in the wood of Fochlad, it was they who said this: *Come, holy Patrick, to save us* <note>original Latin: *veni sancte Patrici salvos nos facere*</note>.] Compare *Stokes’ Goidelica* page 130. In *our hymn*, however, it *does not* state that *Patrick only dreamed* this call of the children. Also the

---

\* The matter becomes evident through the *Book of Armagh* <note>original Latin: *Lib. Arm.*</note> section 7 (*Goidelica*): “*Patrick and Isserninus . . . were with Germanus in Auxerre*”, that is, “*Autissiodurum now Auxerre*”.

2*

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Fiacc's Hymn, shifting from the debate over Patrick’s travels in Gaul and Italy to the specific prophecies made by Irish Druids regarding his arrival. It provides both Latin and Old Irish versions of these famous "Adze-head" prophecies.</meta>

# I 2 Fiacc's Hymn.

The Scholiast <note>An ancient commentator who added explanatory notes to the manuscript</note> does not seem to have accepted this <note>referring to the previous page's debate about Patrick's journey to Scotland or Rome</note>, as is evident from the gloss *throughout Ireland* <note>original Irish: *fo hErinn*</note> accompanying the phrase *it was heard* <note>original Irish: *ro clos*</note> (Verse 16).

### Regarding Verses 19–22:

> Jocelin, Chapter III, 26: . . . However, as Patrick was about to step onto dry land, a multitude of pagans, awaiting his arrival, met and blocked him. For the magicians and soothsayers of that region, by conjuring or prophesying, foreknew that the island would be converted through Patrick's preaching, and they predicted his arrival long before in these words: "One with a shaven head <note>referring to the Roman clerical tonsure</note> shall come in a circle, with his crooked wooden stick <note>his crosier</note>; his table shall be in the east of his house, and his people shall stand behind him, and from his table he shall chant wickedness <note>Jocelin writes from the perspective of the pagan druids who saw the Mass as 'wickedness'</note>, and all his household shall respond: so be it, so be it <note>original Latin: *fiat, fiat*, the equivalent of *Amen*</note>. But when he arrives, he will destroy our gods, overturn temples and altars, lead crowds away after him, subject the kings resisting him, or remove them from their midst, and his teaching shall reign forever . . ."

An Irish version of this druidic prophecy is found in a marginal note to Verse 34, in which the names of the Druids are also mentioned: *these are the druids, namely Luchru and Lucutmel, and this is what they used to say:*

> The Adze-head <note>original Irish: *tálcend*; a druidic nickname for Patrick, mocking his tonsure</note> will come over the wild sea,
> his cloak hole-headed <note>referring to a hooded cowl</note>, his staff crook-headed,
> his table in the east <note>correction: the original text reads 'west' but should be 'east'</note> of his house,
> all his people will answer:
> amen, amen.

Likewise in the Irish *Life*, *Speckled Book* <note>original: *Leabhar Breac*</note> page 26a, line 64: *for his druids had prophesied to Lóegaire the coming of Patrick to Ireland five years before his arrival, namely Lochra and Lothrach and Lucat the Bald and Renell were their names, and this is what they used to say:*

> The Adze-head will come over the wild sea,
> his cloak <gloss>that is, a cowl</gloss> hole-headed, his staff <gloss>that is, a crosier</gloss> crook-headed,
> his table <gloss>that is, his altar</gloss> in the east of his house,
> all his people will answer:
> amen, amen.

Compare O'Donovan's Supplement to O'Reilly under the entry *Tailginn*.

### Regarding Verses 23–42:

These verses generally describe Patrick's holy life and works. A factual explanation of Verse 29 would be desirable, but I am unable to provide one. Since all other passages are immediately understandable, I will refrain from providing corresponding passages (such as from Jocelin’s *Life* chapter XVIII, 160, or the *Speckled Book* page 29a, line 66 onwards), and will only cite for Verse 31 an almost literally identical passage from the Irish *Life*...
