# I 2 Fiacc's Hymn. 21

(*Speckled Book* <note>original: *Lebor Brecc*, page 29b, line 5</note>): in the fourth <note>original: *in cethrumad*; original German: *den vierten Theil der Nacht*</note> part of the night [he lay] on bare earth with a stone under his head and a wet cloak around him.

### Regarding Verses 45—50:

Jocelin, Chapter XIX, 164: After these things, Patrick, the beloved of the Lord, full of days and merits . . . sees the evening of his life approaching: while he was staying in the borders of Ulster <note>original Latin: *Ulidiae*</note>, he hastened his steps toward the city of Armagh <note>original Latin: *Ardmachiam*</note>, his See. For he desired, according to his wish, to enter the way of all flesh <note>a biblical idiom for dying</note> in that place, depositing the remains of his sacred body there, and to be hidden in the womb of the mother of all <note>the earth</note> under the eyes of the sons whom he had brought forth in Christ. But an unexpected event changed the holy man's purpose . . . : for the Angel Victor met him as he was heading where he had intended, and speaking from a bush—which burned without any loss from fire—along the way, he said: "Patrick, stop your foot from the journey begun, because it is not the divine will that your life be exchanged for death in Armagh, or your body be enclosed in a sepulchral home. For in the region of Ulster, which you converted first before all the lands of Ireland <note>original Latin: *Hiberniae*</note>, the Lord has foreseen that you shall die, and be buried honorably in the city of Down <note>original Latin: *Dunensi*; modern Downpatrick</note>: there indeed shall be your resurrection; but in the city of Armagh, which you love, the administration of the grace bestowed upon you shall continue in succession. Be mindful of your word, in which you gave hope to the sons of Dichu, the first new converts of the Irish-born, when, taught from heaven, you foretold and promised that you would die and be buried in their land." The holy prelate, at first taking this a little hard, was stunned: but suddenly returning to himself, he embraced the providence of the Divine arrangement . . . and submitting his own will to God's, he returned to the parts of Ulster.

According to the **Glosses on Verse 54**, Patrick was at Saul <note>original: *Sabull*; "or Saull, Co. Down," according to *O’Curry's Lectures* page 20</note>. The Latin *Life* has only "in the borders of Ulster," but in the Irish *Life* it says (*Speckled Book* 29b, line 9): Now when the end came to Patrick while he was at Saul in Magh Inis in the territory of Ulster, he set out on the road to Armagh so that he might die there. He saw the bush <note>original: *munní*</note> on fire before him and the fire did not burn the bush, and Victor the angel speaking to him out of it, as he said to him: "You shall not go to Armagh," etc. From Saul, Armagh lay to the south <note>original Irish: *fadess*, Verse 47</note>.

### Regarding Verse 51:

According to *Stokes*, the hymn by *Secundinus* beginning with the words "Hear all ye" <note>original Latin: *Audite omnes*</note> is meant here, which begins the *Book of Hymns* <note>original Latin: *Liber Hymnorum*; see *Stokes, Goidelica* 2nd edition, 132</note>, and which is reported in *Jocelin's Life, Chapter XVIII, 155, 156*.

### Regarding Verses 53—54:

Regarding the prophecy of Bishop *Tassach*, I find nothing in the sources accessible to me. However, it is reported everywhere that *Tassach* administered the Holy Communion <note>original German: *h. Abendmahl*</note> to *Patrick* for the last time. Compare:

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Fiacc's Hymn, specifically regarding the events surrounding Saint Patrick’s death, the administration of his final communion by Bishop Tassach, and the miraculous light that followed his passing. # I 2 Fiacc's Hymn. 22

Jocelin, *Life of Patrick*, XIX, 167; Irish *Life* in the *Speckled Book* <note>original: *Lebor Brecc*, page 29b, line 23</note>: "Patrick sent forth his spirit thereafter and received communion and the sacrifice <note>the Eucharist</note> from the hand of Bishop Tassach" <note>original Irish: *Ro fáid P. a spir. iarsin ocus rogab comaind ocus sacarbaic do laim Tassaig espoic*</note>. According to the details of the hymn, Tassach was in Saul <note>original: *Sabull*</note> and administered the holy communion to Patrick before the latter set out for Armagh <note>original: *Ardmacha*</note>. He prophesied that Patrick would soon return; and it happened, for the angel prompted him to return while on his way.

### Regarding Verses 55—60:
The Irish *Life* also reports the miracle following Patrick's death in this way: "neither the light nor the angelic fragrance failed them, but it remained in the entire land until the end of a year" <note>original Irish: *ni thesta forru in t-šollsi ná in bolad angelacda, acht bói isin tír uli co cend m-bliadna*; *Speckled Book*, p. 29b, line 32</note>. However, according to the Latin *Life* (chapter XIX, 170), the continuous light lasted only twelve days.

### Regarding Verses 61—64:
Jocelin, chapter XIX, 169: "A vast multitude of people and clergy also flocked together in crowds . . . Indeed, on the following night, a light-streaming choir of angels kept celestial watch around the body, surrounding the place and all present with radiance, delighting them with fragrance, soothing them with the melody of honey-sweet psalmody, and bathing all things in spiritual joy. Therefore, the sleep of the Lord fell upon all who had gathered, and it held them in slumber until morning, until the angelic funeral rites were completed." <note>original Latin: *multitudo etiam populi et cleri copiosa catervatim confluxit . . . Nocte vero secuta lucifluus chorus angelorum circa corpus caelestes excubias exhibuit...*</note>

### Regarding Verses 65, 66:
I find nothing in the other sources accessible to me concerning the simultaneous death of Patrick and Old-Patrick <note>original: *Senphatraic*; the "Sen" prefix means "Old" or "Senior"</note>, nor regarding the prophecy mentioned in the gloss. According to common tradition, Old-Patrick was instead the successor of Patrick, and this had been prophesied to him by Patrick when he was a boy of seven years; he is said to have outlived Patrick by ten years <note>referencing *Acts of the Saints*, March vol. II, p. 583, 13. 14</note>. Concerning the name of Old-Patrick and the relationship between the two Patricks, the editor of Jocelin's *Life* <note>on page 519, 12</note> says: ". . . but we have enough to suggest in a few words that the other was a brother to Patrick, Sennanus [sic] by name, a deacon by rank, who, before receiving holy orders, was the father of the younger Patrick; this younger Patrick held the primacy of Armagh second after his holy uncle, and as will be said below, was called Saint Sen-patrick—not as if 'Senior Patrick' (although the meaning of the word *sen* points that way), but 'Senanus's Patrick'." <note>original Latin: *sed satis habemus insinuare paucis, alterum Patricio fuisse fratrem, Sennanum... non quasi senior Patricius... sed Senani Patricius*</note>

<meta>This page transitions from the discussion of Fiacc's Hymn to a new section focusing on "Níníne's Prayer," another significant early Irish poem dedicated to Saint Patrick found in the Liber Hymnorum. I 3 Ninine's Prayer.

### 3. Níníne's Prayer.

This short, yet grandly composed glorification of Saint Patrick is arguably the most poetically beautiful piece in the *Liber Hymnorum* <note>The Book of Hymns, a 11th-century manuscript containing much older Irish and Latin liturgical texts</note>. The preface consists only of the words: Níníne the poet made this prayer <note>original Irish: *Níníne écess doríne in n-orthain-sse*</note> *or* Fiacc of Slebte.

A print of the text and the glosses <note>explanatory annotations</note>, not entirely accurate despite being based on a new collation <note>a critical comparison of manuscripts</note> of Stokes’ *Goidelica* [1], was shared by Rev. J. F. Shearman in the *Journal of the Royal Historical and Archaeological Association of Ireland*, July 1874 (No. 19) p. 197: apart from some minor inaccuracies, the error of *dorchraide* for *dorchaide* <note>meaning "dark" or "gloomy"</note> in Verse 9 remained here. The translation is also an unchanged reprint of Stokes' first attempt.

Regarding the division of the verses, I have only diverged from Stokes insofar as he presents verses 3, 4, 5 and 7, 8, 9 without division. If one removes "prime apostle" <note>original Irish: *primabstal*</note> from Verse 7, a certain system in the syllable counts of the verses can be recognized (7 + 5 . 7 + 5 ; 10 . 5 . 9 ; 10 . 5 . 9 ; 10 . 10).

->We invoke* Saint Patrick, the prime apostle of Ireland,<-
->famous is his wonderful name, a flame that baptizes the heathens!<-
->He fought against hard-hearted druids,<-
->4 he subdued* the proud ones<-
->with the help of our Lord of fair heaven,<-
->he purified* Ireland’s meadow-lands, a mighty birth.*<-

---

1 <gloss>that is, we place ourselves in his trust.</gloss> *Compare* "venerate his feast" <note>original: *admuinter a feli*</note> from the *Martyrology of Oengus* (*Oct. 2*) with the gloss: <gloss>that is, he is wondered at, that is, he is blessed</gloss>, similarly in *Hymn* 5, 98. *Stokes translates, misled by the etymologizing gloss:* „We put trust.“

4 <gloss>that is, beautifully he subdued.</gloss>

6 <term>fonenaig</term> <note>purified/cleansed</note>: <gloss>that is, he washed, that is, he performed his washing, that is, his cleansing.</gloss>
<term>mórgein</term> <note>great birth</note>: <gloss>Patrick is the great birth, or great is the mouth of those who are praying to him, that is, the mouths of all the men of Ireland.</gloss> *Stokes translates now (Remarks² p. 69):* „He purified Erin's meadow-lands, a mighty birth.“

I 4 Ultan's Hymn.

> we pray to Patrick [prime apostle],
> 8 who will save us* in the judgment of his sentencing*
> from the ill-wills of dark demons.
> God be with me through the prayer of Patrick, prime apostle!

### 4. Ultan's Hymn
->to Brigit.<-

This hymn is said to have been composed by Ultan, Abbot of Ardbreccan, who died in the year 653 according to the *Chronicon Scotorum* <note>The "Chronicle of the Irish," a medieval Irish chronicle</note> (or in 656 according to the *Annals of the Four Masters*, as noted by Stokes). However, the uncertainty of the tradition is expressed by the fact that it is also attributed to Columcille (died 595), Broccán Clóen (6th or 7th century), three men from the community <note>original Irish: *munter*</note> of Brigit (who died in 523), and Brenainn <note>Saint Brendan the Navigator, died 565</note>.

In each line there are five syllables, and the rhyme always consists of a two-syllable word.

> Brigit, ever-good woman, a golden sparkling flame*,
> may the bright, shining sun lead us* to the eternal kingdom*!

---

7 **prime apostle** <note>original Irish: *prímabstal*</note>: I consider this to be a later addition; see the introductory remarks.
8 **who will save us**: <gloss>who will save us, i.e., he will perform our rescue.</gloss> — **in the judgment of his sentencing**: <gloss>i.e., regarding the judgment of the Last Day.</gloss> Stokes translates this as: 
> „We pray to Patrick, chief apostle who will save us at (the) Judgment from doom to (the) malevolences of dark demons.“
Without wishing to generally dispute the existence of a *T-future* <note>a specific grammatical future tense used in Old Irish</note> in Irish (cf. Stokes, *Beitr.* VII, 28), I cannot readily accept *donn esmart* as such a form. Whether *do esmart* is truly synonymous with *do essurc* or *tessurc* (to save), as the glossator <note>the person who wrote the explanatory notes in the manuscript</note> indicates, is doubtful to me; also, the preposition *do* in Verse 9 must be taken into account.
1^b **golden flame**: compare *bréo óir* <note>flame of gold</note>, an address to Brigit in a poem shared in Z.^2 961.
2^a **may [it] lead us**: <gloss>i.e., may he bring us.</gloss> *bithlaith* <note>eternal kingdom</note> and *taidlech* <note>shining</note> are variants found in the manuscript.

A

# I 5 Broccan's Hymnus.

May Brigit save us
past throngs of demons;
<margin>4</margin> may she win before us
the battles of every plague.

May she destroy within us
the taxes <note>original Irish: *císu*. Here used metaphorically for the "debts" or burdens of fleshly sin.</note> of our flesh;
the branch with blossoms,
the mother of Jesus <note>A traditional honorific for Brigit, often called "the Mary of the Gaels."</note>.

The beloved true virgin
with immense dignity;
<margin>8</margin> I shall be free at all times
with my saint of Leinster <note>original Irish: *Laignib*. Brigit's primary monastery was in Kildare, within the province of Leinster.</note>.

One of two pillars <term>lethcholba: literally "half-pillar," meaning one of a pair of support columns</term> of the kingdom
along with prime Patrick;
a robe over graves <note>The meaning of "uasligaib" is contested; it may refer to a shroud or a covering over sacred places.</note>
is the royal queen.

May our bodies be in haircloth <term>cilice: a garment made of coarse animal hair worn against the skin as a form of penance</term>
after old age;
<margin>12</margin> may her grace sprinkle us,
may Brigit save us.

->Brigit, woman.<-

> Through the praise of Brigit we pray to Christ,
> that we may deserve to possess the heavenly kingdom. Amen. <note>original Latin: "Brigtae per laudem Christum precamur, ut nos celeste regnum habere mereamur. amen."</note>

---

### 5. Broccán’s Hymnus.

This hymn bears the full character of the poems that are so often interspersed within the legendary tales of the Irish. The skill of the poet here, apart from the versification, consists in expressing well-known matters as briefly as possible—

5^a **destroy**: <gloss>i.e., may she destroy.</gloss>
7^a **true virgin** *Ms.* **beloved**: <gloss>i.e., by us or by everyone.</gloss>
8^b **Leinster**: probably to be read as *Lagnaib*.
9 <gloss>i.e., Patrick is the head of the men of Ireland, Brigit is the head of the women of Ireland.</gloss>
9^a **one of two pillars**: <gloss>i.e., Brigit.</gloss> <margin>As there are two pillars in the world [supporting a house], so are Brigit and Patrick in Ireland.</margin> **of the kingdom**: <gloss>i.e., of the sovereignty of Ireland.</gloss>
10^a **over graves**: <gloss>i.e., over decencies/ornaments.</gloss>
10^b **royal**: *Stokes Goidelica* 2nd ed. p. 184; the manuscript says *ríga*. Stokes translates: „the vesture over liga (?), the royal Queen.“ The meaning of this passage is still questionable. In the gloss, one should certainly read *uas socraidib* <note>original German: "uasocraidib" in the text above</note> (*Stokes Goid.* 2nd ed. p. 137 note); perhaps also *uas sligaib* [over roads] in the text? **robe**: Stokes understands *intlacht* as *in tlacht*, the garment (?).
11^a <gloss>i.e., after; i.e., that path.</gloss>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán's Hymn, specifically discussing its literary structure and the historical context of its authorship and subject, Saint Brigit.</meta>

# I 5 Broccan's Hymnus.

to condense [information], as if using only keywords. If one is not already familiar with the subject matter from elsewhere, these verses are often just as difficult to understand as a *Sanskrit Sûtra* without a commentary. I have therefore shared those chapters from the *Life of Saint Brigit* <note>original Latin: *Vita S. Brigittae*</note> attributed to *Cogitosus* (*Acts of the Saints* <note>original Latin: *Acta Sanctorum*</note>, Feb. 1) which are important for understanding this hymn, providing some in full and others with non-essential sentences omitted (see the appendix after the text). It is striking that the sequence of miracles in the hymn is the same as in the aforementioned *Life*. This agreement cannot be accidental; other *Lives* follow a different arrangement. Regarding *Broccán*, with the epithet *cloen* <note>meaning "the squint-eyed" or "crooked"</note>, the preface says nothing more than that *Ultan*, Abbot of *Ardbraccan*, was his teacher <term>aite: an Old Irish term for a foster-father or tutor</term>, and that Ultan prompted him to undertake the task of compiling the miracles of Brigit in the shortest possible form and in poetic meter. According to the *Chronicle of the Scots* <note>original Latin: *Chronicon Scotorum*</note>, *Ultan* died in the year 653. On the other hand, according to the preface, the hymn was supposedly composed under King *Lugaid*, whose death the *Chronicle of the Scots* reports under the year 507. Neither of these claims deserves credit. The author of this hymn was likely placed in connection with *Ultan* because the latter is said to have been the first to collect the miracles of Brigit. The language is archaic; the fairly numerous perfect verb forms are particularly noteworthy.

*St. Brigit* and *St. Patrick* are the national saints of Ireland. However, while Patrick belongs solely to Christian hagiography <note>the study of the lives of saints</note>, Brigit appears at the same time to be the heir of an ancient pagan deity. Her character contains traits that suggest more than just a canonized nun behind her. I am referring less to the miracles attributed to her—though perhaps the nature of these is also not without significance—than to the fact that she is repeatedly called one of the mothers of Christ (Lines 4, 63), that Christ is repeatedly called her son (Line 83), and that she is equated with Mary (Line 105). A gloss in the *Book of Hymns* <note>original Latin: *Liber Hymnorum*</note> folio 2b (*Goidilica* <note>A collection of Old Irish glosses edited by Whitley Stokes</note> page 63) says outright:

<summary>This page analyzes the literary origins and historical problems of Broccán’s Hymn, noting its structural reliance on Cogitosus's Life of Brigit and the saint's potential origins as a pre-Christian deity.</summary>
<keywords>Broccán Clóen, Saint Brigit, Cogitosus, Ultan of Ardbraccan, Irish hagiography, pagan origins, philology, Chronicon Scotorum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán's Hymn, specifically interpreting individual verses and comparing them with other medieval Irish texts like the Leabhar Breac (the "Speckled Book") and the Martyrology of Oengus.</meta>

# I 5 Broccan's Hymn.

...for she is "Brigit, the Mary of the Gaels" <note>original Irish: "Brigit Maire na n-Goidel," a gloss on the Latin phrase "virginem Mariae sanctae similem," meaning "a virgin similar to holy Mary"</note>. Exactly the same remark is found twice in the Irish *Life of Brigit* contained in the *Speckled Book* <note>original Irish: *Leabhar Breac*, a famous 15th-century ecclesiastical manuscript</note>; see page 63, column a, line 10, and page 66, column a, line 50 of the lithograph edition. It is natural here to recall the ancient Gaulish *Matres* <note>The "Mothers," a group of Celtic female deities often depicted in trios</note>.

In the appendix, several passages from the aforementioned Irish *Life* in the *Speckled Book* have been incorporated into the Latin texts, along with some other Irish pieces.

Every half-line has seven syllables.

> Victorious Brigit loved not the world;
> she sat the seat of a bird on a cliff,
> the saint slept a captive’s sleep
> for the sake of her son <note>referring to Christ</note>.
> Not much of slander was found in her;
> [she held] the Trinity with noble faith,
> 4 Brigit, mother of my Great King
> of heaven's kingdom—best of those born.

**1ᵃ** *Victorious Brigit* <note>original: *Brigit buadach*</note>, likewise in the *Martyrology of Oengus* <note>original: *Félire Óengusso*, Prologue 191</note>.
**2ᵇ** The manuscript uses *nóib* <note>saint</note>; otherwise, *oe* is predominantly used in this hymn, and *oi* only appears when a "slender" vowel <note>the linguistic term for 'i' or 'e'</note> has influenced the syllable in question (see verse 90ᵇ). The phrase *for the sake of her son* <note>original: *ar écnairc ammaicc*</note> is translated by Whitley Stokes <note>a prominent 19th-century Celtic scholar</note> as "because of her son's absence," whereas in verse 35, he translates *ar écnairc arríg* as "for love of her king." Compare *ar écnairc na sluag sa*, "for intercession of these hosts," in the *Martyrology of Oengus*, Prologue 266.
**3ᵃ** *Not much of slander* <note>original: *ni mór n-ecnaig*; compare with the phrase "much of evil" in Zeuss's *Grammatica Celtica*, p. 916</note>: a glossator notes "it was not easy [to find] her slander." The glossator must have connected the word *etaide* <note>found/obtained</note> to the following part. To this, the gloss adds: "it is she who had the noble faith of the Trinity." Stokes now translates this <note>in *Remarks on the Facsimiles of Irish Manuscripts*, p. 69</note> as: "not much of carping was found (in her): with the noble faith of Trinity she lived." However, *Trinity* <note>original: *trínóit*</note> can only be in the nominative or accusative case. Regarding *étaide*, compare the form "you would not find" <note>original: *ní co n-étada*</note>; however, here it is likely the 3rd person singular present secondary passive <term>a verb form indicating an ongoing action in the past, similar to "was being found"</term> used in the sense of an imperfect tense.
**4ᵇ** *Best of those born* <note>original: *ferr cinis*</note>: the glossator interprets this as "it is best she was born." The glossator seems to have connected "of heaven's kingdom" to the preceding phrase. Stokes translates: "of heaven's kingdom best was she born" (?). Neither "best" <note>original: *ferr*</note> nor the archaic word for birth <note>original: *cinis*</note> could be easily combined with the genitive case. However, the same construction is found in the *Martyrology of Oengus* for September 24: "The conception of noble John the Baptist is greatest of my stories, but of humans, Jesus is the most wonderful who was born." The same thought is expressed more clearly regarding Saint Columba <note>original: *Columcille*</note> in the *Speckled Book*, page 33, column b, line 46: "There was not born of the Gaels a child who was nobler, nor wiser, nor of better lineage than Columba."

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán's Hymn, specifically focusing on stanzas 5 through 14. # I 5 Broccan's Hymn.

> She was no slanderer, she was not malicious,
> she was no friend of vigorous women's-battles <note>The term "banchath" (women's battle) is debated; it may refer to secular strife or, as a gloss suggests, a form of "red martyrdom."</note>,
> she was no striking, spotted serpent,
> she did not sell the Son of God for gain.
> 
> She was not greedy for treasures,
> she gave without bitterness, without hesitation,
<margin>8</margin> > she was not a hard, stingy miser,
> she loved not the consumption of the world.
> 
> She was not harsh toward guests,
> she was kind to the wretched and weak,
> upon the plain she built a city <note>original: *cathir*, referring to the monastic city of Kildare</note>
> which protects hosts [on their way] to the Kingdom.
> 
> She was no mountain shepherdess,
> she dwelled in the heart of a plain,
<margin>12</margin> > a wonderful ladder for the laity
> to ascend to the kingdom of Mary's son.
> 
> Wonderful is the congregation of Saint Brigit,
> wonderful the name with fame <note>The original "plea co n-hualai" is obscure; it may refer to the "fame of her name."</note>,
> she was alone with Christ [in her keeping],
> a gathering frequent for guests.

---

**5ᵃ** *she was no slanderer*: <gloss>i.e., she was not a slanderer, i.e., she did not slander anyone.</gloss> *In this meaning, the word "écnaire" is not otherwise attested; perhaps a corruption of the text is present here.*

**5ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., she did not love the battle of the sorrowful women.</gloss> *Accordingly, Stokes translates:* "she was not fond of vehement woman’s-wars." *However, should we then not also expect "banchath m-brigach" in the text? — Compare the Martyrology of Oengus <note>original: *Félire Óengusso*</note> for June 8: "after victory and woman-battle," where the latter word is glossed as "the red martyrdom."*

**7ᵃ** <gloss>i.e., she was not greedy for treasures.</gloss>

**8ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., she did not love the world... i.e., the consumption of the world for herself.</gloss> *Stokes translates:* "she loved not the world’s pastime".

**9ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., beautifully she used to be, i.e., showing pity for the wretched weak.</gloss>

**10ᵃ** *upon the plain*: <gloss>i.e., of Leinster</gloss> &nbsp; *she built*: <gloss>i.e., she constructed</gloss> &nbsp; *a city*: <gloss>i.e., Kildare <note>original: *Celldara*</note>.</gloss>

**10ᵇ** *to the Kingdom*: <gloss>i.e., of it</gloss> &nbsp; *which protects*: <gloss>i.e., Brigit or the city</gloss> &nbsp; *In Goidelica² <note>Whitley Stokes' collection of Old Irish glosses</note> p. 142, Stokes translated:* "on a plain she built a town: to God’s kingdom she convoyed hosts"; *in Remarks² p. 69 he translates:* "a town sheltered her: when she went (thence), it protected hosts" (?). *I believe, along with the glossator, that "dollaid" stands for "do flaith" [to the kingdom], compare "derglaid" in Verse 38, i.e., "red kingdom." The same construction appears in the Martyrology of Oengus for March 6 (in the Speckled Book <note>original: *Leabhar Breac*</note>): "May they protect us after diligence to the kingdom of God." The difficulty of our passage lies in the word "arutacht" (compare "ardoutacht" in Verse 73) and in the infixed pronoun contained in "ron snade."*

**12ᵃ** <gloss>i.e., the city or Brigit.</gloss> , **12ᵇ** *to ascend* in the Manuscript.

**13ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., she escaped... i.e., from her very beautiful order she was with power.</gloss> *An obscure passage, which certainly relates to the legend that I share in the Appendix from the Speckled Book.*

**14ᵃ** <gloss>i.e., she was around Christ alone in her taking <note>or "in danger"</note>.</gloss>

**14ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., which is customary for guests, or: her assembly was frequent for the wretched.</gloss>

<summary>This page provides a translated analysis of Broccán's Hymn, focusing on Brigit's rejection of worldly greed and her role as the founder of the monastic city of Kildare. The editor debates various translations by Whitley Stokes, particularly regarding the obscure term "banchath" and the linguistic structure of her "city" protecting hosts.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological commentary on Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit. The previous page discussed her ascetic virtues, her protection of Kildare, and her role as a "ladder" for the laity. This page focuses on the moment of her veiling by Maccaille and compares her sleepless devotion to the asceticism of Saint Kevin of Glendalough.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

> It was a good hour when Maccaille <note>A 5th-century bishop associated with Brigit’s profession</note> set the veil over Saint Brigit’s head;
<margin>16</margin> > she was clear in her wanderings, her prayer was heard in heaven.
> 
> It is God I pray to against every battle, in whatever way my lips may be satisfied;
> deeper than the seas, beyond counting, Three in one Person—a marvel of a story.
> 
> Kevin of fame announced for the battle, the wind moves snow through the
>    storm;
<margin>20</margin> > in Glendalough he suffered the cross <note>referring to a "cross-vigil," an ascetic practice of praying with arms outstretched</note>, so that he reached peace after his fill [of hardship].
> 
> Saint Brigit was not sleepy, she was not cold-hearted regarding the love of God;
> she neither bartered for nor sought the vanishing things of this present life.

---

**15ᵃ** *it was a good hour*: <gloss>i.e., a good omen.</gloss> *Maccaille*: <gloss>i.e., he was the son of the mother of Bishop Mel, and it was he who blessed the veil on Brigit’s head. Maccaille took the veil ... over her head, while Mel was blessing the veil.</gloss>
**17ᵃ** *I pray to him*: <gloss>i.e., I pray to him.</gloss> <note>Compare "not guidiu" in the *Félire Óengusso* (Martyrology of Oengus), Prologue line 265.</note>
**17ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., every way, i.e., they shall satisfy.</gloss> <note>Regarding the form *sasad* with a *d*, compare *comdar* in Verse 42 (as opposed to *comtar* in Verse 44).</note>
**18ᵃ** *deeper than the seas*: <gloss>i.e., deeper than the sea <note>original Latin: *fudumnu quam mare*</note>.</gloss> *beyond counting*: <gloss>i.e., more than a person can tell <note>original Latin: *quam potest hominem narrare*</note>.</gloss> <note>Regarding the construction, see Zeuss² page 917. Similarly: "The counting and recounting of the miracles God performed is more numerous..." from the *Leabhar Breac*, page 33ᵇ, line 42.</note>
**18ᵇ** "a marvel of a story" — so Stokes translates it; <note>compare "it was a marvel of a course" in Hymn II, 9.</note>
**19–20** <margin>On this, the marginal note says:</margin> <gloss>Brigit used to prophesy concerning holy, famous Kevin, that the wind would move him through snow and storm under the cross in Glendalough. For it is told that Kevin remained for seven years standing without sleep, with a cross of his own height around him... just as Kevin was under the cross without sleep, so Saint Brigit was not sleepy.</gloss> <note>The verbal forms in the text present difficulties: *fuacru* is called a "U-imperfect" by Whitley Stokes in *Beiträge VII, 54* (compare the use of *no thercanad* in the gloss, hence Stokes translates: "she prophesied"); Stokes takes *luades* as an S-future (because of *luaithfed* in the gloss), but I doubt that such a form was produced from this denominative verb. Based on the form, it would be closest to take it as *foedes* in Hymn 1, 33. Verse 20 is also not interpreted as indirect speech by Stokes.</note>
**20ᵃ** <note>Stokes rightly remarks that we should expect "in Glind dá locho"; perhaps "in Glind dá" is to be written (compare the *Félire Óengusso*, June 3: "Kevin, the chaste, fair warrior of Glendalough"). Compare also "Glendalough of the two lakes," *Félire Óengusso*, Prologue line 196.</note>
**20ᵇ** <note>Stokes translates inaccurately: "so that he possessed peace after trouble."</note>
**21ᵇ** <gloss>i.e., it was not after intervals [occasionally] that the love of God was with her, but she had it always <note>original Latin: *sed semper habebat*</note>.</gloss>

<summary>This page analyzes stanzas 16-21 of Broccán's Hymn, detailing St. Brigit's veiling by Maccaille and her legendary endurance. A significant portion of the commentary compares her constant devotion to the ascetic rigor of Saint Kevin of Glendalough, who famously prayed standing for seven years.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Brigit, Saint Kevin (Coemgen), Glendalough, Maccaille, monastic veiling, Old Irish philology, cross-vigil, Whitley Stokes, asceticism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit. Having previously discussed her veiling and ascetic vigils, the text now moves to specific miracles (virtutes) performed by the saint, drawing parallels between the Irish verse and the Latin prose life of Brigit by Cogitosus.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

<margin>23</margin>What the King [God] performed of miracles for Saint Brigit,
<margin>24</margin>if they were performed for a human, where has the ear of any living person heard [the like]?

<margin>25</margin>The first herd to which she was sent with the first butter in a wagon,
<margin>26</margin>she took nothing from the provision of her guests, nor did she diminish their attachment.

<margin>27</margin>A load of bacon afterwards in the evening, it was a great triumph;
<margin>28</margin>though the dog was satiated by it, the guest was not sorrowful.

<margin>29</margin>On a day of reaping for her, it was well-reaped; no fault was found there by my pious one [Brigit];
<margin>30</margin>it was dry always in her field, though it rained upon the world.

---

<margin>23</margin> *What he performed... of miracles:* <note>original Old Irish: "andorigenai . . . do fertaib"</note> compare verse 67 and "what good he has done for me" in the Würzburg Glosses 30a.
<margin>24a</margin> The manuscript reads *niadorontai*; *ma dorontai* (if they were performed) is a conjecture by O'Beirne Crowe. Stokes adopts this in *Remarks² p. 69* and translates: "What the king wrought of miracles for St. Brigte, if it hath been wrought for man, where hath ear of any one living heard [it]?" The same phrasing appears in a Latin hymn for Brigit found in the *Book of Hymns* (folio 2b), shared by Shearman in the *Journal of the Royal Historical and Archaeological Association of Ireland* (July 1874): 
<note>original Latin: "Ymnus iste angelice summeque sancte Brigite Fari non valet omnia virtutum mirabilia Que nostris nunquam auribus si sint facta audivimus Nisi per istam virginem Marie sancte similem."</note> 
"This hymn of the angelic and most holy Brigit is unable to speak of all the miracles of her virtues, which our ears have never heard of being performed, except through this virgin who is like unto holy Mary."
<margin>25 26</margin> See Cogitosus <note>author of the earliest Life of Brigit</note>, chapter 4.
<margin>25b</margin> <gloss>i.e., her mistress brought a wagon to her to bring butter in it.</gloss> Stokes translates: "The first herd to which she was sent with first butter in a chariot."
<margin>26a</margin> <gloss>i.e., for feeding the poor.</gloss>
<margin>26b</margin> <gloss>i.e., the attachment the guests felt for her.</gloss> Stokes translates: "She took not from her guest's stock, she diminished not their attachment." The versions of this miracle provided in the appendix cannot be fully reconciled with the hints in this verse. However, "from her guest's stock" is likely incorrect (it would require writing *di rath*). I would point to *do raith* (entirely/at once) in verses 61 and 87. Compare Hymn 4, 12.
<margin>27 28</margin> See Cogitosus, chapter 6. <note>This refers to the miracle where Brigit gave bacon to a hungry dog, but the quantity of bacon remained undiminished for the guests.</note>
<margin>29 30</margin> See Cogitosus, chapter 7. <note>This refers to the miracle where Brigit’s harvest stayed dry during a storm.</note>
<margin>29a</margin> *it was well-reaped:* <gloss>i.e., it was successfully harvested.</gloss> Compare "who was happily born of Mary" in the *Martyrology of Oengus*, Prologue 251. **by my pious one:** <gloss>i.e., by Brigit.</gloss>
<margin>30a</margin> *always* according to Stokes, for *co oidchi* (until night). <gloss>"Always," i.e., "until night,"</gloss> per O'Clery.
<margin>30b</margin> *cia* (though) is missing in the manuscript; added by Stokes.

<summary>This page lists and provides philological commentary on several of Saint Brigit's miracles, including the multiplication of butter, the feeding of a dog with bacon without loss to the guests, and the protection of her harvest from rain. The editor compares these verses with early Latin hagiographies and previous translations by Whitley Stokes.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Brigit, miracles, Cogitosus, Whitley Stokes, Old Irish poetry, multiplication of food, hagiography, philology</keywords>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and theological commentary on Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit. The previous page concluded with miracles concerning the weather and agricultural abundance. This page moves into specific acts of charity and the transformation of elements, such as milk, ale, and salt, while continuing to cross-reference the text with the Latin "Life of Brigit" by Cogitosus.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

Bishops came to visit her; the danger was not small for her,
<margin>32</margin> had the King [God] not provided help\* [by increasing] the milk of the cows threefold.

She tended sheep on a day of heavy rain in the middle of the plain;
scarcely had she finished when she spread her cloak in the house upon a sunbeam.\*

The fierce boy who entreated her, out of love for Brigit and for the sake of her King,
<margin>36</margin> she gave away seven wethers [rams] from it; yet the number of her flock did not decrease.

It is for me to tell\* the good she has done;
a wonder for her was the bathwater\* she blessed\*—around her it became red ale.\*

She blessed the pregnant nun; she became healthy without pain or disease.\*
<margin>40</margin> A greater wonder was another\*: out of a stone she made salt.

---

31 32 *Cogitosus, chapter 8.* 31<sup>a</sup> i.e., "if he gave help." *Stokes, Remarks*<sup>2</sup> p. 33, compares *fu-r-ráith* (which helped), and *fu-m-ré-se* (it will help me) from the *Book of Armagh*, recalling the Greek <note>original Greek: ὑπη-ρετεῖν</note> "to serve" or "to assist." However, he translates: "if it had not been that the king increased the cow's milk threefold." The verse has one syllable too many, so *Stokes, Goidelica*<sup>2</sup> wrote *fo-raid* (omitting *ro*). Compare *He helped in great grief and disease, Calendar of Óengus, Jan. 15*, and the gloss: i.e., he helped those in sickness.

33 34 *Cogitosus, chapter 9.* 33<sup>b</sup> i.e., in the Plain of Liffey. 34<sup>b</sup> i.e., on the sunbeam, that is, on the rays of the sun that were in her right hand. <note>The miracle of hanging a wet cloak on a sunbeam to dry is a common motif in Celtic hagiography, signifying the saint's harmony with nature.</note>

35 36 *Cogitosus, chapter 10.* 35<sup>b</sup> *Stokes* understands "Brigit" here as the Accusative case (*The hard youth besought her, Brigit, for love of her king*; more correctly: *the hard youth who . . .*), while in a similar passage in Verse 43 (*Remarks*<sup>2</sup> p. 70) he now correctly translates it as a Genitive case. 36<sup>b</sup> *Stokes* translates: "her flock's number she lessened not"; more precisely: *her flock, she did not diminish the number of it.*

37<sup>a</sup> *matchous*: i.e., "if I should tell." However, *matchous* stands for *ma atchous*, 1st person singular future of "to set forth" or "to expose" (compare *Stokes, Contributions VII* 45). Since *sous* (for *so-fus*) is two syllables, while *-chous* (with only an intrusive *u*) is measured as one syllable, *ma atchous* should be included in the text for the sake of the meter.

38 *Cogitosus, chapter 11.* 38<sup>a</sup> *the bathwater*: i.e., in which she herself was.
38<sup>b</sup> *senta*: i.e., "she blessed" or "she hallowed." Regarding this form, which is not yet sufficiently explained, see *Zeuss*<sup>2</sup> 456 and *Stokes, Contributions VII*, 27. But could it not be understood here like *fechta* in *Hymn 2, 57*?
*ba derglaid*: i.e., "it was a red liquid" or "it was red ale." <note>In early Irish tradition, "red ale" is often a metaphor for sovereignty and divine favor.</note>

39 *Cogitosus, chapter 12*; see also the gloss: i.e., a nun who was pregnant came to Brigit, and she healed her. <note>This miracle, involving the restorative blessing of a nun who had broken her vows, emphasizes Brigit’s role as a healer and protector of women.</note>

40 *Cogitosus, chapter 13.* 40<sup>a</sup> *Stokes* translates: "there was a greater marvel another [time]". I translate: "Greater than another miracle was this: from the stone she made salt." See Verses 47, 75, 80.

<summary>This page catalogs a series of miracles performed by Saint Brigit, including the multiplication of milk, drying her cloak on a sunbeam, and turning bathwater into ale. The translator provides linguistic analysis of Old Irish verbs and aligns the poetic text with the Latin biography of Brigit by Cogitosus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit. The previous page detailed miracles involving the multiplication of milk and the transformation of water into ale. This page focuses on the restoration of sight and speech, as well as the miraculous preservation of food, continuing the systematic comparison with Cogitosus’s Latin Life of Brigit.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

->I cannot recount, I cannot number all that the holy creature has done,<-
->she blessed the flat-faced one, so that her two eyes were visible.<-

->She brought forth a mute girl—it was one of Brigit’s wonders—<-
<margin>44</margin> ->hand did not go from hand, until her speech was clear.<-

->A wonder was the salted meat she blessed; it was the power of God that secured it;<-
->it remained a full month with the dog, yet the dog did not spoil it.<-

->Even greater was another wonder: a portion she requested from the pot,<-
<margin>48</margin> ->it did not lose the color of its glory; though boiling, it was cast into her lap.<-

---

41a *ni ruirmiu*: i.e., <gloss>I have not numbered</gloss>. <term>Stokes</term> <note>Whitley Stokes, a prominent 19th-century Celtic scholar.</note> therefore writes *ruairmiu*; in any case, the verbal form must be trisyllabic. *ni airmiu*: i.e., <gloss>I cannot find the count of every single one of her miracles.</gloss>

41b *andorigenai* Manuscript, compare verse 81.

42 *Cogitosus, chapter 14.* <note>Cogitosus was a 7th-century monk who wrote the "Vita Sanctae Brigidae," the earliest biography of the saint.</note>

42b *comdar*, but verse 44 *comtar* (representing *combtar* or *combatar*, meaning "so that they were").

43 44 *Cogitosus, chapter 15.*

43 *Stokes* now translates (*Remarks*² p. 70): “*A dumb girl was brought — it was one of her, Brigit’s miracles.*” However, “was brought” is usually *dobreth* (compare verses 59, 82 etc.). Could *amlabar do bert* perhaps correspond to the words <note>original Latin: "ex naturali procreatione muta"</note> "mute by natural birth" in *Cogitosus*? Compare *bert* (birth), *O'Davoren’s Glossary* and *coimpert* (conception), *Stokes Irish Glosses* 847. Regarding *Brigta*, compare verse 35.

43b *a amra*: <gloss>of Brigit’s miracles</gloss>.

44a *Stokes* translates: “*Her hand went not from her hand*,” without, however, changing *laim* (as in the book *Goidelica*¹) into *lám*. This will be necessary, as *laim* cannot be considered a dual form because of the verb *luid* (in the phrase "there are two nuns" in verse 105 the situation is different, compare *Grammatica Celtica*² 490).

44b *a labra* in *Stokes, Goidelica*¹; *a comlabra* in the Manuscript and *Stokes, Goidelica*². A word *comlabar* in the sense of “speech” has not yet been proven; it could only mean the opposite of *amlabar* (mute), and perhaps entered the text here through a thought like *ba comlabar* (she was speaking). The meter also supports *labra*. Compare <note>original Irish: "Sén a Crist mo labra"</note> “Bless, O Christ, my speech,” from the *Martyrology of Óengus, prologue 1*.

45 46 *Cogitosus, chapter 16.*

45b i.e., <gloss>she secured and protected the piece of meat.</gloss>

46b *no con millestar* Manuscript and *Stokes*, although he translates: “*The dog marred it not.*” More precisely: <gloss>without the dog spoiling it.</gloss>

47 48 *Cogitosus, chapter 17.* 47b *dind lucht*: i.e., <gloss>which was in the cauldron.</gloss>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit, following the previous page's discussion of her miracles involving the healing of the blind and mute. This section moves to miracles involving animals—specifically a leper's request for a cow and the supernatural guidance of oxen and chariots—while continuing the scholarly comparison between the Irish text and Cogitosus’s Latin Life of Brigit.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

->The leper who begged a request of her, it was well that she granted it to him,<-
->she blessed the best of the calves, she loved the best of the cows.<-

->She then drove her chariot northward to the Hill of Cobthach the Slender,<-
<margin>52</margin> ->the calf with the leper in the chariot, and the cow following the calf.<-

->The oxen—they approached them—it would have been well had anyone heard them—<-
->the darkness rose against them, yet in the morning they came home.<-

->She unhitched her horse at the end of its bridle-cloth, when they ran down the slope,<-
<margin>56</margin> ->the yoke was not lower on one side; the Son of God extended the royal hand.<-

---

49-52 *Cogitosus, chapter 18.* <note>Referencing the "Vita Sanctae Brigidae" by the 7th-century monk Cogitosus.</note>

49<sup>b</sup> *conid rualaid*: i.e., <gloss>so that she granted it.</gloss> <term>Stokes</term> <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909), a leading scholar of Celtic languages.</note> translates: “*it was good for him that she granted it*”, likewise <term>Ebel</term> in <term>Zeuss’s Grammatica Celtica</term> <note>Johann Kaspar Zeuss’s foundational 1853 work on Celtic grammar.</note> (Z.² 456): “*it was good for him that he granted it*” <note>original Latin: "fuit bonum ei quod id concessit"</note>. However, *dó* <note>to him</note> cannot possibly be connected with *maith* <note>good</note>.

50<sup>b</sup> i.e., <gloss>she loved the choice calf of the cows.</gloss>

51<sup>a</sup> *reraig*: i.e., <gloss>she ran</gloss>, i.e., <gloss>she let its run be to him, to Bri.</gloss> Stokes now translates (*Remarks*² p. 70): “*She afterwards directed her chariot.*”

52<sup>b</sup> *indiaid* <note>afterwards/behind</note> is trisyllabic.

53 54 *Cogitosus, chapter 19.*

53<sup>a</sup> *do da ascansat*: i.e., <gloss>they approached</gloss> (the same gloss as verse 31). Stokes translates erroneously: “*The oxen that had gone away from her — well for them, had any one turned them.*” Compare *ascnam* <gloss>approach, acquisition... to enter</gloss> (Z.² 868). The form *ro... cload*, however, certainly belongs as a 3rd person singular secondary present to *rachlóithe* in the gloss: “*as if you had heard it from God*” <note>original Latin: "am. bid o dia rachloithe acsi a Deo id audivissetis"</note> (Z.² 447). Therefore here: *if someone had heard them.*

54<sup>a</sup> *doub* <note>darkness</note> disyllabic? 54<sup>b</sup> *a tech*: i.e., <gloss>Kildare</gloss> <note>Kildare, or Cell Dara, was Brigit's primary monastery.</note>.

55 56 *Cogitosus, chapter 19.*

55<sup>a</sup> i.e., <gloss>the cloth that is under the neck of the horse.</gloss>

55<sup>b</sup> *do rertar*: i.e., <gloss>they ran.</gloss> *fo fán*: <term>Stokes</term> suggests *fáin* (without *fo*) in the first edition of *Goidelica* for the sake of the rhyme and the meter. I suspect: <gloss>when they ran down the slope.</gloss>

56<sup>b</sup> *ro réraig*: i.e., <gloss>he helped.</gloss> According to this, Stokes in the second edition of *Goidelica*: “*God’s son helped the royal hand*”; in *Remarks*² p. 70, he replaces “*helped*” with “*directed*.” However, compare: *rigid* i.e., <gloss>stretching</gloss>. *as it is: he stretched his hand for his property* <note>original Old Irish: "ut est ro rigid sam a lam dia cruid"</note> <term>O’Davoren’s Glossary</term> (ed. Stokes) p. 110. For the sake of the meter, Stokes deleted the particle *ro* in the first edition of *Goidelica*; the length mark in *réraig* should also be deleted. *in ríglaim*: i.e., <gloss>the hand of the King of Leinster.</gloss> The glossator must be referring to a different version of the miracle than that of Cogitosus.

<summary>This page analyzes verses 49-56 of Broccán’s Hymn, focusing on miracles where Brigit aids a leper and miraculously guides oxen and a chariot through darkness. The commentary debates the precise translation of Old Irish verbs related to movement and "stretching" the hand, noting discrepancies between the poem and Cogitosus’s Latin Life.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit, specifically detailing her miracles involving animals and the protection of outlaws. It maintains the structure of comparing the Old Irish verses with the 7th-century Latin "Life of Brigit" by Cogitosus, while referencing the scholarship of Whitley Stokes and Johann Kaspar Zeuss.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

->A wild boar frequented her herd; to the north she drove the deer;<-
->Brigit blessed them with her staff; they stayed among her pigs.<-

->A gift of a fat pig was brought to her across the Plain of Fea, it was a marvel;<-
<margin>60</margin> ->wild dogs pursued it until it reached the Heights of Gabair.<-

->She gave a wild fox for the sake of the poor tenant;<-
->it escaped toward the wood, though the crowds pursued it.<-

->She was manifest in her journeys; she was the unique mother of the great King’s son;<-
<margin>64</margin> ->she blessed the soaring bird so that she held it in her hand.<-

->Nine outlaws she blessed; they reddened their weapons in a pool of blood;<-
->the man against whom they cast their spears—though wounded—no body-wound was found on him.<-

->What she performed of miracles, there is no one who can recount them accurately;<-
<margin>68</margin> ->a wonder! She took the meal of Lugaid; the champion’s strength did not diminish.<-

---

57 58 *Cogitosus, chapter 21.* <note>Cogitosus was a 7th-century monk at Kildare whose Latin biography of Brigit serves as a primary source for these verses.</note>
57<sup>b</sup> *dosephain* in the manuscript: i.e., <gloss>she pursued.</gloss> *anos*: i.e., <gloss>the wild pig.</gloss>
Stokes <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909), a prominent scholar of the Irish language.</note> translates: “*northwards the beast drove it.*”
58<sup>a</sup> *fria* is monosyllabic, but 58<sup>b</sup> *lia* is disyllabic.
59 60 *Cogitosus, chapter 22.* 59<sup>a</sup> *dí*: i.e., <gloss>to Brigit.</gloss>
61 62 *Cogitosus, chapter 23.* 61<sup>a</sup> *do ráith*: <note>Compare verse 87.</note>
62<sup>a</sup> *conselai*: i.e., <gloss>it escaped</gloss> or <gloss>it sought.</gloss>
62<sup>b</sup> *dosefnatar* for *dos sefnatar*, <note>See the note on verse 57.</note> *intluaig* appears in the manuscript.
63<sup>b</sup> i.e., <gloss>Brigit was one of the mothers of Christ.</gloss> <note>An honorific title reflecting Brigit's status as "The Mary of the Gael."</note>
64 *Cogitosus, chapter 24.*
64<sup>a</sup> has one syllable too few.
65 66 *Cogitosus, chapter 25.*
65<sup>a</sup> has one syllable too many. Stokes wrote in *Goidelica*¹ *a minn*, which is certainly correct if *minn* is a neuter noun <note>referencing Zeuss's Grammatica Celtica, p. 226</note>; added to this is the gloss: <gloss>their weapons.</gloss>
65<sup>b</sup> *lind chró*: <note>Compare the term "crolindech" meaning "blood-streaming," found alongside "crechtach" (wounded) in O'Curry's "On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish," Vol. III, p. 452, regarding the "Fight of Ferdiad" from the Book of Leinster.</note>
66<sup>b</sup> i.e., <gloss>because it was not against a real man that they cast their wounds (spears), but against a stone pillar.</gloss>
67<sup>b</sup> *dorurme*: i.e., <gloss>let him perform its enumeration.</gloss>
68 *Cogitosus, chapter 26.* Regarding this verse, the gloss states: i.e., <gloss>she reduced his food, and yet the strength of Lugaid was no less,</gloss> i.e., <gloss>Lugaid was a champion who lived in Leinster...</gloss> Stokes translates: “*The champion, his strength did not lessen.*” However, *dígaib* is transitive, <note>as seen in verse 26.</note>

<summary>This page lists several of Saint Brigit's nature miracles, including taming a wild boar, holding a bird in her hand, and protecting a man from outlaws by turning their spears toward a stone. It also notes her miraculous interaction with the champion Lugaid, whose strength remained even after his food portions were reduced.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit. It focuses on several of her most famous miracles: the lifting of a heavy oak, the recovery of a silver treasure from a fish, the miracle of the weaving-beam used as firewood, and the miraculous division of a silver ingot. The author continues to compare the Old Irish text with Cogitosus’s Latin Life of Brigit and critiques the translations of Whitley Stokes.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

->The oak that the host did not raise in another time—of excellent fame—<-
->her son brought it to Brigit, to the place where her booth was built.<- <note>original: "irro chloth a both"</note>

->The silver treasure not to be hidden, despite the malice against the warrior’s wife,<-
<margin>72</margin> ->it was cast into the sea along the route, until it was found inside a fish.<-

->A wonder for her: the noble woman-tenant on the Plain of Coil<-
->burned the new weaving-beam in the fire while cooking the calf.<-

->Even more of a wonder was what the Saint performed:<-
<margin>76</margin> ->in the morning the weaving-beam was whole, though the mother-cow had consumed the calf.<-

->The silver treasure that the craftsman did not divide—it was a marvel for her—<-
->Brigit struck it with her hand, and then it broke into three.<-

---

<margin>69 70 Cogitosus, chapter 27.</margin> 70ᵃ Stokes <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909), a leading scholar of Old Irish.</note> translates: “*Her son brought to her for Brigte.*” However, with this translation, the verse has eight syllables <note>original: "dí ammac"</note>, and "Brigte" must be taken as an Accusative case, even though elsewhere in this hymn we only find the form "Brigit"; also, the Irish word *la* <note>meaning "with" or "by"</note> does not correspond to the English "for." I consider this verse to be corrupt <note>Meaning the text was likely damaged or altered by later scribes.</note>. Perhaps it should be written: *dobert dí ammac co airm Brigte irro chloth a both* (where "Brigte" would be understood like "Brigta" in verses 35 and 43), but I consider this suggestion itself to be quite uncertain. <margin>71 72 Cogitosus, chapter 28.</margin>

71ᵃ *nad chlethi*: that is, <gloss>not to be concealed.</gloss> Stokes translates parenthetically: “*not to be concealed.*” However, the word *nad* usually introduces relative or dependent clauses. 71ᵇ "despite malice" <note>original: "ar ulc"</note>; Stokes uses *Goidelica²*, while the manuscript reads *ar ul*. Compare: *not for malice against people* from the *Martyrology of Oengus*, Epilogue 105. "The warrior’s" <note>original: "ind niad"</note>: that is, <gloss>"Nia" is the proper name of a certain poet.</gloss> However, the article proves that what is meant here is "nia" in the sense of a warrior or champion <note>Old Irish: "trénfer"</note>; compare the Latin *vir saecularis et gente nobilis* <note>“a secular man of noble birth”</note> in Cogitosus. Stokes emphasizes that "niad" and likewise "iach" <note>“of a fish”</note> in verse 72 are disyllabic. 72ᵃ has one syllable too few. Perhaps the word *les* <note>“by him”</note> should be added; compare verse 79ᵃ.

<margin>73-76 Cogitosus, chapter 29.</margin>
73ᵃ *ardoutacht*: that is, <gloss>she refreshed.</gloss> Stokes now translates (*Remarks²* page 70): “*who refreshed her*”; in *Goidelica²* he translated: “*who dwelt*”.
74ᵇ compare <note>original: "ic funi mairt don methil"</note> *while cooking a beef for the reaping-party* in the *Leabhar Breac*, page 33, line 8.
75 That is, <gloss>it was even more of a miracle to perform this there, still.</gloss>
76ᵇ *lia* <note>“with her”</note> is disyllabic, as is *fria* <note>“against her”</note> in verse 78. Manuscript reads *lóig*; compare verse 2.

<margin>77-80 Cogitosus, chapter 30.</margin> To this, the marginal note says: <margin>Three brothers, whose father left them a silver ingot, and the craftsmen of Ireland were unable to divide it into three for them, until Brigit broke it with her hand in Kildare.</margin>
77ᵇ "it was a marvel for her" <note>original: "ro bo amru dí"</note>. Stokes translates: “*it was a marvel for her*”, as if the word *amra* were there. It should probably be corrected this way, compare "a wonder for her" <note>original: "amra dí"</note> in verse 73.

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of Broccán’s Hymn to Saint Brigit. It focuses on the miracle of the silver ingot divided into three equal parts, the blessing of garments for Bishop Condlaed, and the miraculous replenishment of a vat of mead. The author continues to compare the Old Irish text with the Latin "Life of Brigit" by Cogitosus and critiques the translations of Whitley Stokes.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn.

->It was placed before the craftsman; a wonder was found thereafter:<-
<margin>80</margin> ->not even a single scruple was found to be more than one third of the others.<- <note>original: "ni furecht cid óen screpul ba mo tríun arailiu"</note>

->What she performed of miracles, there is no man who can tell them;<-
->she blessed the garment for Condlaed, when it was brought to Rome.<- <margin>82</margin> <note>original: "do Letha" - the gloss identifies Letha as Rome.</note>

->When there was danger for her, her Son <gloss>Christ guiding her</gloss> before her did not fail her:<-
<margin>84</margin> ->he brought a garment <gloss>he brought</gloss> in a sealskin chest <gloss>the clothing was in a chest of sealskin</gloss> in a chariot by his grace.<-

->The drinking of mead that was brought to her, there was no shortage <gloss>it was not deep</gloss> in every vessel <gloss>to the one who brought the vat to Brigit</gloss>,<-
->until it was found at the side of the house; it was not discovered there until then.<- <margin>86</margin>

---

**79ᵇ** *fofrith* <note>it was found</note>: read as *co frith*.

**80** Stokes <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909), a prominent Irish philologist.</note> translates:
> “It was not found that even one scruple (one third) was greater than another third.”

However, since the letter *a* in the manuscript is not infrequently written exactly like *u* (see also the note to verse 77ᵇ), then certainly *trían* <note>meaning "a third part," here two syllables</note> should be read instead of *tríun*. Ebel translates (*Grammatica Celtica*² 478):
> “It was not found that even by one scruple was one third larger than the other”;

but *cid oen screpul* <note>even one scruple</note> is the subject of *furecht* <note>was found</note>; the Latin "ablative of measure" is expressed differently in Irish, see *Grammatica Celtica*² 917.

**81–84** *Cogitosus, chapter 31.* <note>Referring to the "Life of Saint Brigid" written by the monk Cogitosus in the 7th century.</note>

**81ᵇ** has one syllable too many. Following Stokes (*Goidelica*¹), the word *do* should be deleted; compare *dagéna* (he will do it) and *dagnítis* (they used to do it) in *Grammatica Celtica*² 331.

**82ᵃ** Stokes translates:
> “She blessed raiment for Condla.”

This does not agree with the narrative of *Cogitosus*.

**82ᵇ** *dobreth* <note>was brought</note>: that is, "he used to go." *do Letha*: that is, "to Rome."

**83ᵇ** that is, "Christ guiding her journey."

**84ᵃ** has one syllable too few. *dobert*: that is, "he brought." Nevertheless, Stokes now translates (*Remarks*² p. 70):
> “There was brought.”

It should be written *dobert dí díllait* <note>he brought a garment to her</note>. *i. criol roncind*: that is, the clothing was in a chest made of sealskin.

**85–88** *Cogitosus, chapter 32.*

**85ᵃ** Compare verse 59.

**85ᵇ** *ni bu ances*: that is, "it was not deep." *cachthucai*: that is, "to the one who brought the vat to Brigit."

**85–86** Stokes translates:
> “The vat of mead that was brought to her, there was no hardship to every one who brought: (the vessel) was found beside (his) house: it was not observed there with her.”

The glossator explains *ances* as *domain* (deep); Stokes (*Cormac's Glossary Translation* p. 14) cites *ainces* with the meaning “doubt,” but here he translates it as “hardship” (it likely has this meaning in the *Leabhar Breac* p. 64ᵇ line 64: "he healed every disease and every hardship that was in the land"). The form *túcai*, however, cannot belong to *tuic* (he places), but only to *daucci* or *tuicci* (he understands) (*Grammatica Celtica*² 431), and specifically likely as a reduplicated perfect (compare *accai*, "he saw").

<summary>This page analyzes verses 79-86 of Broccán's Hymn, focusing on the miraculous division of silver and the story of Bishop Condlaed's garments. The author debates linguistic interpretations of Old Irish terms with Whitley Stokes, specifically concerning the miracle of the mead vat.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Brigit, Broccán's Hymn, Old Irish philology, Whitley Stokes, Cogitosus, Bishop Condlaed, hagiography, miracle of the mead, silver division</keywords>

# I 5 Broccán's Hymn. 37

She paid the tax <note>original: "do raith"</note> to her tenant <gloss>that is: her man of the household</gloss> when he had need of it,
<margin>88</margin> yet no excess was found there, nor did a drop go missing from it.

Upon the prayer of Brigit, may she be with us against danger until she helps us <note>original: "co n-don fair"</note>,
may her weak ones <note>meaning the poor or her followers; original: "lobran"</note> be at our side before going into the presence of the Holy Spirit.

May she come to us with a sword of fire for the battle against the dark hosts <note>original: "íalla ciara," referring to demons</note>,
<margin>92</margin> may her holy prayer protect us in the kingdom of heaven, away from pains!

Before going with angels to the battle, let us run quickly to the church,
commemoration of the Lord <note>original: "fiadat"</note> is better than any poem <gloss>that is: better than all poetry is the poetry made for God</gloss>! Victorious Brigit did not love the world.

->Brigit did not love.<-

I entreat the patron Saint Brigit with the saints of Kildare <note>original: "Cille dara"</note>,
<margin>96</margin> may they be between me and pain, so my soul does not go to waste.

The nun who rode the Curragh <note>The Curragh is a famous plain in County Kildare</note> was a shield against sharp edges <gloss>that is: against the edge of a weapon</gloss>,
no equal <note>original: "asset"</note> was found <gloss>that is: I have not found</gloss> except Mary; we trust in my Bríge <note>A diminutive or affectionate form of Brigit; also interpreted as "my strength"</note>.

---

**87a** *a hathig*: that is: her man of the household <note>a tenant or follower</note>.

**87b** Stokes wrote in *Goidelica* (1st edition) *less* and accordingly 

**88b** *ess*, since *leass* is not Old Irish orthography. *banne* in *Goidelica* (2nd edition).

**89a** That is: may her prayer be upon us; compare Verse 104.

**89b** *sí*: that is: Brigit. Stokes in *Goidelica* (2nd edition, p. 184) unnecessarily conjectures *sith* <note>meaning "long"</note> for this and translates: “long against dangers may she aid us”?
*condonfair*: that is: may she help us...? For the sake of the rhyme, Stokes wrote *fóir* in the first edition of *Goidelica*.

**90a** That is: may the weak ones and the wretched be on our side, praying for us. Stokes translates: “May they be on her weakling's side...” However, a preposition belonging to *leith* <note>side</note> could not have been combined with a possessive pronoun belonging to *lobran* <note>weaklings</note>; *leith* is a locative case, as in the poem VI 1, 9: "on the south side of Gabair glass."

**90b** has one syllable too many, therefore Stokes in the first edition of *Goidelica* struck the article before *spirta* <note>Spirit</note>. *nóeb* <note>holy</note> in the Manuscript. Compare the genitive *lóig* in Verses 52 and 74. On the other hand, *nóeb* occurs in 22 (restored in Verse 2), 41, and 92; *óen* <note>one</note> in 43, 63, and 18; *oeged* <note>guest</note> in 26; *coercha* <note>sheep</note> in 33; *i toeb* <note>at the side</note> in 86.

**91a** That is: may she come to our rescue. Stokes now translates (*Remarks*, 2nd edition, p. 70): “May she help us.” According to that, as in Verse 89, *fóir* would need to be corrected (*fair* in *Goidelica*, 2nd edition).

**92b** That is: against demons, that is: the black swarms of demons. **92a** That is: may she come to our deliverance.

**94a** That is: better than all poetry is the poetry made for God.

**97b** That is: against... the stitching of the edges. In the text as well, *foebra* should be written; see the note on Verse 90b. Whether *fégi* <note>sharp</note> is the correct spelling here might be doubted because of *brígi*.

**98a** That is: I have not found. **98b** *admunemar*: that is: we bless or we beseech.
*mo brígi*: that is: my Brigit. Stokes translates: “we put trust in my Bríge.”

# I 5 Broccán's Hymn. 38

We entrust ourselves to my Bríge <note>A term of endearment for Brigit</note>, may she be a protection for our company,
<margin>100</margin> so that her patronage <note>original: "hérlam"</note> works with me, and we may all achieve salvation!

Praising Christ is a glorious speech, worshiping the Son of God is a gift of victory <note>original: "dán búada"</note>!
May everyone who sang it, everyone who heard it, belong to the kingdom of God without denial!

Everyone who heard it, everyone who sang it, may the blessing of Brigit be upon him!
<margin>104</margin> May the blessing of Brigit and of God be upon us together!

There are two nuns in the kingdom of heaven <note>original: "irriched"</note> whom I strive for with my utmost effort:
Mary and Saint Brigit; may we be under the protection of them both!

->Saint Brigit, most sacred virgin,<-
->in Christ the Lord she was most faithful. Amen.<-

---

**99^b** *company* <note>original: "cure"</note> in the Manuscript; *cuiri* according to Stokes in *Goidelica*.
**100^a** *may she work with*: <gloss>that is: may she help.</gloss> Stokes translates: "May her patronage work with me."
**100^b** *all* <note>original: "huile"</note> in the Manuscript; *huili* according to Stokes in *Goidelica*. The fluctuation between *i* and *e* at the end of words in these hymns is merely due to the scribe's spelling habits.
**101^b** *gift of victory*: "a gift of victory" according to Stokes. However, the word *dán* <term>usually meaning "gift" or "talent"</term> should likely be understood here in the sense of the Latin *ars* <term>meaning a "spiritual craft" or "skill"</term>.
**102^a** *let it be of the kingdom*: Stokes reads *rop flatha* in *Goidelica*. Regarding the use of the genitive case with the verb "to be," see the *Grammatica Celtica* page 916.
**103^a** *sang*: for the sake of the rhyme with *him* <note>original: "fair"</note> in line 103b, it should probably be written as *gaib*.
**104^b** *be upon us*: see verse 89a.
**105^a** *kingdom of heaven*: <gloss>that is, the King's land; that is, in the territory of the heavenly King.</gloss>
**105^b** *whom I strive for*: "whom I rely on (?)" according to Stokes.
**106^b** See Hymns 1, 2 and 6, 24. This verse contains one syllable too many.

The accompanying notes provide a scholarly look at the poem's linguistic structure and corrections to earlier translations by Whitley Stokes.</summary>

# Appendix
### to
## Broccán’s Hymn to Brigit.

---

*The numbers before the Latin passages indicate the chapters in the "Life of Brigit" by Cogitosus (Acts of the Saints, Feb. 1). The Irish passages are taken from the "Life" found in the Lebar Brecc <note>The 'Speckled Book,' a major 15th-century Irish ecclesiastical manuscript</note>.*

**Regarding Verse 13:**

*The following legend about the "wonder of Plea" <note>original: "amra plea"</note> is found in the Lebar Brecc, page 82, where the empty space beneath the text of the Félire <note>The "Martyrology of Oengus," a calendar of saints</note> is filled by a longer fragment from another "Life." That it belongs to a different "Life" than the one preserved on page 61b of the Lebar Brecc is proven by the details shared regarding verses 27 and 28.*

"The wonder of Plea": that is, a city on the shore of the Tyrrhenian Sea; or Plea is a city belonging to Brigit on the shore of an inlet of the sea, and it is her ritual <note>original: "ord," referring to the order of service or liturgy</note> that is followed by Brigit’s community. And it happened thus: Brigit sent seven men from her to learn the ritual of Peter and Paul, because God did not intend for her to go herself. They did not bring back the ritual, so she sent them a third time to the East, and her blind boy went with them, for everything he heard, he remembered. When they reached the Sea of Wight <note>original: "muir n-ichtt," the English Channel</note>, a great storm came upon them, so they lowered their anchor, and it got caught on the steeple of the oratory <note>A small chapel or place of prayer</note>. They cast lots among themselves to see who should go down, and it fell to the boy to go down. He released the anchor and stayed there for a whole year learning the ritual, until the other group returned from Rome to him. A storm came upon them again in the same place, and they lowered the anchor; the blind boy came up with them, bringing the ritual of Plea and a shining bell, and it is that which survives today: the ritual of Plea.

**Regarding Verses 25, 26:**

4. From then on, when the time for churning <note>original: "tempus matutum," literally 'morning time' but here referring to the dairy cycle</note> came, she was sent by her mother to the work of the curds—to collect butter from the stirred milk of the cows—so that, just as other women were accustomed to do this work, she might also do it in equal measure. She was expected to return the yield of the cows and the usual measured weight of butter most fully for use at the appointed time, along with the others. But this virgin, most beautiful in her character and hospitable, wishing to obey God more than men, distributed the milk and butter generously to the poor and to guests. And when the proper time arrived according to custom for everyone to return the yield of the cows, they came to her. And as her fellow workers were showing their completed tasks, the aforementioned blessed virgin was asked to likewise assign her own work. She, trembling with fear of her mother since she had nothing to show (because she had given it all to the poor), not worrying about the next day and kindled by the unquenchable flame of faith, turned firmly to the Lord and prayed. Without delay:

<vocab>Broccán’s Hymn, Brigit, Cogitosus, Lebar Brecc, Martyrology, Old Irish, Latin, wonder of Plea, Rome, Saint Brigid</vocab>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the philological and hagiographical notes related to Broccán's Hymn. It transitions from the discussion of the "wonder of Plea" on page 39 to a specific narrative account of St. Brigit's miracles involving butter and the liberation of her mother from slavery. The text combines Latin excerpts from Cogitosus's "Life of Brigit" with a longer Irish narrative from the "Lebar Brecc" manuscript.</meta>

<page-num>40</page-num>
<header>I 5 Broccan's Hymnus.</header>

The Lord, hearing the voice and the prayers of the virgin, by the bounty of the divine gift—just as He is a helper in times of necessity—was present, and for His virgin who trusted in Him, He restored the butter in abundance. In a wonderful manner in that hour, after prayer, the most holy virgin showed that nothing of her work was missing; rather, abounding above all her fellow workers, she showed herself to have fulfilled her duty... <note>original: "Dominus vocem virginis audiens..." This Latin passage is from Chapter 1 of the "Life of Brigit" by Cogitosus.</note>

Lebar Brecc <note>The 'Speckled Book'</note> p. 63a, 10: Once it came into Brigit's mind, through the grace of the Holy Spirit, to go and seek news of her mother who was in bondage <note>original: "i n-daire," referring to slavery or servitude</note>. She asked her father’s permission, and he did not give it to her. Nevertheless, she went without permission from Dubthach <note>Brigit's father</note>. It was a relief to her mother when she arrived; the mother was weary and diseased, and [Brigit] took her mother's place and began to care for the dairy herd.

Now, the first churning that Brigit did, she divided its product into twelve portions in honor of the twelve apostles of the Creator, and she set a thirteenth portion <note>original: "in tres cuibrend X," meaning the 10th plus 3rd</note> so that it was larger than any other portion, in honor of Jesus Christ; and she then gave it all to the poor of the Lord. The druid's herdsman, however, marveled at the arrangement Brigit made for the butter. Then Brigit said: "Christ with His twelve apostles <note>original: "díb apstolu X"</note> preached to the people of the world; it is in His name that I feed the poor, for Christ is in the person of every faithful poor person."

The charioteer (that is, the herdsman) went to the druid's house, and the druid and his wife asked him: "Does the girl care well for the dairy herd?" as the charioteer (that is, the herdsman) said: "I am satisfied, however it is, and the calves are fat," for he did not dare to slander Brigit in her absence. The charioteer brought a hamper <note>original: "rusc," a basket or vessel often made of bark</note> with him, eight hands <note>original: "dúird," a measurement of height</note> in height. The charioteer said to Brigit: "The druid and his wife will come to fill this hamper with the butter of the gathering" (that is, the dairy). "They are welcome," said Brigit.

The druid and his spouse came to the dairy and saw the fat calves. Brigit welcomed them and gave them food. Then the druid's wife said to Brigit: "We have come to see if the work assigned to you has been done. What butter have you?" She had nothing ready but the yield of one churning and a half, and she gave that half first. At that, the druid's wife laughed and said: "It is a good thing to fill a large hamper for me with this amount of butter!" "Fill your hamper," said Brigit, "and God will provide butter for it." She kept going into her kitchen <note>original: "culid," a store-room or pantry</note> and brought half the yield of each visit out of it, for God did not wish her honor <note>original: "enech," literally 'face' or 'reputation'</note> to be taken away, until the hamper was filled in that way. And this is what she used to recite as she went into her kitchen:

> "O God, O my Great King | who has power over all these things | bless, O God, a cry without prohibition <note>original: "nuall cen geis," a prayer or shout not forbidden by ritual taboos</note> | with your right hand this kitchen | May the son of Mary my friend come | to bless my kitchen | the Prince of the world to its edge | may we have abundance with Him."

The druid and his spouse honored the Lord through the miracle they saw, and then the druid said to Brigit: "The butter and the cows you have milked, I offer them to you; you shall not be in bondage to me, but serve the Lord." Brigit answered him and said: "Take the cows and give me the freedom of my mother." The druid said: "Not only shall your mother be freed, but the cows shall be given to you, and whatever you say, I will do." Brigit distributed the cows to the poor and to the needy of God.

<summary>This page recounts the miracle of the butter, where Brigit miraculously fills a large hamper despite having little at hand, leading to her mother's release from slavery under a druid. It includes a specific prayer in Irish attributed to Brigit for the blessing of her store-room.</summary>
<keywords>St. Brigit, butter miracle, Lebar Brecc, Dubthach, druid, slavery, Mary's Son, dairy herd, charity, apostles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Broccán's Hymn, a Middle Irish poem celebrating the life of Saint Brigit. The previous page concluded the story of Brigit's mother being freed from slavery by a druid. This page details specific miracles associated with verses 27 through 32 of the hymn, focusing on the multiplication of food (specifically bacon) and Brigit's power over the weather during harvest time.</meta>

The druid was baptized and became a believer, and he accompanied Brigit from then on.

### Regarding Verses 27, 28:

6. . . . For when she once had cooked bacon <note>original Latin: "lardum"</note> in a cauldron for arriving guests, she mercifully gave it to a fawning and begging dog; and when the bacon was drawn from the cauldron and afterwards divided for the guests, it was found to be completely full, as if nothing had been taken away . . . <note>This Latin passage is an excerpt from the "Life of Brigit" by Cogitosus, an 8th-century monk.</note>

*Lebar Brecc <note>The 'Speckled Book,' a major medieval Irish manuscript</note> p. 62<sup>b</sup>, 55:* Guests went to Dubthach <note>Brigit's father</note>; Dubthach divided a side of bacon into five portions and left them with Brigit to boil. A hungry, ravenous dog came into the house. Brigit gave him the fifth portion out of mercy. When the dog had eaten that portion, Brigit gave him another portion. Dubthach came afterwards and said to Brigit: "Did you boil the bacon, and do all the portions remain?" "Count them," said Brigit. Dubthach counted them and nothing was missing from them. The guests told Dubthach what Brigit had done. "Many," said Dubthach, "are the miracles of that maiden." The guests then did not eat the food, because they were unworthy <note>original: "esindraicce," meaning they felt they did not deserve to eat food produced by a miracle</note>, but it was distributed to the poor and to the needy of the Lord.

Similarly, in the fragment concerning Brigit in the *Lebar Brecc*, page 82 (see page 39): A robber <note>original: "Foglaid," which can also mean a plunderer</note> came to Dubthach and brought a side of bacon in the cauldron for him and made five pieces of it. He entrusted Brigit with its care; then a wretched dog came to her, and she gave the five pieces from the cauldron [to the dog] in the presence of the young man, and yet the five pieces were afterwards found in the cauldron. This was told to Dubthach, and Dubthach gave her land, namely, the site of an oratory <note>original: "rethet daurthige," a plot of land for a small wooden church</note> in the territory of the Two Plains.

### Regarding Verses 29, 30:

7. And she called together reapers and workers for her harvest: and when that assembly of reapers had been made, that day of the assembly happened to be cloudy and rainy. While rains poured down abundantly from the clouds throughout that whole province in the surrounding area, and streams of droplets ran through the valleys and cracks in the earth, her own harvest alone stood dry without the hindrance and disturbance of the rains. And though all the reapers of that entire region were prevented by the rainy day, her own [workers], without any shadow of darkness or rain, performed the work of the harvest for that whole day from the rising to the setting of the sun through the power of God.

*Compare Lebar Brecc page 65<sup>b</sup>, line 57:* It was one of Brigit's miracles: she had a large band of reapers <note>original: "methél," a traditional Irish communal work party</note> harvesting; rain poured in the plain of Liffey, but not a drop fell in her field through Brigit's prayer.

### Regarding Verses 31, 32:

8. Behold, among her other virtues, this work seems worthy of admiration. For when Bishops arrived and were staying with her as guests, since she had nothing with which to feed them, she was helped by the manifold power of God in her usual manner, and as her necessity required, she had one cow...
