<meta>This page continues the commentary on Broccán's Hymn, specifically documenting the miracles of Saint Brigit of Kildare. It follows a discussion of Brigit’s hospitality and a miracle involving bacon. This page transitions into miracles concerning the multiplication of milk, her power over physical elements (hanging a cloak on a sunbeam), and the restoration of stolen livestock.</meta>

...and she milked the same cow three times in one single day, contrary to the usual custom: and that which is normally obtained from the three best cows, she herself, by a miraculous outcome, obtained from her one cow. <note>original Latin: "eandemque tribus contra consuetudinem..." This concludes a miracle story found in early Latin Lives of Brigit.</note>

> Cf. *Lebar Brecc <note>The 'Speckled Book,' a 15th-century Irish manuscript</note> p. 65b, line 41*: Once seven bishops came to Brigit and she had nothing she could give to them. After the milking of the cows three times, the cows were milked again a third time, and it was more plentiful than any other milking. <note>original Irish: "Fecht ann tancatar VII n-epscoip..."</note>

Similarly, in the fragment on page 82: Eighteen bishops came to Brigit from Uí Briúin Chualann <note>a tribal territory in the area of modern south Dublin and north Wicklow</note> from the "Hill of the Bishops" to the Lake of New Milk <note>Loch Lemnachta</note> beside Kildare to the north. Brigit asked her cook, namely Blathnait, if she had food, and she said no. *And Brigit was ashamed, so an angel told her to milk the cows again; then Brigit milked them so that they filled the vats, and they would have filled all the vessels of the people of Leinster; and the milk went over the vessels until it made a lake of it—hence it is called Loch Lemnachta <note>Lake of New Milk</note>.*

### Regarding Verses 33, 34:

> 9. . . . For while she was tending her sheep as part of her pastoral work, in a flat and grassy place, she was soaked by an excessive downpour of rain. She returned home with wet clothes; and when a sunbeam <note>literally "solar shadow," original Latin: "umbra solaris"</note> entered through the openings of the house, she—with her eyesight dimmed <note>implying she was dazzled or perhaps simply exhausted</note>—thought it was a wooden beam <note>literally "tree," original Latin: "arborem"</note> fixed across the room, and she placed her rain-soaked garment upon it. And as if on a great and firm beam, the garment hung suspended upon that thin ray of sunlight.

### Regarding Verses 35, 36:

> 10. . . . For when Saint Brigit was carefully tending the flock of sheep in the field, a certain wicked youth came, craftily stealing and testing her generosity toward the poor. Changing his appearance each time, he came to her seven times, and carried away seven wethers <note>castrated rams</note> from her in one day and hid them in a secret place. And when the flock was to be driven to the folds in the evening as usual, it was counted most diligently two or three times, and by a wonder the whole number was found complete without any loss. And those who had been involved, marveling at the power of God manifested through the virgin, returned the seven wethers they had hidden to her flock. And the number of the flock was neither more nor less, but was found to be complete as before.

> Cf. *Lebar Brecc page 66a, line 17*: When Brigit was herding sheep, a thief came to her and stole seven wethers from her after first entreating her. Nevertheless, when the flock was numbered, the wethers were found again through the prayers of Brigit.

### Regarding Verse 38:

> 11. Also by a miraculous outcome, when lepers were demanding ale from the venerable Brigit and she had none, seeing the water prepared for the baths and blessing it with the power of faith, she changed it into the best ale and poured it out abundantly for the thirsty . . .

<meta>This page continues the commentary on the miracles of Saint Brigit of Kildare, as recorded in various hagiographical sources and linked to the verses of Broccán’s Hymn. The previous page discussed her power over nature and the multiplication of milk. This page shifts focus to her powers of healing, restoration, and the miraculous transformation of physical objects.</meta>

### Regarding Verse 39:

12. For with a most powerful and ineffable strength of faith, she faithfully blessed a certain woman who, after a vow of integrity <note>a vow of chastity or virginity</note>, had fallen through human frailty into the youthful desire of pleasure and already had a pregnant and swelling womb. By the vanishing of the conception in the womb, she restored the woman to health and to penance without childbirth and without pain . . . <note>original Latin: "Potentissima enim et ineffabili fidei fortitudine quandam feminam... sine partu et sine dolore eam sanam ad poenitentiam restituit." This reflects a medieval hagiographic tradition where the saint "undoes" a pregnancy to allow a woman to return to her religious life without the public shame of the era.</note>

### Regarding Verse 40:

13. For on a certain day, when someone came to her seeking salt—just as countless other poor and needy people were accustomed to come for their necessities—the most blessed Brigit herself, in that hour, generously gave an abundance for the seeker's needs: salt made from a stone which she had blessed . . . <note>original Latin: "salem factum de lapide quem benedixit."</note>

*Cf. Leabhar Breac <note>The 'Speckled Book,' a renowned 15th-century Irish ecclesiastical manuscript</note> page 65b, line 45:* Once, a certain nun of Brigit's community had a desire for salt. Brigit performed a prayer, so that the stone which was before her turned into salt, and the nun was healed <note>satisfied or cured</note> thereafter. <note>original Irish: "Fect ann din ro gab mian araile caillech dó muntir Brigte imm salond..."</note>

### Regarding Verse 42:

14. . . . For following the example of the Lord, she also opened the eyes of one born blind . . . For at times, him whom natural birth brought forth as a blind child, the faith of this same Brigit—compared to and like unto a grain of mustard seed—opened his simple and bright eyes by a great miracle . . . <note>original Latin: "Nam secundum exemplum Domini et haec oculos caeci nati aperuit..."</note>

*Cf. Leabhar Breac p. 65b, line 60:* It was one of Brigit’s miracles that she blessed the "flat-faced" man <note>original Irish: "clarenech," a term often used in Old Irish for a person born blind whose eyes appeared closed or flat</note> so that his two eyes were healthy.

### Regarding Verses 43, 44:

15. And one day, when a woman from outside her community came to visit her with her twelve-year-old daughter, who was mute from her natural birth, she bowed with worthy reverence as all were accustomed to do, and with a humble neck proceeded to Brigit's peaceful kiss. Then Brigit, affable to all and happy with her speech seasoned with divine salt <note>a metaphor for wisdom</note>, spoke healthfully to her. Following the Savior's example of bidding the little ones to come to Him, she held the girl's hand in her own. Not knowing that the girl was mute, Brigit asked what her will was—whether she wished to remain a virgin with a veiled head, or if she wished to be given in marriage. When the mother advised that her daughter could give no answer, Brigit replied to the mother, saying she would not let go of the daughter’s hand until she first gave her an answer. And when she questioned the daughter a second time about the same matter, the daughter replied to her, saying: "I wish to do nothing other than what you wish." And so afterward, with her mouth opened, without any impediment of the tongue and with her bond loosened, she spoke healthily. <note>original Latin: "Et cum filiam secunda vice interrogaret... non aliud nisi quod tu volueris, agere volo."</note>

### Regarding Verses 45, 46:

16. . . . For when her soul was intent on the meditation of heavenly things, as she was always accustomed, raising her conversation from earthly things to heavenly things— <note>The text breaks off here as Brigit enters a state of deep spiritual contemplation.</note>

<meta>This page continues the medieval commentary on the miracles of Saint Brigit of Kildare, keyed to the verses of the 8th-century "Hymn of Broccán." The previous page described her miraculous production of salt from stone and her compassion for a pregnant woman. This page moves into a series of nature miracles involving animals (dogs, cows, and oxen) and the protection of her property from thieves.</meta>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn

...she left a not small, but rather large portion of bacon <note>original Latin: "lardi"</note> with a dog. When it was looked for a month later, it was found untouched and whole in the very spot where the dog usually stayed. For the dog did not dare to eat what was entrusted to it by the blessed virgin; instead, it stood as a patient and capable guardian of the meat, restrained and tamed by Divine power contrary to its natural instinct.

### Regarding Verses 47, 48:

17. . . For when a certain poor man in need of food asked her for help, she hurried to those who were cooking meat so that she might bring something from them to the poor man. But one of those servants, a most dull-witted man who had cooked the meat, foolishly threw a piece of meat that was not yet cooked into the white fold of her garment <note>the "receptaculum" or pocket formed by her robe</note>. She carried it and gave it to the poor man, and the cloth was not stained, but remained in its bright white color.

### Regarding Verses 49—52:

18. . . Because the poor and pilgrims were flocking to her from all sides, drawn by the great fame of her virtues and her extreme generosity, a certain ungrateful leper approached among them. He demanded that the best cow from the herd, along with the best calf of all the calves, be given to him at the same time. Hearing his prayers, she did not delay, but soon willingly gave the sick man the cow she knew to be the best of all, as well as an elegant and excellent calf belonging to another cow. In her mercy, she sent her own chariot with him for the long journey across a very wide plain, so that the sick man—exhausted by the long trek—would not suffer the trouble of driving the cow. She ordered the calf to be placed behind his back in the chariot. And so the cow, licking the calf with her tongue and loving it as if it were her own, followed them to the destined place without anyone forcing her . . .

### Regarding Verses 53, 54:

19. And after a certain interval of time, other most wicked thieves who feared neither God nor men came from another province to commit robbery. Crossing a great river where the passage was easy on foot, they stole her oxen. But as they were returning by the same way, the force of a great river suddenly flooded and threw them into confusion. The river, rising up like a wall, did not permit the most wicked theft of Blessed Brigit’s oxen to pass; instead, it submerged the thieves and swept them away. The oxen, freed from their hands with the leather straps still hanging from their horns, returned to their own herd and stall <term>boekium: a Latinized form of an Irish word for a cow-house or bypass</term>.

---

\* Verse 50, however, only becomes understandable if one takes into account another detail found in the *Life of Saint Brigit by an anonymous author, chapter VII, 49 (cited above, page 124):* "Then they chose the best calf, and letting it go, the best of the cows ran to it with a great lowing; and they loved each other so much that almost no one could separate them."

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn. 45

*Compare the Speckled Book <note>original: "Leb. Br." or Leabhar Breac, a famous 15th-century Irish manuscript</note> page 65b, line 59: It was among the miracles of Brigid that when thieves stole her oxen, the River Liffey rose up against them; the oxen returned home the next day with the thieves' clothes upon their horns.*

### Regarding Verses 55, 56:

20. When on a certain day the most holy Brigid herself, compelled by some necessary business, was visiting a gathering of the people, she was traveling in a chariot drawn by two horses. And while she was in her vehicle, leading a heavenly life on earth through divine meditation <note>original: "meditatione theotica," likely referring to "theoticos" or godly contemplation</note> and praying to her Lord as was her custom, one horse—leaping with a brute spirit—fell from a high place. Unrestrained by the reins and violently wrenching itself away, it freed itself from the yoke. While the other horse remained alone under its yoke, the terrified horse ran through the fields. And so the Divine hand, supporting the hanging yoke without any fall, and with the crowd watching as a testimony to Divine power, she—praying securely in her vehicle—arrived unharmed at the assembly of the people with a gentle pace, despite having only one horse positioned under the chariot...

### Regarding Verses 57, 58:

21. ...When a wild boar, solitary and woodland-dwelling, was frightened and fleeing, it came with headlong speed to the herd of pigs belonging to the most blessed Brigid. When she saw him coming among her own swine, she blessed him. Thereafter, unafraid, as if he were a domestic animal, he remained with her herd of pigs...

### Regarding Verses 59, 60:

22. For once, when someone from a distant province among others offering gifts to her offered some fat pigs, he asked that others be sent with him to his estate, which was located a long distance away, so that she might receive the pigs from him. Since the journey was a long stretch of three or four days or more, she sent her companions with him. After one day's journey, on a mountain at the border of the regions which is called by its proper name Gabor <note>likely Gabrán or the Wicklow mountains</note>, they observed her pigs—which they thought were in distant regions—coming toward them, driven and herded along the road by wolves. And when they were closer, and he understood they were his own, recognizing the specific pigs and seeing the wild wolves—who, out of the greatest reverence for Blessed Brigid, were working as fit shepherds from the great forests and the wide plain of the Liffey <note>original: "campo Liffi"</note> in gathering and driving the pigs—the wolves left them unharmed as the messengers arrived. Having understood this wonderful event, they departed in their usual manner. And so on the second day, those who had been sent returned home with the pigs, recounting the miraculous deed.

### Regarding Verses 61, 62:

23. ...For on a certain day, when someone supported by no knowledge <note>i.e., an ignorant person</note> saw a fox walking through the King's palace, thinking with his blinded senses that it was a wild beast, and because it was familiar in the King's hall and...

These stories emphasize Brigid's authority over nature and the animal kingdom.</summary>

# I 5 Broccán’s Hymn. 46

...that it might be gentle ... she did not know, ... he killed it ... And the king, being angry upon learning what had happened, ordered the man to be killed unless a fox similar in all its cleverness to the one his own fox used to perform were restored to him ... And when the holy and venerable Brigid learned of the matter ... ordering her chariot to be harnessed ... she set out on the way that led to the king's palace. Without delay: the Lord, hearing her as she poured forth her constant prayers, sent one of His wild foxes to come to her; which, coming with a very swift run across the fields and approaching the most blessed Brigid's chariot, lightly leaping up, entered the chariot and, placing itself under the shelter of Brigid's garment, sat quietly with her in the vehicle ... And when the king would not agree to her prayers ... she herself brought forward her fox into the midst, which in the presence of the king and the entire multitude, acting out all the behaviors and teachable cleverness of the other fox in the same form as the first, openly played with various tricks before everyone. Then the king, seeing these things, was appeased ... and ordered the man, who had previously been guilty of the offense, to be released and go free. Not long after, when St. Brigid, the release and freedom having been achieved, was returning to her home, this fox, twisting itself deceitfully among the crowds and moving cleverly—appearing just like the other—fled as a fugitive to the deserted and wooded places to its own den, mocking the many horsemen and dogs pursuing it, and fleeing through the open fields, it escaped unharmed.

> *Compare the Speckled Book <note>original: "Leb. Br." or Leabhar Breac, a 15th-century Irish manuscript</note> page 65b, line 48:* Once there was a laborer <note>original: "bachlach"</note> of Brigid’s people cutting firewood. It happened that he killed a pet fox belonging to the King of Leinster. The laborer was seized by the king. Brigid ordered a wild fox to come out of the woods. It came then and was performing tricks and playing for the hosts and for the king at Brigid’s command. When the fox had finished its performance, it went safely into the woods, though the hosts of Leinster, both foot and horse and dog, were after it.

### Regarding Verse 64:

24. And when on another day the Blessed Brigid saw ducks swimming in the water with their natural animal instincts <note>original: "pectore carnali," literally "with a carnal breast"</note> and sometimes flying through the air, she summoned them to come to her. They, with feathered flight and such ardor of obedience to her voice as if they were accustomed to human care, flew to her without any fear of the crowd. Touching and embracing them with her hand and doing this for a short time, she allowed them to return and fly into the air on their wings ...

### Regarding Verses 65, 66:

She saw nine men in a certain special form of vain and diabolical superstition <note>This likely refers to the "díberg," a pagan or semi-pagan vow of brigandage common in early Ireland</note>, making a noise of ridiculous voices to the greatest infamy of their minds. In whose ways there was destruction and unhappiness; who, thirsting for the shedding of blood for the ancient enemy <note>meaning: the Devil</note> who reigned in them, with most wicked vows and oaths, before the Calends <note>the first day of the month</note> of that coming month should arrive, they arranged to commit the slaughter and murder of others. # I 5 Broccan's Hymn. 47

The most reverent and approachable Brigid preached with a honey-tongued abundance of words, so that, having abandoned deadly errors, they might blot out their crimes through heartbreak <note>The Latin *compunctionem* refers to a piercing of the heart by grief for one's sins.</note> and true penance. Those who, in the dullness of their minds... proceeded on their own way: ... those wicked ones departing, seeing an image like a man whom they should have killed, immediately stabbing him with their spears and beheading him with swords, returned as if after a triumph over their adversary and enemy with bloody and gore-stained weapons, appearing to many. In a wonderful way, although they killed no one, it seemed to them that they had fulfilled their vows; and since no one was missing from that province over whom they might triumph, and no doubt remained for anyone concerning this matter, the generosity of the divine gift made through Saint Brigid became known to all...

*Regarding Verse 68:*

26. . . . For a certain Lugidus, a very powerful man and the strongest of men, who performed the work of twelve men by himself through the great strength of his body, laboring on a single day as he wished, and similarly eating food enough for twelve men to be sufficiently fed . . ., prayed that she would pray to the Almighty Lord for him, so that he might temper his gluttony <note>The Latin *ingluviem* refers to excessive or gluttonous eating.</note>, by which he devoured excessively, and that he might not lose the ancient strength of his body because of this. And so Brigid herself, blessing him and praying to the Lord for him; he afterward remained satisfied with the food of one man, and, laboring as twelve workers used to labor, he remained in that same ancient strength.

*Regarding Verses 69, 70:*

27. A certain large and very great tree, prepared for some use by those woodcutters who practiced their craft in the forest, was cut down with axes . . . And when neither a multitude of men nor the strength of oxen nor the various skills of craftsmen could in any way move or drag that tree, while everyone withdrew from it, through the very strong faith of Blessed Brigid . . . lifting that extremely heavy tree by Angelic power through Divine mysteries without any mortal help, they brought it without any difficulty to the place St. Brigid intended . . .

*Regarding Verses 71, 72:*

28. And it occurs to our mind not to exclude this miracle through our silence, which the same venerable Brigid performed among her innumerable miracles. For a certain secular <note>A man of the world, rather than a member of the clergy or a monk.</note> man, noble in birth but burning with deceitful habits, lusted after a certain woman. Thinking craftily how he might enjoy her company, he entrusted his precious silver brooch <note>original: *sentem argenteam*; likely referring to a *spinther*, a common type of Roman or Celtic brooch or pin.</note> to her for safekeeping, which he then deceitfully took back without her knowing and threw into the sea, so that when she could not return it, she would become his slave and he could afterward use her embraces as he wished . . . And the modest woman, fearing these things, fled to Saint Brigid as if to a most safe city of refuge. When she had learned of the cause, or

### *Regarding Verses 73—76:*

29. Following these miracles, she was to be joined in glorious and most bright hospitality with a certain faithful woman. As Saint Brigid was making a prosperous journey in accordance with God's will across the vast plain of Breg <note>The *Mag Breg*, a fertile plain stretching across modern-day counties Meath and Dublin.</note>, and as the day declined toward evening, she came to the woman's dwelling and stayed the night with her. Because of her poverty, the woman had nothing to feed the fire, nor food to cook to feed such guests. She cut up the wooden beams of her loom <note>The Latin *ligna telaria* refers to the frame and tools used for weaving cloth.</note>—on which she used to work her weaving—to feed the fire. She then killed the calf of her cow, placing it upon the pile of that wood to roast it with a willing heart. After the meal was finished with praises to God and the night passed in the usual vigils, Brigid woke after that night in the early morning. To ensure that the hospitable woman (who had lost her cow’s calf) suffered no loss for her reception and refreshment of Saint Brigid, she found another calf of the same form with her cow, which she loved as much as the first. Likewise, she saw her weaving loom restored better than before, in the same shape and size as the previous ones had been...

*Compare the "Speckled Book" <note>The *Lebar Brecc* (Old Irish for "Speckled Book") is a 15th-century Irish manuscript containing many hagiographical texts.</note> page 65$^b$, line 64:* It was one of Brigid's miracles when she happened upon the widow in the plain of Chail <note>Original: *muig Chail*</note>. She killed her cow's calf for Brigid and burned her weaving-beam <note>Original: *garmain*</note> under it. God brought it about for Brigid that the weaving-beam was whole again the next day, *and the calf was there with its mother.*

### *Regarding Verses 77—80:*

30. . . . Indeed, she bestowed a silver vessel upon three lepers oppressed by infirmities. To ensure there was no cause for discord or fighting among them if they tried to divide it themselves, she spoke to a man skilled in the weighing of gold and silver, asking him to weigh this vessel into three equal parts for the three men. When he began to excuse himself, saying it could not be weighed out equally, Brigid—the most blessed of women—grabbed the silver vessel and struck it against a stone. It broke into three equal and identical parts, exactly as she wished. In a wonderful manner, when those three parts of the silver vessel were afterwards measured by weight, not one part was found to be smaller or larger than the others, not even by the weight of a single obol <note>A small silver coin or a unit of weight.</note>, among these three parts...

### *Regarding Verses 81—84:*

31. . . . For the overseas and foreign vestments of Bishop Conlaed <note>Saint Conlaed was the first Bishop of Kildare and a contemporary of Brigid.</note> of the honored heights—which he used while offering the sacred mysteries at the altars and in the sanctuary during the solemnities of the Lord and the vigils of the Apostles—

# I 6 Sanctan's Hymn.

<note>The following Latin passage describes a miracle of Saint Brigid regarding her charitable giving and divine replacement of garments.</note>
...was given to the poor. And when the time of the solemnity arrived, according to the custom that the high priest of the people himself should be clothed in his changing robes, Saint Brigid—who had given the former garments of that bishop to Christ appearing in the form of a poor man—handed over other garments for the others. These were similar in every way to the previous garments, both in texture and color, which she had received at that hour from Christ (whom she had clothed through the poor man), brought to her in a chariot of two wheels...

### Regarding Verses 85—88:

32. . . . For a certain man, driven by a particular necessity and in need of a measure of honey, entreated her. And while Brigid herself grieved in her mind, as she did not have honey ready to give to the one asking, the humming of bees was heard beneath the floor of the house in which she was then staying. And when that place, in which the bees were sounding with their voices, had been dug up and examined, there was found in it as much as sufficed for the work of the requester. And so, having received from her the gift of honey as much as his necessity required, he returned rejoicing to his own dwelling. (Verse 88 seems to be the author's own embellishment.)

## 6. Sanctan's Hymn.

Bishop Sanctán is considered the author of this hymn; a Briton by birth, he immigrated to Ireland just like his brother Matóc. This hymn contains a relatively large number of obscure passages (Verses 3, 5, 11, 18). It originally ended at Verse 20; the following verses, in which Sanctán himself is invoked, were only added later.

Each half-verse contains seven syllables; in Verses 21—24, every first and third half-verse has eight syllables.

<note>The following is translated from Old Irish.</note>
->I pray to the wonderful King of angels, for that is the name that is strongest,<-
->God be on my track, God to my left, God before me, God to my<-
->right*!<-

<note>* 2^b One syllable is missing in the original meter.</note>
<sig>4</sig>

# I 6 Sanctan's Hymn.

God to my aid! A holy invocation <note>original: *nóebtogairm*</note> against every danger I risk <note>original: *nodguasim*</note>—
<margin>4</margin> May the bridge of life be beneath me, and the blessing of God the Father above me.

May the noble Trinity raise us <note>original: *don foscai*</note>, for whom a holy death is certain <note>original: *bas baile*</note>;
The bright <note>original: *an*</note> Holy Spirit, the power of heaven, God the Father, and the great Son of Mary.

The Great King knows our kin, the witness above the faultless <note>original: *dillocht*</note> world;
<margin>8</margin> For my soul against every shameful fault, may the demon’s anguish <note>original: *dibocht*</note> not touch me <note>original: *nim tharle*</note>.

May God be with me to ward off every suffering <note>original: *soeth*</note>, may Christ attend <note>original: *frisinnle*</note> to my tribulations;
May apostles meet around me, to my aid may the Trinity be witness <note>original: *testa*</note>!

To my aid may mercy come swiftly from Christ whose songs are not hidden <note>original: *nad cétla celar*</note>;
<margin>12</margin> May neither death nor grief <note>original: *amor*</note> reach me, may neither plague <note>original: *mortlaid*</note> nor sickness come to me!

May no bitter shot reach me, besides the Son of God who confuses <note>original: *medras*</note> and deafens <note>original: *bodras*</note>;
May Christ protect us <note>original: *ainsiunn*</note> against every violent death, against fire, and against the troubling sea-swell!

---

**3ᵃ** <gloss>holy invocation: that is, of God.</gloss> **3ᵇ** <gloss>that I risk: that is, in which I am in danger.</gloss>
. . . (*Rest illegible*). Stokes translates tentatively: “*against every danger that I risk (?)*”. However, *nodguasim* likely cannot be correct if only for the sake of the rhyme.

**5ᵃ** <gloss>that is, let him awaken us from the sleep of sin... because we are made in the likeness of God.</gloss> **5ᵇ** <gloss>that is, it is for Him alone for whom death or madness is not certain. If it were we, however...</gloss> Stokes translates: “*Let (the) high Trinity raise us, to every one a holy death (be) certain!*” Compare: *since it is certain to go to death | to be under the will of Mary's son* (Codex Boernerianus).

**6ᵃ** <gloss>bright: in miracles and in wonders.</gloss>

**7ᵇ** <gloss>faultless: that is, de-faulted, that is, God is without fault.</gloss>

**8ᵇ** <gloss>let not... touch me: that is, they may not visit me.</gloss> <gloss>anguish: that is, without God with him but...</gloss> Stokes translates: “*let not demon's anguish (?) touch me.*”

**9ᵃ** *seth* in the manuscript (compare Verse 19ᵇ): <gloss>that is, every sorrow or disease.</gloss>

**9ᵇ** <gloss>attend to: that is, may he minister to.</gloss>

**10ᵇ** <gloss>that is, may the witnessing Trinity come to my...</gloss>

**11ᵇ** <gloss>that is, He is not hidden in songs, or His songs are not hidden.</gloss> Regarding this, Stokes translates: “*whose songs are not hidden*” (?).

**12ᵃ** <gloss>that is, may He not put the color of deathly pallor upon me.</gloss> <gloss>grief: this is 'amor' [sorrow], that is, 'alas, ah'.</gloss>

**12ᵇ** <gloss>plague: that is, when very many perish from one disease, that is, swift-deaths.</gloss>

**13ᵃ** <gloss>let not reach me: or 'may I not stumble', that is, may He not put a stumbling upon me.</gloss>

**13ᵇ** <gloss>confuses: that is, confusing-knowledge, that is, he confuses the lack of knowledge.</gloss> <gloss>deafens: that is, deafening-knowledge, that is, the knowledge is troubled... a shot... deafens besides the Son.</gloss>

**14ᵃ** <gloss>protect us: compare Hymn 1, 11.</gloss>

# I 6 Sanctan's Hymn.
<sig>4*</sig>

Against every sickness <note>original: *éiclind*</note> that is dangerous <note>original: *eslinn*</note> to my body with terrifying storms <note>original: *ainbthib huathaib*</note>,
<margin>16</margin> come to my aid <note>original: *domm air*</note>, O Lord <note>original: *fiado*</note>, at every hour, against wind, against swift waters!

I will swiftly speed the praises <note>original: *luathfe molthu*</note> of Mary’s Son, who fights noble <note>original: *finna*, literally "white"</note> battles <note>original: *arbaga*</note>;
the Elemental God <note>original: *Dia dulech*</note> will answer <note>original: *friscéra*</note> the breastplate <note>original: *lurech*</note> of my tongue’s battle <note>original: *arbaig*</note>.

In entreating <note>original: *digde*</note> God of the heavens, may my body be lasting <note>original: *sigith*</note> and laborious <note>original: *soethrach*</note>,
<margin>20</margin> so that I may not reach terrible hell; I entreat the King whom I have entreated <note>original: *adroetach*</note>.
->I entreat the King.<- <note>This repeats the opening of the poem, a traditional Irish closing device called a *dúnad*.</note>

---

**15ᵃ** <gloss>that is dangerous: that is, a safe death.</gloss> <note>The commentator notes that *bas* here is the 3rd person singular relative present subjunctive. The scholar Whitley Stokes translates this as: "that is dangerous to my body." For the word *eslinn* (dangerous/weak), compare the phrase "he is a leaky bark in dangerous peril" found in the Book of Leinster.</note>

**15ᵇ** <gloss>that is, which is with storms and with horrors.</gloss> <note>The commentator notes that *uath* (horror) is used here as an adjective.</note>

**16ᵃ** <gloss>come to my aid: that is, let him come to my assistance.</gloss> | <gloss>at every hour: that is, both day and night.</gloss> <note>Compare the phrase "celebration of every hour, he prays without intermission" from the *Liber Hymnorum*.</note>

**16ᵇ** <gloss>against the blast of the wind.</gloss> | <gloss>against swift waters: that is, in rivers <note>original Latin: *fluminibus*</note>.</gloss>

**17ᵃ** <gloss>praises;</gloss> <note>Compare the Old Irish phrase "his praise, more beautiful than treasures, shall be sung in lays by me."</note>

**17ᵇ** <gloss>great battle.</gloss> <note>Stokes translates this as "who fights for white fights," and suggests it means "for good causes." The German editor asks: Why not "who fights good fights"? (Compare the term *irbága* for contentions; *finna* is glossed as "good"). Perhaps it should be written as *fenna*.</note>

**18** <note>Stokes translates: "Elemental God will answer, 'a corslet which battles against my...'" | *dulech* (elemental): the editor suggests reading this as *dulem* (Creator).</note>

**18ᵃ** <note>This line is noted as having one syllable too few.</note> | <gloss>will answer: that is, he responds.</gloss>

**18ᵇ** <gloss>breastplate: that is, God.</gloss> | <gloss>of the battle: that is, he who fights, i.e., he who performs the battle.</gloss>

**19ᵇ** <gloss>lasting:</gloss> <note>Stokes cites a gloss from the Dublin manuscript H. 3. 18 which quotes this verse: "lasting, i.e., eternal, as in 'in entreating God of the heavens may my body be lasting and laborious' etc."</note> | <note>The manuscript reads *sethrach* (laborious), with a variant gloss *sethach*.</note>

**20ᵃ** <gloss>so that I may not reach: that is, so I do not arrive.</gloss>

**20ᵇ** <gloss>I have entreated: that is, I have appealed to.</gloss>

# I 7 Patrick's Hymn.

->Bishop Sanctan, holy <note>original: *sancta*</note> senior, soldier of angels, of white pure fame,<-
->may he save my body on earth, may he sanctify my soul in heaven!<-

->May I have a prayer from you, O Mary; may the King of heaven be merciful to us,<-
<margin>24</margin> ->against wound, against danger, against peril, O Christ, *under your protection* <note>original: *for do snádud*</note> to us!<-

->I entreat the noble eternal King, the only-begotten of God to watch over us,<-
->may the Son who was born in Bethlehem protect me from sharp dangers.<-

---

### 7. Patrick's Hymn.

This remarkable hymn is attributed to Saint Patrick himself. According to the preface, he is said to have composed it for himself and his monks as protection against the ambushes <note>literally "snares" or "plots"</note> of King Loegaire <note>The High King of Ireland during Patrick's mission</note>. However, it then became for everyone a <term>Lorica</term> <note>A "breastplate" prayer intended to spiritually arm and protect the wearer</note> against all kinds of enemies of body and soul; it was called *faed fiada*, which <term>Stokes</term> <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909), a prominent Irish philologist</note> translates as "guard's cry" <note>This phrase is also traditionally translated as "The Deer's Cry," referring to a legend where Patrick and his followers appeared as deer to escape the King's assassins</note>. Stokes also shares a second version found in the *Tripartite Life of Saint Patrick* <note>original: *Vita Tripartita S. Patricii*</note> (Oxford Manuscript Rawl. B. 512). Although I have not been able to re-examine this version myself, I am printing it here according to Stokes (B), because it is of great value for critical analysis. This hymn certainly had a metrical form originally. However, it has been very much distorted by <term>interpolations</term> <note>Later additions or insertions into the original text</note> and by changes to the original wording—

---

<gloss>21ᵃ For the abbreviation *sca*, Stokes wrote in *Goidelica* (2nd edition, page 148) *serca* ("love thou"); in *Remarks* (2nd edition, page 70) he corrects this to *sancta* and translates *sancta sruith* as "holy senior" ("*sruith*", like "*flaith*" [lord/sovereign], is grammatically feminine, though applied to a male). This isolated Latin word was likely chosen because of the name Sanctan. I have only one metrical concern: the three following verses have eight syllables in the first half, so we should expect eight syllables in this verse as well. This is the meter recorded in O'Donovan's *Irish Grammar* (page 480) under the name *Seadna*.</gloss>

<gloss>21ᵇ *gel glan* in the Manuscript. The reversal to *glan gel* was done following Stokes's example for the sake of the rhyme. Regarding "soldier of angels" <note>original: *milid aingel*</note>, compare "soldier of Christ" in the *Martyrology of Oengus* <note>original: *Fél. Juni 3*</note>, with the gloss: "that is, he is a soldier of Christ."</gloss>

<gloss>24ᵇ *under your protection to us* <note>original: *for do snádud dún*</note>: compare Hymn 1, verse 2 and Hymn 5, verse 106. The Manuscript reads *donádud*.</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces the "Lorica" or "Breastplate" of Saint Patrick, a protective prayer-poem. It includes an Old Irish invocation and scholarly notes on the hymn's legendary origin as a defense against King Loegaire.</summary>
<keywords>Saint Patrick, Lorica, Breastplate, King Loegaire, Faed Fiada, Whitley Stokes, Old Irish, Bishop Sanctan, Seadna meter, protective prayer</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly introduction to Saint Patrick’s Hymn, also known as the "Breastplate" or "Lorica." The previous page introduced the hymn's legendary origin as a protective prayer. This page focuses on the technical linguistic challenges of the text, specifically debating the grammatical cases (Dative vs. Accusative) of key Old Irish words and comparing the author's editorial choices with those of the philologist Whitley Stokes.</meta>

# I 7 Patrick's Hymn.

...has been obscured. I have pointed out the traces of the metrical form in the notes below the text, without changing anything in the text itself. In the division of the lines, I have only deviated from Stokes in verses 53–57.

->I arise today through a mighty strength, the invocation of the Trinity,<-
->through belief in the Threeness, through confession of the Oneness, in the Creator of creation.<-

---

1 *Is "strength" <note>original: niurt (neurt in Manuscript B)</note> to be taken with Stokes as a Dative, or is it to be understood as an Accusative? In favor of the Dative is first of all the form itself; we find the unmistakable Accusative in Verse 43 in "against every strength" <note>original: fri cech nert (fri cech neurt in B)</note>. That the Dative particle "to" <note>original: do</note> is missing is striking, but may be interpreted as a particular antiquity, which Stokes <note>Whitley Stokes, Goidilica (2nd edition), p. 154</note> has documented elsewhere. The letter "n" after "strength" <note>niurt</note> in Verse 5 is cause for concern. However, this initially only proves that the scribe of the manuscript considered "strength" <note>niurt</note> to be an Accusative. After all, the scribe of B even treated "sense," "eye," etc., in Verse 28 ff. as Accusatives. If "strength" <note>niurt</note> were truly an Accusative, one would also expect the "n" before "birth" <note>gene</note> in Verse 3, and before "God" <note>dé</note> in Verse 26, where it is present in neither Manuscript A nor B. — How "invocation of the Trinity" <note>togairm trinoit</note> is to be construed is not easily determined. Stokes translates it as “an invocation of (the) Trinity,” taking "Trinity" <note>trinoit</note> as an irregular Genitive form. Through the repetition of the verse at the end of the hymn, the wording is well-attested, so it would be risky to change it through conjectures. I consider "Trinity" <note>trinoit</note> to be an apposition <note>a descriptive word placed next to another to explain it</note> to "invocation" <note>togairm</note>, although this word in the <term>Félire</term> <note>The Martyrology of Oengus, an early Irish calendar of saints</note> is indeed usually construed with the Genitive; cf. "invocation of Simeon" <note>togairm Semeoin</note> Jan. 5, "invocation of Fintan" <note>togairm Findtain</note> Feb. 21, and "of Alexander" <note>Alexandri</note> 26; however, "invocation" <note>togairm</note> is probably a shortened Dative form <note>Zeuss, Grammatica Celtica (2nd edition), 269</note>, standing parallel in construction to "strength" <note>niurt</note>.*

2 *Stokes reads "confession" <note>foisitin</note>, while Manuscript A has "foisin". Stokes refers to the <term>Liber Hymnorum</term> <note>The "Book of Hymns," a collection of early Irish and Latin hymns</note> folio 11: "belief in the Oneness with confession of the Threeness" <note>original: cretem óenatad co foisitin tredatad</note> (Goidilica, 2nd ed. p. 101). He translated: “I believe in a Threeness with confession of an Oneness in (the) Creator of (the) universe,” but has recently explained "in the Creator of creation" <note>i n-dulemain dail</note> as “in the God of judgment” <note>original: in Deo judicii</note>. I take issue with the fact that Stokes translates as if the preposition "with" <note>co</note> were also transmitted in our text. Since the preposition is also missing in Verse 63, it is risky to simply supplement it. Perhaps "confession" <note>foisitin</note> is likewise a Dative, parallel to "strength" <note>niurt</note>; in that case, I would also want to take "belief" <note>cretim</note> the same way (not as the 1st person singular present verb "I believe," but as the Dative singular of the noun "belief" <note>cretem</note>), though this would admittedly necessitate changing "Threeness" <note>treodataid</note> into the Genitive form <note>treodatad</note>. My interpretation of "belief" <note>cretim</note> is supported by the fact that in the following sections, "I arise" <note>atomriug</note> is the only verb. Finally, however, "Oneness" <note>óendatad</note> will have to be changed to "unity" <note>óentad</note>, at least...*

<summary>This page analyzes the linguistic nuances of the opening lines of Saint Patrick's Breastplate, debating whether the nouns for strength, belief, and confession are used as verbs or as objects of a spiritual invocation. The author provides a technical critique of Whitley Stokes' earlier translations, favoring an older, more consistent grammatical structure.</summary>

<keywords>Saint Patrick's Hymn, Lorica, Old Irish grammar, Whitley Stokes, Trinity, Philology, Manuscript analysis, Martyrology of Oengus, Liber Hymnorum</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the linguistic structure of Saint Patrick’s Hymn. The previous page discussed whether the word for "strength" (niurt) should be interpreted as a Dative or Accusative case, noting the scribe's inconsistent use of the "n" prefix. This page moves into the transcription of the second and third stanzas and offers a comparative analysis of the grammar and syllable counts across different manuscripts.</meta>

# I 7 Patrick's Hymn.

I arise today through the strength of the birth of Christ with His baptism,
<margin>4</margin> [through] the strength of His crucifixion with His burial,
[through] the strength of His resurrection with His ascension,
[through] the strength of His coming for the judgment of Doom. <note>The "judgment of Doom" refers to the Last Judgment.</note>

I arise today through the strength of the rank of Cherubim, <note>original: grád hiruphin</note>
<margin>8</margin> in the obedience of angels,
in the service of archangels,
in the hope of resurrection for the sake of reward,
in the prayers of patriarchs,
<margin>12</margin> in the predictions of prophets.

---

*...the former form has not occurred to me elsewhere. Then Verse 2 consists of three five-syllable pieces.*

*Verses 4–6 in Manuscript B: . . . through the strength of His crucifixion with His burial, through the strength of His resurrection with His ascension, through the strength of His coming for the judgment of Doom.*

*Verse 6 reads "strength of coming" <note>original: niurt toniud</note> in Manuscript A, and "through the strength of his coming" <note>neurt a thoiniuda</note> in Manuscript B. Since a Genitive <note>a grammatical case showing possession</note> depends on "strength" <note>niurt</note> in the three preceding verses, one should probably also write "strength of coming" <note>original: niurt toniudo</note> here (following Manuscript B). Then Verse 6 has ten syllables, while the preceding ones have only eight. Should the word "of Doom" <note>original: bratha</note> be deleted?*

*Verses 7–16 in Manuscript B: I arise through the strength of the rank of Cherubim, in the obedience of angels, in the service of the archangels, in the hope of resurrection for the sake of reward, in the prayers of patriarchs, in the predictions of prophets, in the preaching of apostles, in the faiths of confessors, in the innocence of holy virgins, in the deeds of righteous men.*

*7 Stokes interprets "rank" <note>original: grád</note> as a Genitive Plural <note>original: Gen. Plur.</note>. However, should we then not expect an "n" at the beginning of the following word? Additionally, the Cherubim form only one "rank" or order <note>original: grad (Latin: ordo)</note> of the heavenly hosts. Compare the Liber Hymnorum <note>The Book of Hymns</note> 11b (Goidilica, 2nd edition, p. 67): "These are the nine ranks, namely: angels, archangels, virtues, powers, principalities, dominations, thrones, cherubim, and seraphim." This is a well-known medieval concept. Therefore, one should either write "of a rank" <note>original: graid</note> in the Genitive, or "rank" <note>grad</note> is a Dative standing parallel to "strength" <note>niurt</note>. Regarding the other verses, Stokes correctly supplemented Verse 9 from Manuscript B, though I believed I could omit the definite article following the analogy of Verse 8. Most of the following lines have six syllables. Verse 10 deviates the most: I consider this verse not to belong here because it interrupts the list of holy persons in a striking way. Verse 14 contains seven syllables: if one objects to the plural, one could write "in faith" <note>original: i n-iriss</note>; if one emphasizes the agreement between both manuscripts, one could write "in faiths" <note>original: i n-irsib</note> (Genitive Singular <note>original: Gen. Sing.</note> of *irise* and *irse*, see Zeuss, Grammatica Celtica, 2nd edition, p. 241). Then only Verse 11 remains with eight syllables.*

The commentary provides a detailed philological comparison between Manuscripts A and B, debating syllable counts and grammatical cases.</summary>

**Lines 17–25 in Manuscript B:** I arise through the strength of heaven, light of sun, brilliance of moon, swiftness of wind, depth of sea, stability of earth, firmness of rock. <note>Note that Manuscript B substitutes "moon" for "snow" and alters the order of the elements.</note>

**17–25** As the *aspiration* <note>a linguistic term for a change in the sound of a consonant, often marking a grammatical relationship</note> after *splendor* <note>original: áne</note> and the form *stability* <note>original: tairisem</note> prove, all the initial words in these lines are to be understood as *nominatives* <note>the grammatical case used for the subject of a sentence</note>. (*Stokes* <note>Whitley Stokes, a prominent 19th-century Celtic scholar</note> translates inaccurately: “In light of Sun” etc.) In lines 18, 20, 21, and 22, there are four syllables each; in lines 19, 23, 24, and 25, there are five syllables each. Since, furthermore, *brilliance of snow* (though note the variation in Manuscript B) interrupts the sequence of fiery things, it was likely originally placed after *swiftness of wind*.

**26–41** *This section will also originally have possessed a specific metrical form, but it is difficult to restore the same. Six verses (counting from "strength of God" [niurt Dé]) have six syllables; line 34 can likewise be brought to six syllables by including the word for "protection" [imdíten] from Manuscript B. It would be more daring to write "preceding" [remthecht] (compare with the word for "transgression" [tairmthecht] in Zeuss's Grammatica Celtica, 2nd ed. page 879) instead of "leadership" [remthechtas] in line 33. However, lines 28 and 30 remain with five syllables, line 31 with seven, and lines 27 and 35 with eight; the final three lines have nine, seven, and nine syllables, assuming the well-known abbreviation for "and" [ocus] is correctly expanded. — In line 38, Stokes now translates: "Against solicitations of nature" (Remarks, 2nd ed. page 70). In line 28, "for my guidance" [domm imthús] is translated by Stokes as "to guide me".*

**Lines 42–49 in Manuscript B:** I summon between me today all these powers against every cruel, merciless power that stands against my body and my soul; against the incantations of false prophets; against the false laws of women and smiths and druids; against every knowledge that corrupts body and soul for me.

**42–49** *The difference between the two manuscripts is very significant here. If one writes the word for "heretical" as "hertecda" instead of "heretecda" in line 46, then lines 44–47 each have seven syllables. Lines 42, 43, 48, and 49 have either never had a metrical form, or have lost it to the point of being unrecognizable. Regarding line 42, compare the "Vision of Adamnán" [Fís Adamnáin] section 1: "he calls up to him unto heaven the folk of charity" (as translated by Stokes). Stokes translates the current passage as: "So have I invoked all these virtues between me [and these] against every cruel,"*

"merciless power." <term>Tocurius</term> <note>an Old Irish verb meaning "to summon" or "to invite"</note> is, however, equivalent to "I have invited" or "I have summoned"; compare the phrase "it is the banishing of demons, it is the summoning of angels" in the *Epilogue of the Félire Óengusso, line 53*. By adding "and these," <term>Stokes</term> <note>Whitley Stokes (1830–1909), a prominent Celtic scholar</note> seeks to establish the construction usually used with the word "between" <note>original: "eter"</note> (compare *Zeuss, Grammatica Celtica, page 656*), but to what should "these" refer? "Between" always requires two parties; could the designation of the second perhaps be introduced here by the word "against" <note>original: "fri"</note>? Regarding line 49, Stokes translates: "Against every knowledge that defiles men's souls." However, the meaning of <term>arachuiliu</term> <note>a difficult verb likely meaning "to prohibit" or "to hinder"</note> is only guessed at. Stokes connects it to "sin" <note>original: "cuil"</note>, and further to a word in the *Milan Glosses (63a)* meaning "profaning," and assumes a verb *archuilim*. In *Beiträge VII, 54*, he considers *arachuiliu* to be a "U-imperfect" ("with an obscure infixed pronoun" according to his work *Goidelica, page 156*); but where is the imperfect meaning? For the word "souls," Stokes requires *anmain* (the accusative plural of "soul"); however, *anmain* is actually the accusative singular, just as "man" <note>original: "duini"</note> is the genitive singular.

## II.

# The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.

1. The Irish have two ancient saga cycles <note>original: "Sagenkreise," referring to the distinct bodies of mythological stories involving specific groups of heroes.</note>. This saga belongs to the older of the two, in which Conchobar, King of Ulster, Ailill and Medb, the ruling couple of Connacht, as well as the heroes Cúchulainn, Fergus, and others are the main characters. Conchobar is said to have died around the year 20 AD <note>original Latin: "p. Chr." (post Christum)</note>. The events of the second saga cycle are placed by tradition over two hundred years later: Finn mac Cumaill, the main hero of this saga cycle, is said to have been slain in the year 273; Finn’s son was Oisín (Ossian). See Hennessy, *Academy* 1871 p. 366. — As the quite numerous handwritten copies in the British Museum prove, the saga of the exile and the downfall of the sons of Usnech must have enjoyed great popularity. It contains good material for a tragedy and is combined with two other sagas of equally tragic character into the group of the “Three Sorrowful Tales of Erinn.” <note>These three tales are "The Children of Lir," "The Children of Tuireann," and "The Children of Usnech."</note> Four versions of our saga have become known to me, all of which are already available in print, and which I name briefly after the names of their editors:

1) **O’Curry’s Version.** This is found in the oldest manuscripts and may also be described as the oldest in terms of its overall character. It was published by O’Curry (along with the two other *Sorrowful Tales*) with an English translation in the 3rd volume of the journal *Atlantis*, which is unfortunately difficult to access, but long before him by O’Flanagan, even if...

<summary>This page introduces the "Exile of the Sons of Usnech," placing it within the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology and comparing it to the later Fenian Cycle of Finn mac Cumaill. The author highlights the story's tragic nature and its categorization as one of the "Three Sorrowful Tales of Erinn."</summary>
<keywords>Sons of Usnech, Ulster Cycle, Conchobar, Cúchulainn, Finn mac Cumaill, Oisín, Three Sorrowful Tales of Erinn, O’Curry, Irish Mythology</keywords>

...from a less reliable manuscript, in the book mentioned under 3) on pages 145–177, likewise with an English translation.

2) **Keating’s Version.** This is found in Keating’s *History of Ireland* under the year of the world 3940 <note>This refers to "Anno Mundi," a dating system based on the biblical timeline of creation.</note>, in the Dublin edition from the year 1811, Vol. I, pp. 370–377. I have also found it in two manuscripts in the British Museum, one of which (Egerton 107, fols. 55, 56) was written in 1638, the other (Gift from J. Milles <note>original Latin: "Ex dono J. Milles"</note> Plut. CLXVIII E, fols. 40, 41) in 1694. It is a free retelling of the first-mentioned version (the poetic passages are missing), and likely originates from Keating himself, as he was already writing around the year 1630 (cf. O’Curry, *Lectures on the Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History*, p. 21).

3) **O’Flanagan’s Version**, known from his *Deirdri* (pp. 16–135), a work that forms the second part of Vol. I of the *Transactions of the Gaelic Society of Dublin* (Dublin 1808). An old manuscript of this “more modern and diffuse version” is not known; according to O’Curry (*Atlantis* III, p. 378) the original for O’Flanagan’s text is a manuscript from the year 1758 (Trinity College Dublin, H. I, 6). The story begins only with the recall of the three sons of Usnech from Scotland, and then tells of their downfall in great detail, with many variations in the particulars.

4) **Macpherson’s Version**, represented by Macpherson’s English poem *Darthula*, for which no Gaelic text has yet come to light. The ancient saga is only recognizable here in the names—which are in part badly distorted—and in a few main features. O’Flanagan compared this poem more closely with the Irish saga, including a bitter polemic against Macpherson’s “monstrous fabrication.” <note>James Macpherson was a famous 18th-century poet who claimed to have discovered ancient epics by a bard named Ossian; however, he was often accused by Irish scholars of inventing or heavily altering authentic Irish legends to suit Scottish nationalist interests.</note>

2. For our purposes, only the oldest form of the saga (Section 1, Part 1) is considered. I know of three old manuscripts in which this is contained:

1) The **Book of Leinster** (Trinity College Dublin, H. 2, 18, fols. 192, 193), from the first half of the 12th century;

->## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.<-
Page 61

2) The **Yellow Book of Lecan** (Trinity College Dublin, H. 2, 16, col. 749), written around the year 1390;
3) A vellum <note>Vellum is a high-quality writing surface made from specially prepared animal skin, typically calfskin, used for important medieval documents.</note> manuscript in the British Museum, designated **Egerton 1782** (p. 129), dating from the 15th century (?).

O’Curry based his edition on the text of the **Yellow Book of Lecan** (Lc), which he strangely considered to be superior to that of the **Book of Leinster** (see *Atlantis III*, p. 387). O’Flanagan’s text corresponds very closely with the Egerton manuscript; perhaps the manuscript he used (see O’Curry in the work cited above, p. 378) was a copy of it. I am publishing the text from the oldest manuscript, the **Book of Leinster** (L), but I include the more significant variations from the other two manuscripts below the main text. I personally collated <note>To collate is to compare different versions of a text side-by-side to identify differences and errors.</note> all three manuscripts during my stays in Dublin (1871) and London (1874), but I was saved much time and effort because Mr. Stokes and Mr. O’Grady most kindly placed their own transcripts from the Leinster and Egerton manuscripts at my disposal.

3. These three manuscripts are independent of one another; it is also quickly apparent that they cannot be traced back directly to one and the same original. When the saga was first fixed in the form we have before us, and who did so, is unknown—as is the case with every ancient Irish saga—but this fixing certainly dates back to a very ancient time. Unfortunately, every new scribe gave the text more or less the linguistic "clothing" of their own era. Therefore, with Irish texts, the age of the manuscript in which they are contained is of particular linguistic importance. National saga and history were carefully cultivated at the courts of the ancient Irish kings, where there was a specific office for the narrator. Our saga begins in the house of such a man, for Fedlimid is called the storyteller (<term>scelaige</term> — a professional narrator of traditional tales) of King Conchobar. Having originated in pagan times, the saga was also propagated into the Christian era, and as a rule, without becoming entangled with Christian elements. But also<unclear>r</unclear> <meta>The final characters "aur" likely belong to "auch" (also/too) or represent a catchword meant to lead into the next page.</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the preservation and transcription of Irish sagas, moving from the physical manuscripts to the cultural and religious influences that shaped the recorded texts. It specifically addresses how Christian scribes handled pagan mythological content.</meta>

->## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.<-
Page 62

...to the influence of Christianity one must attribute the fact that the specifically pagan elements in Irish sagas have been obscured and pushed into the background. Nevertheless, there are still many sagas with clearly mythological content. The Christian monks were certainly not the first to record the old sagas, but they later faithfully transcribed and propagated them after Ireland was converted to Christianity. For the scribe of the *Lebor na hUidre* <note>The *Book of the Dun Cow*, dating to the late 11th or early 12th century, is the oldest surviving manuscript written entirely in Irish.</note> was a poor monk, and the scribe of the *Book of Leinster* was even a bishop (cf. *O'Curry Lect. p. 182 ff.*). Remarkable in many respects is the monkish judgment on the most famous Irish saga, the *Táin Bó Cualnge* <term>Cattle Raid of Cooley: The central epic of the Ulster Cycle, often compared to the Iliad.</term> (The Raid of the Cattle of Cooley), which is found at the end of the only complete copy of it in the *Book of Leinster* (first half of the 12th century): "But I who wrote this history, or rather fable, do not place my trust in certain things in this history or fable. For some things there are by the deceptions of demons, some however are poetic fictions, some similar to the truth, some not, and some for the delight of fools." <note>original Latin: "Sed ego qui scripsi hanc historiam an verius fabulam, quibusdam fidem in hac historia an fabula non accomodo. Quaedam nam ibi sunt per strigia demonum, quaedam autem figmenta poetica, quaedam similia vero, quaedam non, quaedam ad delectationem stultorum."</note>

5. As far as the external form of the ancient Irish saga is concerned, the narrative parts are always in prose; indeed, the prose is all the more plain and simple the older the form of the saga and the manuscript are. Only in the later forms of the saga and in later manuscripts do we encounter that turgid prose which, clad in the trappings of poetry, rattles so dreadfully with them at every step that one could lose all taste for the beautiful principle of alliteration. <note>In Irish literature, alliteration was not just a decoration but a strict structural requirement for many poetic forms.</note> The importance of the ancient Irish saga in general lies in the fact that we have the saga here in its natural form, which precedes individual poetic treatment and the synthesis into great poetic wholes. Why is it that this abundance of sagas, with their interesting plots, picturesque descriptions, and sharply defined characters, never found their Homer? Macpherson's Ossianic poems <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796) published poems he claimed were translated from ancient sources by the blind bard Ossian, though they were largely his own creative reworkings of Gaelic lore.</note> can...

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the literary form of Irish sagas, specifically contrasting prose narrative with the verse sections found within them, before beginning the summary of the "Exile of the Sons of Usnech."</meta>

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.
Page 63

...neither in content nor in tone can they be considered a poetic rendering of the ancient Irish saga.

However, metrical form is not entirely absent from the old Irish saga: in the midst of the prose narrative, dialogues and lyrical monologues appear in verse—a fact that is certainly not unimportant for the history of the epic. These verses are placed in the mouths of the saga characters themselves, as if they were the poets (cf., for example, Chapter 17 of our saga and elsewhere). I suspect that Oisin <note>Also known as Ossian, the legendary warrior-poet of the Fenian Cycle.</note> became a poetic figure in this way. The poems attributed to him in the saga were considered his work and gradually became the prototype for an entire literary genre. The oldest poems of this kind certainly belong to a specific place within the fixed saga; without knowledge of the entire saga, they are sometimes incomprehensible. Further below, I share two such individual poems found in the <term>Book of Leinster</term> <note>A major 12th-century Irish manuscript that serves as a primary source for early Irish literature and mythology.</note>, one of which is attributed to Oisin and the other to his father <term>Finn mac Cumaill</term> <note>Finn McCool, the legendary hunter-warrior and leader of the Fianna.</note>.

A feat of the professional poets and scholars at the royal courts also consisted of summarizing the content of a saga as briefly as possible in metrical form. Such verses are particularly difficult to understand (cf. the remarks on page 25 regarding Broccan’s Hymn). These, too, are frequently found incorporated into the saga or added at the end (cf., for example, the poem at the end of the saga shared under section III).

### 5. The content of our saga is briefly as follows:

Fedlimid, the storyteller at the court of Conchobar, King of Ulster, has organized a feast. His wife is pregnant. As she walks through the house to go to rest, the child in her womb cries out loudly. All the guests are horrified; the Druid Cathbad is consulted regarding the meaning of this wonder. Cathbad prophesies that Fedlimid’s wife will give birth to a child who, once grown into the most beautiful of women, will one day bring great ruin upon Ulster. Derdriu shall be her name...

<meta>This page continues the summary of "The Exile of the Sons of Usnech" (Longes mac n-Usnig), transitioning from the introductory remarks on the previous page regarding the child’s prophecy and birth.</meta>

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.

...be her name. The child is born. There is a desire to kill her immediately, but Conchobar <note>Conchobar mac Nessa, the legendary King of Ulster.</note> forbids this and has the girl raised in seclusion so that she might one day become his wife. **Derdriu** <note>Also known as Deirdre of the Sorrows.</note> grows up in isolation; aside from Conchobar, she sees only her foster-mother and **Lebarcham**, Conchobar’s "messenger-woman" <note>original: "conversation-woman." Lebarcham was a satirist and messenger who, in Irish myth, was one of the few people permitted to travel freely throughout the kingdom.</note>. 

Once, Derdriu saw Conchobar slaughtering a calf in the snow, and a raven came to drink the red blood. She wished for a man whose hair was black as the raven, whose cheeks were red as the blood, and whose body was white as the snow. From Lebarcham, she learns that **Nóisi**, the son of Usnech, matches this wish. Derdriu meets him and binds him by his honor to elope with her. At his call, his brothers arrive, and out of necessity, they resolve to flee with Derdriu; their followers accompany them. 

Conchobar, however, pursues them, so they eventually cross over to Scotland. The King of Scotland takes them into his service. There, Derdriu is spotted by an official of the King and is deemed worthy to become the King’s wife. This proposal is made to her in secret, but she rejects the offer and warns Nóisi of the new danger in which he stands. Upon her advice, they flee to an island. 

Then, their friends in **Ulster** persuade King Conchobar to recall them. **Fergus**, **Dubthach**, and **Cormac** (the son of Conchobar) are to be the guarantors of their safety. Yet, Conchobar intends betrayal. The guarantors are detained under a pretext, and **Eogan**, King of **Fernmag**, who had previously been at odds with Conchobar, buys back the King's friendship by murdering Nóisi. 

Fergus, Dubthach, and Cormac avenge this betrayal in a bloody fashion and turn to **Ailill** and **Medb**, the King and Queen of **Connacht**. Derdriu remained in Conchobar’s power, but she was never happy again. In a moving manner, she laments her sorrow. Once, Conchobar asked her who in the world she hated most. "You yourself and Eogan," she replied. Thereupon, Conchobar intended to hand her over to Eogan for a year. Conchobar mocks her as she rides in the chariot with both men. At that, she dashes her head against a rock.

<vocab>Longes mac n-Usnig (The Exile of the Sons of Usnech), Derdriu (Deirdre), Conchobar (Conor), Lebarcham, Nóisi (Naoise), Usnech, Ulster (Ulaid), Fergus (Fergus mac Róich), Dubthach, Cormac, Eogan (Eoghan mac Durthacht), Fernmag (Farney), Ailill, Medb (Maeve), Connacht</vocab>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of "The Exile of the Sons of Usnech" (Longes mac n-Usnig), specifically contrasting the authentic Irish manuscript tradition with James Macpherson’s 18th-century Scottish "Ossian" poems.</meta>

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.
->5<-

6. Macpherson's poem <note>Referring to "Darthula," one of the poems published by James Macpherson in the 1760s, which he claimed were translations of an ancient Scottish bard named Ossian.</note> agrees with the Irish saga only in its broadest outlines: the heroine flees with her favored lover; the rejected but more powerful lover destroys his rival along with his two brothers; and the heroine herself does not survive their downfall for long. The most significant deviation lies in the fact that the rejected lover is not King Conchobar, but King "Carbair" (<term>Gaelic</term> Cairbre). This figure, however, does not belong to the first saga cycle, but to the second <note>Irish mythology is traditionally divided into "cycles." The first is the Ulster Cycle (featuring Conchobar and Cú Chulainn); the second is the Fenian Cycle (featuring Fionn mac Cumhaill and his son Oisín). Macpherson is accused here of mixing characters from different eras.</note> (see page 59): Cairbre and Oscar, the son of Oisín, kill each other in a duel. The old poem I shared under VI 1 refers to this. O'Flanagan <note>Theophilus O'Flanagan (1760–1814), an Irish scholar who published an early edition of this saga in 1808.</note> has already pointed out these and other deviations from the ancient Irish saga in the work cited; we wish only to emphasize the main result: that in Macpherson's *Darthula*, as is often the case in other "Ossianic" poems (cf. Hennessy, *Academy* 1871, page 366), the two main saga cycles are mixed together. The Irish tradition keeps these as strictly separate as Greek myth separates, for example, the voyage of the Argonauts from the Trojan War. As for the remaining names, they are admittedly only available to us in their anglicized form. The Irish names **Derdriu** (Genitive **Derdrenn**), **Nóisi** (Genitive **Nóisen**), **Andle**, **Ardan**, and **Usnech** correspond in Macpherson’s work, in order, to: **Darthula**, **Nathos**, **Althos**, **Ardan**, and **Usnoth**. Even if we take the process of anglicization into account, the difference in the first three names is so great that the Scottish forms cannot be viewed as an organic development of the Irish ones; they look instead like mutilations of non-native names. The fourth name is the same on both sides. The difference between **Usnoth** and **Usnech** is minor: in later manuscripts, it is not uncommon in Irish for *th* to be written instead of *ch*. An impartial critic cannot doubt that Macpherson's poem represents, in every respect, a more recent form of the ancient saga.

<summary>This page critiques James Macpherson’s "Darthula," arguing it is a modern, distorted version of the Irish legend that incorrectly mixes characters from the Ulster and Fenian saga cycles.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the editorial methodology used for the Irish saga "The Exile of the Sons of Usnech," transitioning from a critique of Macpherson’s modern adaptations to the technical details of transcribing the original medieval manuscripts.</meta>

# II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.
->66<-

7. Regarding my treatment of the manuscripts, I have only cited from Lc <note>The Yellow Book of Lecan, a major late 14th-century Irish manuscript.</note> and Eg <note>Egerton 1782, a 16th-century manuscript in the British Library containing many legal and literary texts.</note> what constitutes a real difference in the text: different wording, different inflected forms, deviating word order, additions, or omissions. If I had also wanted to list every orthographic <note>spelling</note> variation, the *Variant Readings* <note>original: "Varia Lectio"; a scholarly list of differences between manuscript versions.</note> would have swollen into a chaotic mass. I have briefly summarized the peculiarities of Lc and Eg—which usually represent a later stage of the language—in the appendix following the text.

I have generally indicated the expansion of abbreviations used in the manuscripts by using *italics* <note>original: "cursiven Druck"</note>, following the example of Whitley Stokes <note>A prominent 19th-century Irish scholar and philologist.</note>. However, I deemed this unnecessary when individual letters are expressed in a well-known manner: namely, *n* indicated by a stroke, *m* by a wavy line, *r* by a superscript vowel, and the syllable *con* <note>original: "con"; a common shorthand in medieval scribal practice.</note> by an inverted "c." Furthermore, the syllables *en*, *er*, and *or* are often abbreviated by a stroke: in certain frequently occurring words—namely, in the forms of the root *ber* and in the preposition *for*—the abbreviated spelling is only indicated where it seemed advisable for some specific reason. I chose the spellings *com-bai* and *com-batar* when the first syllable was indicated by the aforementioned abbreviation for *con*. 

Otherwise, the abbreviated spelling has remained unmarked in the following cases: 1) In the frequently occurring case forms <note>Grammatical variations of a noun indicating its role in a sentence, such as subject or object.</note> of the names *Conchobar*, *Usnech*, *Ulaid* (Ulster), *Eriu* (Ireland), and *Alba* (Scotland), which are often written out in full in one or another manuscript, but often abbreviated in all three: *Conchobar*, *Conchobair*, *Uisnig*, *Ulaid*, *Ulad*, *Erenn*, *Erinn*, *Alban*; also in other names (*Cormac*, *Dubthach*, *Durthacht*), provided they were written out in full in at least one of the three manuscripts and there can be no doubt about the correct spelling; — 2) in the <term>conjunction</term> *ocus* (and), which in this text appears as *ocus* only a few times in Eg. and Lc., but is otherwise always indicated by the Latin *et* or the common shorthand symbol for it; — 3) in the word *mac* (son), the vowel of which is only occasionally written in Eg.

<summary>The author explains his editorial methods for transcribing the Irish manuscripts, detailing how he handles variant readings and the common shorthand abbreviations used by medieval scribes.</summary>

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.

The particle **din** <note>A common Old/Middle Irish conjunction often meaning "then," "moreover," or "therefore."</note> is usually written in full in manuscript **L** <note>The Book of Leinster.</note>. In contrast, the particle corresponding to the Old Irish *dono*, *dano*, or *dana* is always expressed by the abbreviations *dā* or *dō*. Since the full spelling *dana* occurs in one place in the saga communicated under section III (compare also O'Donovan's Supplement to O'Reilly's Dictionary), I am introducing it into these Middle Irish texts following the example of Whitley Stokes and William Maunsell Hennessy, although I have found the spelling *dna* (without any sign of abbreviation) in another manuscript.

---

->**The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.**<-
<note>This is the famous tale of Deirdre and the sons of Usnech, one of the "Three Sorrowful Tales of Erin."</note>

The asterisks in the text mark the difficult words or generally difficult passages: for most of these, I have shared in the appendix what has been achieved toward their understanding.

What caused the exile of the sons of Usnech? *It is not difficult to tell.* <note>original: "ni insa"; a standard formulaic opening in Irish literature where a question is posed and then answered.</note>

The Ulstermen <note>original: "Ulaid"; the people of the northern province of Ireland.</note> were drinking in the house of Fedlimid son of Dall, the storyteller of King Conchobar. Now, the wife of Fedlimid was serving the host from above, and she was pregnant. Drinking horns and portions of food went around, and they raised a drunken shout. When they were going to bed, <margin>5</margin> the woman went to her bed. As she went across the floor of the house, the child screamed in her womb, so that it was heard throughout the entire enclosure. Every man in the house rose up against the other at that scream, so that they were head-to-head <note>A state of high tension or confrontation.</note> within the house. Then Sencha son of Ailell <note>A wise judge and mediator in the Ulster Cycle.</note> cried out: "Do not stir!" said he, "let the woman be brought to us, so that it may be known <margin>10</margin> what this noise is." Then the woman was brought to them.

6. “Let the girl be killed!” said the young warriors. “Not so,” said <margin>5</margin> Conchobar. “The girl shall be taken by me tomorrow,” said Conchobar, “and she shall be reared according to my own will, and she shall be the woman who will be with me.” And the men of <term>Ulster</term> <note>original: "Ulaid"; the people of the northern province of Ireland.</note> did not dare to dispute with him about it. This was then done. She was reared by Conchobar until she was the most beautiful girl in <term>Ireland</term> <note>original: "hErinn".</note>. It was in a separate court <note>original: "liss"; an enclosed dwelling or earthen fort.</note> she was reared so that no man of <margin>10</margin> Ulster should see her until the time she should sleep with Conchobar. There was no person allowed into that court except her foster-father and her foster-mother, and also **Lebarcham**; for one could not keep her out, because she was a <term>female satirist</term> <note>original: "banchainte"; a woman of high social standing who served as a messenger and poet, often feared for her ability to compose insults.</note>.

7. Once, then, the girl's foster-father was skinning a <term>weaned calf</term> <note>original: "lóig fothlai".</note> <margin>15</margin> on the snow outside in the winter to cook it for her, and she saw a raven drinking the blood on the snow. Then she said to Lebarcham: “Beloved would be the one man who would have those three colors yonder: hair like the raven, and the cheek like the blood, and the body like the snow.” “Dignity and luck to you,” <margin>20</margin> said Lebarcham, “he is not far from you; he is in the house near you, namely, **Naoise** son of Usnech.”
