# II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech. 72

...son of Usnech.” “I shall not be truly well,” she said, “until I see him.”

8. Once, then, Naoise was alone on the rampart of the fort, that is, of **Emain**, <term>chanting</term> <note>original: "andord"; a low, musical humming or chanting, often described as a foundational tradition of the sons of Usnech.</note>. Sweet, moreover, was the chanting of the sons of Usnech. Every cow and every beast that heard it would give two-thirds of additional milk. Every person who heard it found it a sufficient peace and delight. Their valor, too, was great: even if the whole <term>province of Ulster</term> <note>original: "coiced Ulad"; one of the five ancient provincial kingdoms of Ireland.</note> were around them in one place, provided the three of them stood back to back, no one could defeat them because of the excellence of their parrying and protection. They were as swift as hounds in the hunt; they killed deer by their speed.

9. While Naoise was thus alone outside, she <term>stole out</term> <note>original: "mos etlann"; implies a quick, stealthy movement.</note> to him as if to go past him, and he did not recognize her. “Fine,” said he, “is the <term>heifer</term> <note>original: "samaisc"; a young cow, here used as a metaphorical term for a young woman.</note> that goes past me.” “Heifers must be big,” she said, “where there are no bulls.” “You have the bull of the province with you,” said he, “that is, the King of Ulster.” “I would choose between you two,” she said, “and I would take a young little bull like you.” “Not so,” said he... <note>original: "Ni thó"; he refuses her advances because of the prophecy.</note> At that, she made a leap <note>Towards him.</note>...

# II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech. 73

...to him, and she grabbed his two ears on his head. “These are two ears of shame and mockery,” she said, “unless you take me away with you.” “Go away from me, woman!” he said. “You shall have them!” she replied. <note>original: "Rot bia"; literally "It shall be to you." Deirdre is threatening Naoise with permanent social disgrace—symbolized by the "ears of a slave"—if he rejects her plea for protection and love.</note> At that, his **chanting** <term>andord</term> <note>A specialized, low-frequency musical drone or humming. The sons of Usnech were famous for this sound, which was said to have magical calming properties, though here it signals Naoise's extreme distress.</note> rose up from him. When the Ulstermen inside heard the chanting, every man of them rose up against the other. <margin>5</margin>

10. The sons of Usnech went out to restrain their brother. “What are you doing?” they asked. “Let not the Ulstermen kill us because of your crime!” Then he told them what had been done to him. “Evil will come of it,” said the youths. “Even though it be so, you shall not be under shame as long as we are alive. We will go with her into another land. There is no king in Ireland who will not give us a welcome.” That was their counsel. They set out that night: three times fifty warriors they were, and three times fifty women, and three times fifty hounds, and three times fifty servants, and Deirdre was with them. <margin>10</margin>

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.

...the borders of Scotland, and they took up residence in the wilderness there. When the mountain game failed them, they turned to taking the cattle of the men of Scotland for themselves. 

The Scots went out to destroy them in a single day, but the brothers went to the King of Scotland, and he took them into his service, and they took up mercenary work <term>amsai</term> <note>Professional military service, often performed by landless warriors or exiles in exchange for maintenance or pay.</note> with him. They set up their houses on the palace green; the houses were built specifically because of the girl [Deirdre], so that no one might see her with them, lest the brothers be killed on her account.

12. Once, then, the steward went out early in the morning and made a circuit around their house, and he saw the couple <note>Naoise and Deirdre</note> asleep. He went then and woke the King. "We have not found," said he, "a woman worthy of you until today. There is in the company of Naoise son of Usnech a woman worthy of the King of the Western World. Let Naoise be killed at once, and let the woman sleep with you," said the steward. "No," said the King, "but go and woo her for me every day in secret." This was done. However, whatever the steward would say to her by day, she would tell it to her husband that same night at once. Because nothing could be gained from her, the sons of Usnech were ordered into dangers and battles and difficult places <note>original: "drobelaib"; refers to treacherous or impassable terrain used as a death trap.</note>, so that they might be killed. Nevertheless, they were strong in every combat, so that nothing could be done to them in those military encounters.

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech. 75

13. The men of Scotland were gathered to kill them following the advice given to the king. Deirdre told Naoise of this. "Depart from here," she said, "for unless you leave tonight, you will be killed tomorrow." They departed that night until they reached an island in the sea. That news was reported to the men of Ulster. "It is a pity, O Conchobar," said the Ulstermen, "for the sons of Usnech to fall in the lands of enemies because of an ill-fated woman <note>original: "drochmna"; literally 'bad woman'. The Ulstermen blame Deirdre's beauty for the brothers' predicament.</note>. It would be better to provide them protection and a guard <note>original: "a n-imguin"; suggesting both protection and fighting on their behalf.</note> and for them to return to their own country, rather than falling at the hands of enemies." "Let them come then," said Conchobar, "and let guarantees of safety be sent to them." That message was brought to them. "It is welcome to us," they said. "We shall go, and let Fergus come with us as our guarantee <term>commairge</term> <note>A legal protector or surety. To harm someone under a 'commairge' was a supreme insult to the protector's honor.</note>, along with Dubthach and Cormac, the son of Conchobar." These men went and took them by the hand at the sea <note>A gesture signifying the formal acceptance of protection and the beginning of their safe conduct.</note>.

14. However, a plot was formed to invite Fergus to ale-feasts <note>original: "cormannaib"; feasts involving the ritual drinking of ale.</note> on the secret advice of Conchobar, for the sons of Usnech had declared they would eat no food in Ireland until they first ate the food of Conchobar. Therefore, Fiacha, the son of Fergus, went with them while Fergus and Dubthach remained behind at the feast. The sons of Usnech traveled on until they were on the green <term>faidche</term> <note>The open assembly area or meadow surrounding a royal residence like Emain Macha.</note> of Emain Macha. It was then that Eoghan mac Durthacht, the King of Fernmag, arrived to make peace with Conchobar, for he had been at war with him for a long time. It was he who was commissioned to kill the sons of Usnech, and Conchobar’s mercenaries <term>amsai</term> were gathered around him so that the brothers might not reach the King.

## II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech. 76

15. The sons of Usnech were standing in the middle of the assembly-ground <note>original: "faidchi"; the open green or meeting area in front of a royal fort.</note>, and the women were sitting on the ramparts of Emain <note>Emain Macha, the royal capital of Ulster.</note>. Then Eogan <note>Eogan mac Durthacht, a king who had been at enmity with Conchobar but was now doing his bidding.</note> came with his troop across the assembly-ground; Fergus’s son <note>Fiacha, who remained with the brothers while his father was detained at the feast.</note> also came and stood at Naoise’s side. Eogan welcomed them with a thrust of a great spear into Naoise, which broke his back through him. At that moment, Fergus’s son lunged and put both arms around Naoise; he threw himself under him and cast himself down upon him. And thus Naoise was struck from above, through the body of Fergus’s son. They were then killed throughout the assembly-ground, so that none escaped except those who fell by the point of the spear and the edge of the sword; and she <note>Deirdre</note> was brought over to Conchobar to be at his side, and her hands were bound behind her back.

16. Word of this was brought then to Fergus, and to Dubthach, and to Cormac. They came and immediately performed great deeds: namely, Dubthach killed Mane, the son of Conchobar, and Fiachna, the son of Fedelm, Conchobar’s daughter, with a single thrust; and Fergus killed Traigthréun, the son of Traiglethan, and his brother. This was done because of the violation of Conchobar’s word to them <note>The "violation" refers to the breaking of the 'commairge' or legal protection Fergus had guaranteed the brothers.</note>. A battle was fought between them on that same day, so that three hundred of the Ulstermen fell among them. Before morning, the maidens of Ulster were killed by Dubthach, and then Emain was burned by Fergus. It is there they went <note>The text begins to describe their flight to the province of Connacht.</note>

**[Leinster Manuscript Version:]**

Dear was the firm, just mind,
dear the tall, modest warrior,
after traveling through the woods of Ireland <note>original: "Fál," a poetic name for Ireland</note>
dear was the gathering in the late watch.

Dear the grey eye that women loved,
it was fierce against enemies,
after a journey through the forest, a noble union,
dear the deep chanting through the dark wood.

I do not sleep now,
and I do not paint my nails;
joy comes not to my mind,
since the sons of Indle <note>referring to the sons of Uisneach</note> do not come.

I do not sleep
half the night in my bed,
my mind wanders among the crowds,
yet I neither eat nor laugh.

Joy today is not for me
in the assembly of Emain <note>Emain Macha, the royal seat of Ulster</note> where nobles gather,
neither peace, nor pleasure, nor rest,
nor a great house, nor fair companionship.

**[Egerton Manuscript Version:]**

Dear the firm, just gentleness,
dear the tall, modest warrior,
after traveling the wood-plain of Ireland,
dear the attendance in the late watch.

Dear the grey eye that women loved,
it was fierce against enemies,
after a forest circuit, a noble union,
dear the deep chanting through the dark wood.

I do not sleep now,
and I do not paint my nails;
joy comes not to my mind,
since the sons of Uisneach do not come.

I do not sleep
half the night in my bed,
my mind wanders among the crowds,
yet I neither eat nor laugh.

Joy today is not for me
in the gathering of ale where nobles are filled,
neither peace, nor pleasure, nor rest,
nor a great house, nor a king’s companionship.

O Conchobar, what are you doing?
You have heaped sorrow upon me through weeping;

# Appendix I.

## Peculiarities of the Manuscripts *Lc.* and *Eg.* <note>Referring to the Yellow Book of Lecan and Egerton 1782, two vital medieval Irish codices</note>

I briefly summarize here the points in which I have not consistently reported the readings of *Lc* and *Eg* in the *Variant Readings* <note>original: 'Varia Lectio'</note>. The number of examples gives an approximate *scale* for the frequency of occurrence. However, I must first state that I do not list certain things for each manuscript individually. They are the following: The manuscripts fluctuate between *s* and *ss* in the middle and at the end of words: *so it was heard* <note>original: 'co clos' and 'co closs'</note>; *under the court* <note>original: 'fon less' and 'fon les'</note>; *in the house* <note>original: 'isin tig' and 'issin tig'</note> and so on. The same applies to *rr*, which has arisen from *assimilation* <term>assimilation: when two speech sounds become more similar to one another</term>: *so that he moved* <note>original: 'coro lai' and 'corro lai'</note>. Noteworthy is the alternation between *he went* <note>original: 'doluid' and 'dolluid'</note>. Also regarding the infection <term>infection: here referring to lenition or the softening of consonants in Celtic languages</term>, the manuscripts are inconsistent: thus, in the first chapters, the forms Usnig, curid, ceile, cluinethar, cluined in *L* correspond to: the forms Uisnich, cuirid, celi, clunethar, cluned in *Lc*, and the forms Usnigh, cuirid, celiu, cluinethar, cluinid in *Eg*. Even less was it my intention to consider in detail the fluctuation in the improper diphthongs *ai*, *oi*, *ui* created by the intrusion of *i* from *a* or *o*: *under your breast* <note>original: 'fot broind' L. Lc, 'fot bruind' Eg</note>; *in Emain* <note>original: 'i n-Emain' L, 'Eamuin' Lc, 'Emuin' Eg</note> etc. Here it must further be considered that a certain type of *a* (especially often in *Eg*) was, for me at least, indistinguishable from *u*, even where I observed particularly closely with the desire to recognize an *a*, as in *he was born* <note>original: 'dogenuir' Eg</note>. In such cases I have always written *u* (*pearl-like* <note>original: 'némunto'</note>, *it was done* <note>original: 'dorónud'</note> as well as *in disgrace* <note>original: 'fo mebuil'</note>, *in your guilt* <note>original: 'at chinuid'</note> against *nemanda*, *doronad*, *fo mebail*, *it chinaid* in *L*);

---

1 *you make* <note>original: 'gniisi' Eg. 'gnisiu' Lc.</note> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 2 *large omitted in Eg.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; *before her head Eg.*
*she threw herself Eg. she let herself Lc.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 3 *fragments Eg. fragments Lc.* &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; *so that she was dead Eg.*
4 *The Exile of the Sons of Uislenn and the exile of Fergus and the death of Deirdre (sic) Lc. The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach there and the cause of the exile of Fergus and the death of Deirdre. The end. Eg.*

<meta>This page continues the philological analysis of the Irish saga "The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach," specifically comparing linguistic variations across the primary medieval manuscripts.</meta>

# II The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach <note>original: "Longes mac n-Usnig"</note>
<sig>6*</sig>

Even the form *hingenu* <note>daughters</note> is found alongside *ingeni* in the *Egerton 1782* manuscript <note>abbreviated as Eg.</note>. However, I leave it to the reader's discretion to correct *u* to *a* in such cases. In Middle Irish, *i* and *e* in final syllables—specifically in inflectional syllables <term>Flexionssilben: the endings of words that indicate grammatical case or number</term>—are no longer as strictly distinguished as they were in Old Irish: Nominative Singular *cride* <note>heart</note> in the *Book of Leinster* <note>abbreviated as L</note>, *cridi* in the *Yellow Book of Lecan* <note>abbreviated as Lc.</note> and *Eg.*; Genitive Singular *taige* <note>of a house</note> in *L*, *tighe* in *Eg*, *tigi* in *Lc*; "throughout the whole court" <note>original: "fon less uile"</note> in *L*, *uili* in *Lc*, *uli* in *Eg*. This is particularly relevant regarding the *ia*-stems <term>a specific category of nouns in Old Irish grammar</term>. In some of these, as is well known, the *a* has become fixed. Here again, there is diversity in treatment: "at the color of snow" <note>original: "fri dath snechtai"</note> in *L*, *snechta* in *Lc. Eg*; "a shout of drunkenness" <note>original: "gáir mesca"</note> in *L*, *mescai* in *Lc*, *meuscco* (Genitive); "a fierce, hateful deed" <note>original: "gním n-granni n-garg"</note> in *L*, *granda* in *Lc*, *granna* in *Eg*. All these points have not been consistently accounted for in the *Variant Readings* <note>original: "Varia Lectio"</note>.

## Peculiarities of the Yellow Book of Lecan (Lc).

1. **Voiced stops** <term>Media: the consonants b, d, g</term> in place of Old Irish un-aspirated **voiceless stops** <term>Tenuis: the consonants p, t, c</term>: *fidir, badar, berdís, marbdais, tiagad, congabsad, ro sudigsead, tangadar, arrofeadadar, comaidecht, dorochradar, doleigi, lia naimdi, airfided, comm ég, ag* (for *oc*). This occurs more frequently than in *Eg.*

2. **Voiced stops** aspirated between vowels or at the end of a word: *lepaidh, sluagh, cruaidh, druadh, roghmar, it chinaidh, atchuaidh, ardaigh, righ, cloidhib, biudh, Feidhlimthi, Eoghan, adhgeoin, cia beidh.*

3. The aspirated pronunciation of the **voiced stops** in the specified positions must have occurred early, for the interchange of *d* and *g*—already detectable in *L*—is based upon it: *ursclaige* for *ursclaide* <note>defense/warding off</note> chapter 8 (*ursclaidi* in *Lc*). In *Lc*: *cobsaig* for *cobsaid* <note>firm/steady</note> 17, 37; *geissig* for *geisid* <note>cries out</note> 4, 10?

    For the more common spellings *buredaig, corodusig, Usnig*: *buredaich, coro dusich, Uisnich* (also *Uisnech*).

4. *th* for *ch*: *ro scaith, suaithnid*. The same difference exists between *sechnon* in *Lc* and *sethnon* in *L. Eg.* <note>meaning "throughout"</note>.

5. *p* appears rarely: *lenap, erptha* (*ro herbbad*).

6. *nd* for *nn*: *indaidchi sin* <note>that night</note> (also found in *L* chapter 10), *do chormandaib, cuslendaig, tuindi.*

7. *ndn* for *nd* or *nn*: *indnithruib, indnaidchi, indnund, indni sin.*

8. *ea* for *e*: *cuibreand, leass, fear, Seancha, feastar, bean, leachtan, Leabarcham, cluinead, cuiread, tairmeasc, imbeathaid, tria indlead, siardeas, Easruaid, Feargusa, for feada, seach, atbeiread*. Alongside these, however, one finds *cluned, fecht* and others.

<summary>This page provides a detailed linguistic comparison of Middle Irish orthography across three major manuscripts, focusing on the phonetic shifts and spelling variations in the Yellow Book of Lecan.</summary>
<keywords>Middle Irish, Old Irish, Yellow Book of Lecan, Book of Leinster, Egerton 1782, phonology, orthography, voiced stops, voiceless stops, Uisneach</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the linguistic analysis of the Irish saga "The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach" (Longes mac n-Uisnig), specifically focusing on the grammatical and orthographic variations found in the Yellow Book of Lecan (Lc) and the Egerton 1782 manuscript (Eg).</meta>

# II The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach.

9. *Strange epenthesis <note>The insertion of a sound or letter within a word</note> of the i:* tarimchell, ferig, tairib, amisear *for* tairmchell *and so forth.*

10. *The n of the accusative singular is not always preserved:* i tir ele, fecht and (*alongside* fecht n-and).

11. *The preposition "in" doubles its "n" even in Old Irish before pronominal elements (Zeuss² p. 627). But here, except in cases like* inna cotlud (*alongside* ina sesam) *also before nominal forms:* inn Emain, inn Eri, inn Erind, inn Ultaib, ind essid. *This is likely how to understand* innoenló, *compare* isinnoenló. *The later form* a *for* i *in:* aliss, at chin, am farrad (*alongside* it farrad) *and others.*

12. *Alongside the old form of the preposition* oc *also* ic, *occasionally* ag (aga hailgnigud); etrom, etorru *alongside* itir.

13. *The article, which in Middle Irish has abandoned the specific form of the dative plural, also no longer strictly distinguishes the neuter:* in mid, in tigi (*nominative plural*); *but* andeilm.

14. *The particle* din *often stands where the Book of Leinster (L) has* dana *or* dono; dana *is rare in the Yellow Book of Lecan (Lc).*

15. *For* "he says" <note>original Latin: "inquit"</note> *usually* or *and* ar, *occasionally* ol.

16. *The length marks are particularly sparsely placed in the Yellow Book of Lecan (Lc).*

---

### Peculiarities of the Egerton Manuscript (Eg).

1. *Media <note>voiced stops like b, d, g</note> in place of Old Irish unaspirated tenuis <note>voiceless stops like p, t, k</note>:* fidir, badar, berdais; óag, com ég; gach, gu ro la, gu ro dúisig, gusin tan, gusaniud.

2. *Aspirated media between vowels or at the end of a word:* sluagh, Usnigh, Fedlimidh, scelaighi, issin tigh, broghtar. *For aspirated* b, u *is written:* in uen.

3. *th for ch:* ro scaith.

4. *cc for g:* arccuit (*of silver* <note>original Latin: "argenti"</note>), Euccuin, aircc, tuarccaib. *Very often the unaspirated tenuis of Old Irish is designated this way:* corccra, foccla, olcc, ind óicc, a coiccert, tánicc, acc dul, hicc ól (*alongside* ac ól *etc.*). *Similarly* lottar, ro lasatt.

5. *Frequently* p *for* b: ispert, uimpi, impi (*feminine*), ina (*feminine*) pruinn, compui, compatar, erptha; hidrupurt, tuip, ipius (*for Old Irish* ibes), droibéluip, lip, leunup; co ru gap, foiltleauphar.

---

-> * These partly bear a dialectal character, e.g., those mentioned under 10, 12, 13, 14. Traces of dialectal differences in the older language have not yet been given much attention. <-

It focuses on vowel insertions, prepositional changes, and the shifting use of consonants in the Lecan and Egerton manuscripts.</summary>

# II The Exile of the Sons of Uisnech. 85

6. **$\breve{u}$ denotes the *pronunciation of 'f' following an originally present nasal***: *a$\breve{u}$fail* <note>original: "a$\breve{u}$fail (= i fail)"</note>; but also *itir fianuib $\breve{u}$fer n-Alban* <note>among the warrior-bands of the men of Scotland</note>.

7. ***q* is frequently used for *cu***: *qcu*, *qgi-sium* (*alongside* *chuicce*); but also *innoidqi* (*this night*), *cinn ar qnn* (*head to head*).

8. ***th* for original *t* between vowels or at the end of a word, where the old language already preferred *d***: *cona haicciuth*, *atcluiniuth*, *atberiuth*, *coiciuth*, *foath*, *cuidbiutho*.

9. ***ea* for *e***: *longeas*, *bean*, *tairmcheall*.

10. ***iu* for Old Irish *e***: *atcluiniuth*, *cona haicciuth*, *atberiuth*; *ind ingiun* (*alongside* *ingen*), *a muimiu*, *a celiu*, *rechtairiu* (**Nominative**); *mu mochligiu*, *hi tir n-ailiu* (**Accusative**); *mo craidiu* (**Vocative**); *gen n-gairiu*, *Muirtemniu* (**Genitive**); *ipius* (*Old Irish* *ibes*); *coiciuth*; *luingius*. **Isolated instance**: *suiliub* (**Dative Plural**).

11. ***eu* for *e***: *cech feur*, *leunup*, *meuscco*, *do leupthugad*.

12. ***au* for *a***: *diau imdaid*, *seallglausaib*, *im faurrud*, *siaurdess*, *do taubairt*, *for láuim*.

13. ***When 'i' intrudes into 'a', it becomes 'oi'***: *oiti*, *caemoinech*, *amoig* (*alongside* *amuig*), *oirfitigh*, *toirb*, *foilte*, *rí Fernmoige*, *oil cloichiu*.

14. ***Preference for 'o' in the final syllable***: *con facco*, *con derno*, *cualo*; *for tua ina hEmno*, *maro*, *a lámo*, *datho*, *na fiado*, *snechto*, *cóeco n-gillo* (*alongside* *coeca*), *gnímo moro*, *fiadoch*, *fri cíano*.

15. **Isolated instances**: ***io* for *i***: *míol*. — ***eau* for Old Irish *e***: *foiltleauphar*. — ***æ̆i* for Old Irish *ái***: *cæ̆in* (*twice*). — ***ea* for *ia***: *eat*, *éat*. — ***eu* for *ia***: *eurum*.

16. **Inaccurate Genitive forms**: *Erinn*, *Uislinn*, *mac Uisnech*; *tri mac n-Uislinne* (*compare* *Derdrinni* *in the signature in Lc* <note>Lc refers to the Yellow Book of Lecan manuscript</note>).

17. **The preposition *in***: *a tigh*, *'alleṡ*, *a$\breve{u}$fail* (= *i fail*); *inn Eriu*, *hind inis*; **on the other hand** *issin tigh* etc. — **The preposition *oc* also appears in the forms *ac*, *ic*.**

18. **The prepositions *im* <note>around</note> and *etar* <note>between</note> with the Dative**: *impaib*, *etarruib* (*immediately followed by* *etaru*).

19. **The Article is generally regular, except that the specific form for the Dative Plural is missing and the Neuter is no longer distinguished from the Masculine and Feminine**: *in deilm*. **Isolated instance**: *an tan*.

20. **Alongside *din* <note>then/therefore</note>, *dono* is found several times.**

21. **For *inquit* <note>Latin: "he/she says"</note>, usually *ol* (13 times), less frequently *or* (4), *ar* (3), *uar* (1).**

# Appendix II.

## Remarks on the Text.

*Here I share how *O'Flanagan* and *O'Curry* translated the difficult passages. The latter undoubtedly understood the older language better than the former, but even *O'Curry* silently bypasses every difficulty and has a translation for everything. I myself see in many places only that they have not yet been correctly interpreted, without being able to completely remove the difficulties myself. Furthermore, I have cited the passages which *Stokes* cited and translated in his grammatical works for the sake of individual forms.*

Chapter 1. The question and answer with which the saga begins points us back to the times of oral storytelling: the question belongs to the one who wished to hear the story, the answer to the narrator. *Not difficult* <note>original: "Ni insa"</note> is a popular answer, usually written in abbreviated form as *ni*; it is found in full spelling, rhyming with *with me* <note>original: "limmsa"</note>, in one of the poems contained in a manuscript from the monastery of *St. Paul* in Carinthia, see *Goidelica* <note>A collection of Old Irish glosses and scholia edited by Whitley Stokes.</note> 2nd edition, p. 176.

> "Let the woman be brought to us, he says, so that we may learn the cause of this noise" <note>original: "Tucthar chucuinn in ben ol se co fiasmais cid diatá in deilm si"</note> *Stokes* Contributions VII, 53.

Chapter 2. The metrical form was first recognized by *Ebel* in *O'Flanagan's* text (which, as mentioned, agrees with the *Egerton* manuscript), *Zeuss* 2nd edition, p. 962. From the *Egerton* manuscript one sees clearly that there must be six seven-syllable half-verses. *O'Flanagan* translated: „. . . what violent noise is it that spreads alarm through the house, woman, saith he; it sounds from thy womb; roars from thy comprehension; ears hear it; a gleam of strong light is its similitude. Many an individual within its compass. My hardy blood it wounds.“ — *O'Curry* translated: „What startling sound [is that], o woman! said he, which ascends in the cavity of thy rumbling, compressed womb? by ears is heard the growl between thy two sides powerfully swelling; great terror has seized upon me; my heart is wounded by steel.“ — Since the 3rd person singular passive of *I hear* <note>original: "cluinim"</note> is *is heard* <note>original: "cluiner"</note> (*Cormac's Glossary Translation* p. 15 *auchaide*), then *cluinethar* <note>A specific verb form in Old Irish.</note> must be understood differently.

Chapter 3. Even if this piece originally had a metrical form, it is particularly obscured here by an old corruption of the text. *O'Flanagan* translated: „Then Cathbad said, Listen unto Cathbad of mild generosity, civil, a great mild chief, magnified,

# II The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach. 87

exalted through the science of Druidism. Then Felim said, "Since mine are not the fair words of explaining knowledge, as women I understand not; what in concealment within thy womb screamed so loud." — O’Curry translated: "Let Cathbad hear [said she] the comely-faced, beautiful; a chief, pure, great, magnificent, who is moved by the druidisms of Druids; since not to myself belong fair words, from which Felim might obtain illumination of knowledge; since the woman does not know, what in her womb there be, though it is in the receptacle of my womb it screams." <note>The author is comparing his own reading with that of Eugene O'Curry, a 19th-century scholar of Irish manuscripts.</note>

**Chapter 4.** Here the metrical form is unmistakable: each half-verse must have seven syllables, compare Ebel in the *Journal of Comparative Philology* 2, 962. Since there are 19 half-verses in total, either one must have been lost or one added (perhaps line 8 *niamdai* — *partardeirg*? <note>original: "shining — crimson-red"</note>). I suspect that we have two different poems here; the second begins at Verse 10 (*Geisid* <note>original: "She screams"</note>). The first is particularly corrupt <note>meaning the manuscript text is damaged or contains errors</note>, although it was translated without hesitation by both O’Flanagan and O’Curry: "under the girdle round thy womb hath screamed a female infant of shining yellow hair, of poignant eyes, of ears sensitive to sound; her cheek of purple red, with the colour of snow; I compare her teeth to pearls; I identify her lips to strawberries; a virgin from whom shall arise many misfortunes to the Ultonians <note>The people of Ulster</note>" — O’Flanagan. "In the receptacle of thy womb screams a maiden of yellowest yellow hair; her eyes the most enchanting, blue-glancing Sticadoes <note>likely a reference to a type of flower or gem</note>; her cheeks blue crimson, to the colour of snow we liken them, her teeth without blemish, pearly; her lips of cherry-red; a maiden on whose account there will be many woundings between Ultonian heroes." — Verse 1 is to be read as *becestair* <note>original: "screamed"</note> following the Lecan and Egerton manuscripts; Verse 2 *budi* (Comparative?) *budichaiss* <note>original: "yellow/curly-yellow"</note>, if the latter is the correct word. Verse 3 is corrupt. Regarding Verse 4, compare *her two cheeks were redder than the foxglove of the mountain* (*The Wooing of Étaín*, Egerton Manuscript 1782). Regarding Verses 6 and 7, compare *a row of teeth, even and high in her head, it is a lustrous pearly thing, etc.*

Verse 9 *among the chariot-chiefs of the Ultonians* cannot be correct: originally both words probably stood in the Accusative case.

Verses 10—14 are translated by O’Curry: "There screams in thy womb, there shouts a maiden fair, tall, long-haired, for whom champions will contend; whom many high kings will solicit; [kings] who shall be seeking the heavy loaves of [military] pay of Conchobar’s province."

---

**Chapter 5.** Verse 1 The meter suggests *manderamár* <note>original: "great destruction"</note>. O’Flanagan translates: "on whose account there shall be great weeping", O’Curry: "for whom we have prophesied", Stokes: "about whom we speak".

Verse 3 *cesfaitit* etc. "Ulstermen shall suffer in thy time" — Stokes, *Contributions to Irish Philology* VII, 35.

# II The Exile of the Sons of Usnech.

Verse 7. *cluinti* means "**hear this**" (2nd person imperative) <note>Reference to Whitley Stokes, *Beiträge zur vergleichenden Sprachforschung* VII, 42</note>.

Verses 9—12. Since the preceding stanza refers to the **exile of the sons of Usnech**, and the following one to the **exile of Fergus**, this stanza likely refers to the **recall** and the **murder of Nóisi**. Verse 11 has one syllable too many in the *L* <note>Leinster</note> manuscript; in both other manuscripts, it is missing. O'Flanagan translates: "**There is objection in thy visage, tho' it be hereafter, that shall destroy a prince's mighty sons**"; O'Curry: "**Its wickedness shall be rued, even afterwards, when shall fall the sons of mighty kings.**" Stokes cites *Beiträge* VII, 49 *fofoesat maic rig rogmair* "**Sons of a fortunate (?) king will fall.**" On page 70, line 4 from the bottom, read *fofoesat*.

Verse 15. "**And a deed that hath contracted disgrace**" O'Flanagan; "**and a deed from which cryings shall come forth.**" The reading of *L* is corrupt here; *ar cóemsat guil* and then *guin Fiachnai* will be closer to the original text.

Verse 21. "**You will do a deed ugly, wild**" Stokes *Beiträge* VII, 18.

Verse 23. "**Your little heap shall be not in its due place**" O'Flanagan; "**thy grave shall lie in a place not native**" O'Curry.

Chapter 6. *Ailebthir dom réir fein* "**she shall be reared <note>original Latin: *nutrietur*</note> according to my will**" Stokes *Beiträge* VII, 64.

Chapter 7. *Ni pam slánsa* . . . "**I shall not be well, she says, until I see him**" Stokes *Beiträge* VII, 40. VI, 472.

Chapter 9. *no thogfaind-se etruib far n-dís* "**I would choose between you two**" Stokes *Beiträge* VII, 36 (isolating the sentence); *no gebaind tairbín óc amalt-sa* "**I would take a young, small bull like you**" Stokes *Beiträge* VII, 23. — Of the words missing in *L*, *cith fobithin faitsine Cathbad* <note>even because of the prophecy of Cathbad</note> still belongs to Nóisi's answer. Then Derdriu asks: *In dom femid-sa deiri-si sin* (**do you say this to reject me**)? Nóisi answers in the affirmative: *Bid do imorro* <note>It is for that indeed</note>.

Chapter 10. *Cid no tái* etc. "**What are you about, say they, is not there the fated destruction of Ulster**" O'Flanagan. "**What art thou about, for which the U. should wound thee for thy faults**" O'Curry.

*Is and dochuaid dóib*: it should be written as *atchúaid* (as in the *Lc* and *Eg* manuscripts), since only this compound has been proven so far to have the meaning "**to report, to tell.**"

*Regmait-ne lee i tír ele* "**we will go with her into another land**" Stokes *Beiträge* VII, 20.

## II The Exile of the Sons of Uisnech

->**Faesam**<-
"escort or protection which a guest received on his visits while passing from one house to another" O’Donovan Supplement to O’Reilly’s Dictionary. <term>Faesam: A legal right of protection or sanctuary</term> was the right which every <term>Trebaire: A head of a household or freeman</term>, or chief of household, possessed of entertaining and protecting strangers, or of giving sanctuary to those charged with offences or debt, during a certain number of days according to his rank, without having to enter into recognizances to the <term>Fine: The agnatic family unit or sept in ancient Irish law</term>, or being held specially responsible for their acts" Sullivan, O’Curry’s *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish* Volume I p. 293 (see Volume III Index).

**Ardáig na ro marbtais impi** "so that they would not be killed for her" (better "on her account") Stokes, *Contributions to Irish Philology* VII, 61.

**Chapter 12. Marbthar etc.** "Let Nóise be slain forthwith and let the woman sleep with thee, says the steward. No! says the king" Stokes, *Cormac's Glossary* Translation p. 15. <note>Nóise is one of the three sons of Uisnech and the lover of Deirdre.</note>

**Chapter 13. Imthigid etc.** "go away, she says, for if you do not go away tonight, you will be killed tomorrow" Stokes, *Contributions* VII, 5. — **Mani thistai as hinnocht** "if you do not go away tonight" ibid. 42. — Regarding the rarer construction **maic Uisnig do thuitim** <note>original: "the falling/death of the sons of Uisnech"</note> compare *Grammatica Celtica* second edition p. 924; the Leabhar Breac and Egerton manuscripts have the more common construction, in which the subject of the infinitive follows in the dative with "do" [to/for].* — **Ragthair** "it shall be gone" <note>Latin original: "ibitur"</note> Stokes, *Contributions* VII, 62. — **Tiagait side co ragbaiset etc.** "Let them come till we take them into hand from beyond sea" O’Flanagan. ". . . and they took them by the hand from off the sea" O’Curry, with the note: "They led them by the hand, that is, they took them under their protection, off the island to which they had fled."

**Chapter 14. Ar isbertatar etc.** "For Uisnech’s sons said that they would eat no food in Ireland" Stokes, *Contributions* VII, 53.

In O’Flanagan’s version, it is told in more detail how Fergus was held back: Fergus is bound <note>by a 'geis' or magical prohibition</note> never to refuse a feast (loc. cit. p. 32). Conchobar builds his plan upon this. Borrach, one of his confidants, whose <term>dún: A fortified residence or fort</term> lay near the sea (p. 41), must invite Fergus to a feast when he arrives there with the sons of Uisnech (p. 51). Fergus may not refuse the invitation; on the other hand, he has promised Conchobar to bring his protégés to <term>Emain Macha: The ancient capital of Ulster</term> without delay. He stays behind and sends his sons with the sons of Uisnech.

**Chapter 15. Co tuc fói etc.** "until he put him under him, and until he laid down upon him" O’Curry.

\* Should one not actually expect: **Ba ferr a comaitecht oldás a n-imguin**? <note>original: "Their accompaniment/protection would be better than their slaying."</note>

Chapter 16. *Sarugud Conchobair impu* <note>original: "Sarugud Conchobair impu"</note> “Conor was put to flight by them” O’Flanagan; “and C. was insulted in regard to them” O’Curry. “*Sarughadh* violation” O’Donovan Supplement to O’Reilly’s Dictionary. — “*Sarughudh*, an insult or assault not amounting to the shedding of blood; female violation, the violation of a church or ecclesiastical dignity; the violation of any sanctuary” Sullivan, O’Curry’s *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish* III, 473 (*Crith Gablach* <note>An Old Irish law text concerning social status</note>).

*Ar ro fetatar* etc. <note>original: "Ar ro fetatar" - For they knew</note> “for they knew that they were a couple that would kindly receive them; and that they were not affectionately disposed towards Ulster” O’Flanagan; “. . . who would sustain them, and it was not turning their back on love for the Ultonians <note>The people of Ulster</note>” O’Curry.

Chapter 17. Verses 5—8. “Naisi, companion accomplished, mild, lament him at once with me; Ardan, subduer of the luxurious boar, bewail Andli of mighty vigour” O’Flanagan; “Noisi at mead-making, brimming, sweet, — I preparing a bath for him at the fire, — Ardan with an ox or fat hog, Annle’s shoulder-bundle [of faggots] over the high river” O’Curry. Verse 8 is likely to be read with the Egerton manuscript as *asclang* <note>meaning a load or bundle carried on the shoulder</note>.

Verse 11. *baithium* <note>original: "baithium"</note> was it to me? compare *bithom* <note>original: "bithom"</note> *Contributions to the History of the German Language and Literature* VII, 41. But in the Leinster manuscript a syllable is missing. “Ever more delightful to me thro’ life” O’Flanagan; “I have known, ere now, a chase upon a doe” O’Curry. Stokes writes in a letter: “*ba haithnium* is right: there has been knowledge-to-me (*aithne* + *um*, the suffixed personal pronoun 1st Person Singular).

Verses 13, 14. “Whenever mighty Naisi would set out to hunt the woods, the fair wide plains” O’Flanagan; “when Noisi the noble would set a mess on the faggots of the wild plains” O’Curry.

Verse 17. *In cach mí* (every month) seems to be corrupt. “Tho’ sweeter to you is the dirge of pipes and horn trumpets” O’Flanagan.

Verse 23. In the Leinster manuscript a syllable is missing; *ba* should be added before *binniu* <note>meaning "sweeter"</note>.

Verses 29—32. “Naisi’s grave has been made, sorrowful indeed was its consequence; he supplied numbers, by might, of waving beverage, in their slaughter” O’Flanagan; “. . . sorrowful to me that banquetting, which a party have given through guile, the deadly draught of which they have died” O’Curry.

Verses 33, 34. “Delightful their birth of most beauteous bloom, whose manhood rose to highest vigour” O’Flanagan; “Beloved the trimmed one of finest beard, with us the chosen one though unbearded” O’Curry.

Verses 35, 36. *ba dirsand* etc. <note>original: "ba dirsand"</note> “it is sad that I will not rise up today” Stokes *Contributions* VII, 45, in which he traces *nadresco* back to *na-adrecsu*, and wrongly considers this to be a future tense. He himself immediately thereafter cites a real future form of this root in *nocho n-erus* “I shall not rise.” “How sad the consequence to-day! The sons of Usnach have been immolated” O’Flanagan; “Sorrowful that I

Instead of *dam* <note>to me</note>, one should write *lem* <note>with me</note> according to the Leabhar Lecan manuscript for the sake of the rhyme.

V. 10. 
> „The coming of the slaughter of Usnach's sons through me" O'Flanagan. 
> „The coming of death over Uisle's son" O'Curry.

V. 11. 
> „Black corses made of their fair frames" O'Flanagan. 

*Curnan cirdub* „a dead-black heap" O'Curry.

V. 12. Instead of . . . *m-ban* <note>of women</note>, one should read *fer* <note>of men</note> with the Leabhar Lecan manuscript for the sake of the rhyme. O'Flanagan writes *sech ilu*; in the Egerton manuscript, the *r* of *ilur* <note>multitude</note> is only expressed by the superscript position of the *u*.

V. 29, 30. 
> „Sent to us was Fergus fair to bring us o'er the wide main" O'Flanagan. 
> „Against us transgressed" (*forruich rind* <note>he has injured us</note> in the Leabhar Lecan manuscript) O'Curry. 

V. 29 has the correct number of syllables only in the Egerton manuscript.

# II The Exile of the Sons of Uisneach <note>Original title: Longes mac n-Usnig. This is a famous tragic tale from the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology.</note>

**Verses 33, 34.** O'Flanagan writes *long since they would be*, "However long they would be on the plain, the nobles of Ulster <note>Original: Ulad</note> at the beck of Conor." "Though they were upon the plain, the men of Ulster all before the face of Conor." — O'Curry.

**Verse 35.** "I would give them all up for Naoise's <note>The text uses the initial 'N.' for Naoise, the tragic hero of the tale.</note> company." — Stokes, *Contributions* <note>Original: Beitr. (Beiträge zur vergleichenden Sprachforschung)</note> VII, 22. For the sake of the meter, the word *se* <note>A grammatical particle in Old Irish</note> is to be deleted.

**Verses 39, 40.** O'Flanagan divides the lines incorrectly: *The affection is stronger that lives, though my darling be dead, o Conor!* <note>Original: Is tresi comand a mair, Mo dae o la, Chonchobhair</note> "Sorrow is more powerful than the sea, if you would know it, o Conor." — O'Curry.

### III.

# The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.

1. This saga, which is remarkable for its descriptions of customs and other details, has not yet been printed. The plot, which forms its content, is recounted by O'Curry in *Lectures on the Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History*, page 486, and *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume III, page 372.

The manuscripts which I have used are the following:
1) *Book of Leinster* (12th century), folios 72 and 73, designated by **L**;
2) *Miscellanea Hibernica* (Trinity College Dublin H. 3, 18, a paper manuscript of the 16th or 17th century), Part II, pages 743–748, designated by **M**;
3) *Harleian Manuscript 5280* in the British Museum (a vellum manuscript written circa 1560), folio 50, designated by **H**.

Naturally, the text of **L** has been used as the foundation, specifically in a transcript by Mr. Hennessy, which I collated <note>compared and verified</note> with the original in 1871. Due to a lack of time, I was only able to use the second manuscript (**M**) for difficult passages. For **H**, I possess a collation made by Mr. O'Grady in 1871, as well as my own from 1874.

2. The content of the story is briefly as follows:
The King of Leinster, Mesroida, called Mac Dáthó <note>original: "Mac Dáthó," meaning "Son of the Two Mutes"</note>, possessed a famous dog named Ailbe, the ownership of which both Conchobar, the

### III The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.

The King of Ulster, as well as the ruling couple of Connacht, Ailill and Medb, each wish to purchase the dog through rich counter-gifts. Mac Dáthó is in great embarrassment as to how he should decide between the embassies that have appeared simultaneously. On the advice of his clever wife, he promises the dog to each of the two parties, but stipulates that the kings, accompanied by a select troop, should come to collect it themselves. These arrive on one and the same day, and Mac Dáthó slaughters his fabulous pig in their honor. Then the question arises: who shall carve it? Where the bravest heroes of Ireland <note>original: "Erinn"</note> are gathered together, the deeds of which they can boast must decide. After a short exchange of words between individual champions of the two hostile provinces, Cet mac Matach (Magach in manuscripts H and M) of Connacht steps forward, sits down by the pig with a knife in his hand, and challenges the men of Ireland to contest this place with him. Now the most proven heroes of Ulster rise one after another, but Cet is able to remind each one of a defeat that either the opponent himself, or his father, or his brother has suffered at Cet's hands. "He thus brought insult upon the entire province." Just as he is about to prepare for the carving, Conall Cernach, a chief hero of Ulster, arrives and leaps into the hall. The two heroes greet each other in short rhapsodies <note>stylized, poetic speeches common in Old Irish literature</note>. But then Conall commands Cet to step back. Cet must acknowledge Conall's superiority, but says that Anluan would take up the contest with Conall if he were in the house. At that, Conall pulls Anluan's head out of his pouch and throws it at Cet's chest, so that a stream of blood broke from his mouth. No one dares to stand against Conall. The latter takes the tail of the pig in his mouth and carves: the men of Connacht receive nothing more than the two forefeet. Over this insult, a bloody armed struggle arises, first inside the house but soon in the open air, in which Connacht gets the worst of it. Mac Dáthó releases his dog, and it takes the side of Ulster. The dog attacks the chariot of Ailill and Medb,

<summary>This page summarizes the climax of the Irish saga "The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig," detailing the verbal and physical contest between the heroes of Ulster and Connacht over the "champion's portion." It describes the triumph of Conall Cernach and the ensuing battle triggered by the unequal distribution of the meat.</summary>

<header>III. The Story of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.</header>
<page-num>95</page-num>

...but is killed by the charioteer, so that the plain was named *Mag Ailbe* <note>original: "Plain of Ailbe," named after the hound Ailbe</note> after the dog. The battle stretches far and wide; all the places it touches are enumerated. Yet Connacht also has its triumph: *Fer Loga*, the charioteer of *Ailill*, had hidden himself in the heather and leaped onto the chariot behind Conchobar, so that the latter was in his power. Conchobar must ransom himself through concessions that were humiliating for Ulster.

The verses at the end (Chapter 21) refer to these same events, but contain names that are not mentioned in the version of the saga available to us. In addition, there are twelve verses in manuscript H <note>H refers to the Trinity College Dublin manuscript H.3.18</note> regarding Mesroeda's pig (cf. Chapter 5).

### 3. Editorial Principles

Regarding the abbreviations in the manuscript, I have proceeded here according to the principles set forth on page 66. The sign that looks almost like a "3" consistently has the value *us* in this narrative in manuscript L <note>L refers to the Book of Leinster</note>; therefore, I have not marked my expansion in words like *beus* <note>still/further</note>, *chetumus* <note>firstly</note>, and so on.

The expansion of abbreviated spellings has been performed silently in the following words: *Connacht*, *Ulaid* <note>Ulster</note>, *Eriu* <note>Ireland</note>, *Conchobar*, *Ailill*, *acht* <note>but</note>, *ocus* <note>and</note>, *for* <note>upon</note>, *mac* <note>son</note>, *iarum* <note>afterwards</note>, *chucum* <note>towards me</note>, *immum* <note>about me</note>, and in the forms of the root *ber* <note>to carry/bear</note>. The Genitive Plural *Connacht* is written out in full in Chapters 4 and 19; Accusative Plural *Connachta* in Chapters 2 and 19; Genitive Plural *Ulad* in Chapter 12; Dative Plural *Ultaib* in Chapters 9 and 13; Accusative Plural *Ulto* in Chapter 9; Vocative Plural *Ulto* in Chapter 12; Dative *do Ailill* in Chapter 4; *iarum* is written out in Chapters 5, 6, 17; and *immum* in Chapters 11 and 20.

The particle *dana* <note>moreover/also</note> is written in full (*plene*) in Chapter 17 in manuscript M; the form *dna* is found in Chapters 2 and 19 in manuscript H. — Nominative Singular *mec* in Chapter 12, Genitive Singular *mec* in Chapter 1 in manuscript H.

# III The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.

### Here begins the Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.

<note>The editor notes: Small print in the text indicates words blurred in the Book of Leinster manuscript, which were reconstructed using other manuscripts. Asterisks mark difficult words or passages discussed in the appendix.</note>

1. There was a wondrous king over the men of Leinster, named Mac Dáthó <note>literally "Son of Two Silent Ones"</note>. He possessed a dog that used to protect all of Leinster. Ailbe was the dog's name, and all Ireland was full of his fame. Messengers came from Ailill and from Medb <note>the King and Queen of Connacht</note> to beg for the dog. At the very same hour, messengers
<margin>5</margin> from Conchobar mac Nessa <note>King of Ulster</note> also arrived to beg for the same dog. They were all made welcome and were brought to him in the hostel. That was one of the six* hostels <note><term>bruiden</term>: a great hall of ritual hospitality</note> *that were in Ireland at that time*: namely, the hostel of Da Derga in the territory of Cualu, the hostel of Forgall Monach, the hostel of Mac Dareo in *Brefne*, the hostel of Da Choca in
<margin>10</margin> *western Meath*, and the hostel of Blai the Landowner in Ulster. There were seven doors in that hostel, seven paths through it, seven hearths within it, and seven cauldrons; there was an ox and a salted pig in every cauldron. The man who came along the path would thrust *the flesh-fork into the* cauldron, and *whatever he brought up* at the first draw, that is what he ate. If
<margin>15</margin> he got nothing at the first try, *he got no second chance.*

2. The *various* messengers were then brought to him to be entertained before the feast. They delivered their message: "We have come to ask for the dog on behalf of Ailill and Medb," said the messengers of Connacht, "and sixty hundred milch cows shall be given *immediately*, along with
<margin>20</margin> a chariot and the two best horses in Connacht, and the same again <note>at a later date</note>."

“...and they shall have drink and food, and they shall take the dog, and they are welcome.” The messengers of Connacht <note>The western province of Ireland</note> were thankful for that answer.

He went then to the messengers of Ulster <note>The northern province of Ireland</note>. “I have been in doubt,” said he, “but I have given the dog to Conchobar <note>The King of Ulster</note>, and he shall be proud of it. <margin>5</margin> Let the nobles of Ulster come for it. They shall take gifts, and they shall have a welcome.” The messengers of Ulster were thankful.

5. On the same day, however, they were all appointed to come, both from the East and from the West. Nor was it neglected by them; the two provinces of Ireland came on the same day until they were at the door of Mac Dáthó’s hostel <note>A *bruiden*, or Great Hall, known for hospitality</note>. <margin>10</margin> He himself comes out and bids them welcome. “We did not expect you, O warriors, but nevertheless, you are welcome. Come into the enclosure.” They all went then into the hostel, half of the house for the men of Connacht and the other half for the men of Ulster. The house was not small: there were seven <margin>15</margin> doors in it and fifty beds between every two doors. However, those were not the faces of friends at a feast who were in that house. Many of them had been at war with one another. For three hundred years before the birth of Christ, there had been war between them. Then Mac Dáthó’s pig was killed for them. For seven <margin>20</margin> years, sixty milch cows <note>term: *gamnach* — a cow that has not calved for a year but is still giving milk</note> had been feeding it. It had been fed on venom, moreover, so that the slaughter of the men of Ireland might be laid around it.

6. Then the pig was brought to them, and forty oxen as a side-dish along with the rest of the food. Mac Dáthó himself was acting as the steward <note>term: *ferdaigsecht* — the office of the house-master or distributor of food</note>. “Welcome to you,” said he, “there is nothing like it to be found among the pigs and cattle of Leinster...”

He has pigs among the men of Leinster. Because of that, one shall be slaughtered for you tomorrow.” “The pig is good,” said **Conchobar** <note>King of Ulster</note>. “It is good indeed,” said **Ailill** <note>King of Connacht</note>. “How shall the pig be divided, O Conchobar?” asked Ailill. “How else,” said **Bricriu son of Carbad** from above <note>Bricriu is the famous troublemaker of Irish mythology; he is likely sitting in a high gallery or "imda"</note>, “in the place where the warriors of the men of Ireland are gathered, but that it be divided according to deeds of valor and trophies of battle? 7. “Your lads will be needed tonight, Conchobar,” said **Senlaech Arad** from the Luachair of Conalad in the west. “Often was a fat ox of yours left with me, and the roads of Luachair Dedad <note>A mountainous region in Munster</note> were under its backside <note>A colloquial way of saying the cattle were driven off as plunder</note>.” “It was a fatter ox you left with us,” <insert>said Munremur son of Gerginn</insert>, “namely your own brother, **Cruachniu son of Ruadlum** from Cruachna Conalad.” “He was no better,” said **Lugaid son of Cú Roí**, “than the great **Loth son of Fergus** son of Lete, who was left by **Echbel son of Dedad** at Tara Luachra.” “What do you think of this, then?” asked **Celtchair son of Uthechar**, “that I myself killed **Conganchness son of Dedad** and struck the head from him?”

8. It happened at last, however, that one man prevailed over the men of Ireland: **Cet son of Mágu** <note>original: "Cet mac Matach"</note>. He raised his weapons high above the weapons of the host, took a knife in his hand, and sat down by the pig. “Let there be found now,” said he, “among the men of Ireland, one to stay me in combat, or else leave the dividing of the pig to me!”

### III The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig

9. This cast the men of Ulster into silence. "You see this, Loegaire?" said Conchobar <note>The High King of Ulster</note>. "It is not right," said Loegaire, "for Cet to divide the pig before our very faces." "Wait a little, Loegaire, that I may speak to you," said Cet. "It is a custom among you Ulstermen," said Cet, "that every youth <margin>5</margin> who takes up arms among you makes us his first target <note>original: "cend a ḃáire" - literally 'the end of his goal-run', a metaphor from the game of hurling</note>. You, too, came into the borderlands, and we met there: you left behind your wheel, your chariot, and your horses, and you fled yourself with a spear through you. You will not get the pig in that manner." Loegaire sat down.

10. "It is not right," said a tall, fair warrior, stepping out from his chamber, "that Cet should divide the pig before our faces." "Who is <margin>10</margin> this?" asked Cet. "A better warrior than you," everyone said, "that is Oengus, son of Hand-Withered <note>Lám Gabaid</note> of the Ulstermen." "Why is your father called Hand-Withered?" asked Cet. "Why indeed?" "I know why," said Cet. "I went eastward once. A cry was raised around me; everyone reached me, <margin>15</margin> and Hand-Withered reached me too. He threw a great spear at me. I threw that same spear back at him, and it struck his hand off, so that it lay on the ground. What would bring his son to contend with me?" Oengus went to his seat.

11. "The contest continues still," said Cet, "or else the pig is mine to divide." "It is not right that you should divide it first," said <margin>20</margin> a tall, fair warrior of the Ulstermen. "Who is this?" asked Cet. "That is Eogan, son of Durthacht," everyone said, "the King of Fernmag." "I have seen him before," said Cet. "In what place did you see me?" asked Eogan. "At the door of your house, when I was taking a cattle-spoil from you. A cry was raised around me...

12. "Attend to the contest further, O men of Ulster!" said Cet. "You shall not divide it <note>The pig/the champion's portion</note> now," said Munremur <term>Munremur: "Thick-Neck"</term> son of Gergeu. "Is this Munremur?" asked Cet. "What then," said Cet, "the son of the peasants with the nicknames comes to contend with me? For it was from me your father received that name; for it was I who struck his heel off him with my sword, so that he carried away only one foot from me. What would bring the son of the one-footed man to me?" He sat down then.

...spear at me. I hurled another spear at you, which went through your thigh and through the upper part of your testicles. What brings you here to challenge me?" *That one then sat down.*

<margin>5</margin> 14. "The contest continues!" said Cet. "You shall have it," said Cúscraid the Stammerer of Macha, the son of Conchobar. "Who is this?" asked Cet. "Cúscraid," everyone said, "he has the appearance of a king." "No thanks to you," said the lad. "Well," said Cet, "You came to us for your first feat of arms, young man. We met at the border; <margin>10</margin> you left a third of your followers behind, and you left with a spear through your throat so that you cannot speak a word in a proper way, for the spear destroyed the tendons of your throat. That is why you have been called Cúscraid the Stammerer ever since." In that manner, he cast shame upon the entire province <note>referring to the province of Ulster</note>.

<margin>15</margin> 15. Just as he was flourishing his knife in his hand to carve the pig, they saw Conall Cernach <note>Conall the Victorious, one of Ulster's greatest heroes</note> enter the house. He leaped into the middle of the floor. The men of Ulster gave a great shout of welcome to Conall. Then Conchobar <note>The King of Ulster</note> took the hood from his head and shook it in greeting. "We are pleased that our portion is ready," said <margin>20</margin> Conall. "Who is dividing it for you?" "It has been granted to the man who is dividing it," said Conchobar, "namely, Cet mac Mágach." "Is it true, Cet," said Conall, "that you are the one to divide the pig?" Then Cet spoke:

> [*Poem*] Welcome, Conall! Heart of stone.
> Fierce glow of fire. Glitter of ice.
> <margin>25</margin> Blood-red strength of anger. Under the breast of a hero.
> Wound-inflictor, battle-victor, I see the son of Findchoem before me.

16. “Get away from the pig then!” said Conall. “But why should he give it to you?” said Cet. “It is true,” said Conall, “to seek a contest <term>comram: a formal competition of valorous deeds to claim the best cut of meat</term> from me. I will give you one contest, Cet,” <margin>15</margin>
said Conall. “I swear what my people swear: since I first took a spear in my hand, I have not often been without the head of a Connachtman under my head while sleeping, and I have not been without wounding a man every single day and every single night.”
“It is true,” said Cet, “you are a better warrior than I. If Anlúan were <margin>20</margin>
in the house, he would offer you yet another contest. It is a pity for us that he is not in the house.” “He is, however!” said Conall, taking the head of Anlúan from his belt; he flung it at Cet’s chest so that a spout of blood broke over his lips. Cet then moved away from <margin>25</margin>
the pig, and Conall sat down by it.

### III The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig

17. “Let them come to the contest now!” said Conall. Not a single warrior was found among the men of Connacht <note>The western kingdom of Ireland, rivals to the Ulstermen</note> to stand against him. However, a screen of bucklers was held up in a circle around him, because there was a bad custom in the house of evil people throwing missiles unfairly. Then Conall went <margin>5</margin> to divide the pig, and he took the end of the belly-piece in his mouth until he had finished dividing the pig. He sucked up the belly—which was a load for nine men—so that he did not leave a single bit of it.

18. He did not give the men of Connacht anything, however, except the two forelegs of the pig. The men of Connacht thought their share was small. <margin>10</margin> They rose up, and the men of Ulster <note>The northern kingdom</note> rose up as well, until each reached the other. There was many a blow over the ear in that place, until the heap of corpses on the floor of the house was as high as the side-wall of the house, and the streams of blood reached the doors. Then the hosts broke through the doors and raised a great shout so that a mill-wheel <note>original: *mol*</note> could have turned on the blood on the floor of the courtyard, <margin>15</margin> as each man was striking the other. It was then that Fergus <note>Fergus mac Róich, a famous hero of the Ulster Cycle</note> pulled a great oak tree that was in the middle of the courtyard up by its roots. Then they broke out of the courtyard. The battle was joined in front of the courtyard gate.

19. Then Mac Dáthó went out with the hound in his hand to let it loose between them, to see which side it would choose—that is, the hound's instinct. <margin>20</margin> The hound chose the Ulstermen and set to the slaughter of the men of Connacht, for the men of Connacht had been defeated. They say it was in the plains of Ailbe that—

### III The Story of Mac Dáthó’s Pig

<column-break/>

The hound <note>Ailbe, the famous guard dog of Mac Dáthó</note> seized the pole of the chariot belonging to Ailill and Medb <note>The King and Queen of Connacht</note>. It was then that Ferloga, the charioteer of Ailill and Medb, struck at it, so that its body was cast to one side, while its head remained fixed to the chariot pole. They say, moreover, that it is from this that the Plain of Ailbe <note>Mag Ailbe</note> takes its name, for Ailbe was the name of the hound.

**20.** The rout fled southwards over the Pass of Mugna, over the Ford of Midbine in Mastiu, past Druim Criaig—which is called Kildare today—past Ráith Imgain into the Wood of Gaible, to the Ford of the Sons of Lugna, past the Ridge of the Two Plains, and onto the Bridge of Cairpre. At the Ford of the Dog's Head in Bile, that is where the dog’s head fell from the chariot.

As they were traveling westward through the heather of Meath, Ferloga—Ailill’s charioteer—hid himself in the heather and then leaped into the chariot behind Conchobar <note>The King of Ulster</note>, seizing him by the head from behind.

"Grant me quarter and protection, Conchobar!" he cried.
"Choose your terms," said Conchobar.
"It will not be much," said Ferloga, "namely: that you take me with you to Emain Macha, and that the single women of Ulster and their young maiden daughters sing a choral song <note>original: "cepóc"; a type of celebratory or mocking chant</note> around me every evening, saying: 'Ferloga is my sweetheart.'"

This had to be granted, for they did not dare do otherwise because of Conchobar. A year later, Ferloga was sent back west across the Ford of Luain <note>Athlone</note>, taking with him two of Conchobar’s horses with golden bits.

**21.** Of this it was sung:

O youth of Connacht, I do not deny
your heaviness in telling lies,

[H:] <note>Version from the Harley 5280 manuscript</note>
O youth of Connacht, I do not deny
your heaviness in speaking lies

### III. The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.

Famous was the fine, praised one, as is sounded through the old lore,
<margin>4</margin>without hiding the judgment that won fame: **forty** oxen were its foundation <note>The "foundation" refers to the amount of food required to fatten the pig.</note>.

Witnessed by hands—it was believed a burden—the weight of nine men was in its heavy belly,
though it was divided with great strength and grace, Conall Cernach <note>A legendary hero of the Ulaid (Ulster) known for his prowess.</note> consumed it.

Though Ailbe <note>The famous hound of Mac Dáthó.</note> went about in pure love, the dog for whom every contest began,
<margin>8</margin>though he warded off attacks from the place, the great pig was no less a marvel.

They came for fierce iron-deeds, the five provinces of Ireland;
their contention brought them to it; it was no small gathering for a single pig.

Mesráeda and Mesgedra the noble, the two sons of Dáthó of the heavy companies;
<margin>12</margin>it was Mesráeda, with skillful care, who raised the great pig.

<meta>catchword: Pig.</meta>

# Appendix I.

## The Harleian Manuscript.

The following points are not consistently taken into account in the *Communication of the Variant Readings* <note>original: "Mittheilung der Varia Lectio"</note>:

1. *Voiced stops instead of voiceless*: <term>go</term> (to), <term>tugad</term> (was given), <term>codolta</term> (of sleep), <term>cudal</term> (shame), <term>ro fedur sa</term> (I know); <term>doid</term> (for <term>duit</term>, to you), <term>rod bia</term> (it shall be to you <note>original Latin: "tibi erit"</note>), <term>uaid</term> (from you <note>original Latin: "a te"</note>), <term>ad</term> (you are <note>original Latin: "du bist"</note>); <term>bragaid</term> (neck); <term>anaid</term> (stays), <term>tongad</term> (swears), <term>asperad</term> (would say), 3rd *Person Plural.*

2. *Aspirated voiced stops and aspirated m*: <term>eghtir</term> (outside), <term>amoigh</term> (out there), <term>braghid</term> (neck); <term>turbaidh</term> (disturbance), <term>modh</term> (manner), <term>codlodh</term> (sleep), <term>codhaise</term> (you have come <note>original Latin: "venisti"</note>), <term>maidhid</term> (breaks); <term>gabhail</term> (taking), <term>fri mnaibh</term> (with women); <term>a mhenmo</term> (his mind), <term>nemh</term> (heaven), <term>animh</term> (soul), <term>daomh</term> (to me <note>original Latin: "mihi"</note>), <term>comramha</term> (triumphs).

3. *gh for dh*: <term>maidigh in sluag</term> (the host breaks).

4. *gh for ch*: <term>seght</term> (seven), <term>teghtoi</term> (they come), <term>laegh</term> (calf); <term>co tegta</term> (so that they come), <term>laeg</term> (calf).

5. *c for g*: <term>cuincid</term> (seeks), <term>Forcaill</term>, <term>eirc</term> (go!), <term>farcoib</term> (left). *But also cunchid (Chapter 18).*

6. *d for th*: <term>adair</term> (Father).

7. *p for b*: <term>diplinaib</term> (in both), <term>oc tapairt</term> (at giving), <term>asperat</term> (they say). *In Chapter 4 in one sentence: comtabairt, comtapairt and cunntaŭairt. Conversely, always carbat (chariot).*

8. *ŭ for b*: <term>nir ŭo</term> (was not).

9. *ld for ll*: <term>araild</term> (another).

10. *ea and eu for e*: <term>ithead</term> (eating); <term>seuch</term> (past), <term>fri neuch</term> (with anyone).

11. *iu for e*: <term>biuc</term> (small, Chapter 8), <term>no tegiud</term> (they used to go), <term>ro hegiud</term> (it was cried out), <term>nisailiud</term> (they are not <note>original Latin: "non sunt"</note>), <term>oincoisiuda</term> (one-footed, *Genitive*); <term>hi fid n-Gaibliu</term> (in the wood of Gaibhle). *Additionally frium alongside frim (with me).*

# III. The Tale of Mac Dáthó’s Pig.

12. **au** for **a**: *laur* <note>enough</note>, *cauch* <note>blind</note>; *also sounds* <note>original: "auch laut"</note> (*with you, Chapter* 2), *daum* (*to me, Chapter* 8), *daomh* (*Chapter* 7).

13. **oi** for **ai**: *eroili* <note>another</note>, *amoigh* <note>outside</note>, *tarloic* <note>he threw</note>, *do roind* <note>to divide</note>; *atoi* (2nd Person Singular).

14. **oi** and **ai** *in the final syllable for simple vowels*: *da cocoi*, *techtoi*; *doratusai*, *ascetai*, *etorrai*.

15. **ie** for **ia**: *iersin* <note>after that</note>, *cid dieta* <note>what is it for</note>, *tiestair* <note>he should go</note>.

16. **o** *in the last syllable*: *cetno* <note>same</note>, *gillo* <note>lad</note>; *ro tanoc sa* (1st Person Singular), *acco* (2nd Person Singular), *ni dabor* <note>is not given</note> (Chapter 6); *Conoll*.

17. Isolated **ao** for **oi**, **oe**, **ae**: *laoch* <note>warrior</note>; **io** for **i**: *crios* <note>belt</note>.

18. *Predominantly* **i** *for* **e** *in the final syllable*: *lathi* (*Nominative Singular*), *failti* (*Nominative*), *tuili* (*Genitive*), *cori* (*Nominative Singular/Plural*), *araili* (*Accusative Singular*), *mathi* (*Genitive Plural*); *nis raindfi* (2nd Person Singular).

19. **e** for **i**: *sen* (*this* <note>original: "hic"</note>), *es* (*is* <note>original: "est"</note>); *likewise* *mec* (*Genitive Singular*).

20. *For* **chuci-sium** <note>to him</note>: *cucesim*, *-sem*; *ol esem* (*he said*); *for* **do** *possessive pronoun*: *di*, *de* (*from his head, from his head* <note>original: "as di cinn, as de chinn"</note> Chapter 11); *for* **duit** (*to you* <note>original: "tibi"</note>): *det* *several times. Likewise* **di** (*preposition*) *for* **do** *in the dative*: *di Chonnachtaib* <note>to the Connartmen</note> (*e.g., Chapter* 18). *Furthermore* *diradat* (Chapter 17), *deradusa* (Chapter 4); **di** *and* **do** *are simply used interchangeably.*

21. *In inflection*: *tecait* for *tecat* <note>they come</note>, *ataid* for *atát* <note>they are</note>, *lecad* for *lecud* <note>letting</note>; *Erind* (*Genitive*); *do airec*, *do celi* Dative; *a Ulta* Vocative <note>O Men of Ulster</note>.

22. *The article is predominantly* **an**: *on* for *ond*, *for an* for *for sin*; *and lis* Genitive in Chapter 18.

23. *The preposition* **i(n)** <note>in</note> *is predominantly* **a(n)**, **and**: *am láim* (*in my hand*), *and Erind*. *Likewise* *atat* for *itaat* (Chapter 6), *amach* for *immach* <note>outwards</note> (Chapter 18).

24. *For* **said** <note>original: "inquit"</note> *predominantly* **ol** (*about 40 times*);— *the forms* **or**, **ar**, **al** *together about 10 times*.

## Appendix II.

->Remarks on some difficult passages.<-

Chapter 3. Verse 3. *The difficulty lies in* **a sói** (*a sui* in Manuscript M.), *for* **dosói uaim fri fraig** *will mean: "he turns away from me toward the wall."*

Verse 4. *Regarding* **dos beir mod** <note>he gives heed/work</note> *compare*: *there is a face that is pleasing to him, he gives heed to the women-folk* <note>original: "do fil gnuis as grato do, dobeir mod don bancured"</note> *Lebor na hUidre* 55^b, 38 = *there is a lovely face for him, he gives heed to the women-folk* <note>original: "ro fail gnuis n-grátam dó, dober mod don banchuiriu"</note> *Book of Leinster folio* 42. b (*Táin Bó Cúailnge*).

### III The Story of Mac Dáthó’s Pig

V. 6. Perhaps, taking into account the readings of manuscripts *M* and *H*, one should write: *main ar mug ni aithenar* “A precious object is not entrusted to a slave”? <note>original: "main ar mug ni aithenar"</note> Compare *aithnir* "it is committed" or "is given in charge" in O’Donovan’s Supplement to O’Reilly’s Dictionary.

V. 7. 8. The words *airi* and *aili* must be restored in the rhyme.

V. 10. The second half has one syllable too few. Perhaps it originally read: *bid lia turim lín a chath* “the number of battles for him will be greater than can be told.” <note>original: "bid lia turim lín a chath"</note> Compare the note on *Hymns* 5, 18.

V. 11. In the second half, the word *is* should be deleted for the sake of the meter (it is missing in manuscript *H*).

V. 12. “His hosts will leave nothing more of cattle or of land.”

V. 13. In the first half it must read: *Mada do Ailill era* “if there is a refusal to Ailill.” In the second half, *silis* (“havoc, destruction” according to O’Reilly) should probably be adopted from manuscripts *H* and *M* instead of *leis*, but what is *falmag*? <note>The editor is questioning a specific term in the Middle Irish text.</note>

V. 14. In the second half, the phrase *ata neblai* is particularly incomprehensible to me.

V. 17. For *doberi*, one should write *dobir* or *dobeir*.

V. 18. The first half is corrupt. <note>In philology, "corrupt" means the text is so damaged or altered by scribal error that the original meaning is lost.</note>

Chapter 4. O’Curry must have misunderstood the text here, for he says in *Lectures on the Manuscript Materials, p. 486*: “Mac Dáthó told the messengers of the two kings respectively that he had already promised the hound to the master of the other.” Likewise in *On the Manners and Customs, p. 372*. However, Ailill is the King of Connacht, and Conchobar is the King of Ulster; Mac Dáthó promises the messengers of Connacht that he will give the dog to Ailill, and the messengers of Ulster that he will give the dog to Conchobar. <note>Mac Dáthó is playing both sides against each other to avoid conflict, a central plot point of the saga.</note>

Chapter 6. Instead of *dorat cách buille díb*, it should probably be written as in manuscript *H*: *dorat cách díb buille* (“each of them gave a blow”).

Chapter 8. *Fagabar . . do feraib hErend tairismi comrama frimsa no lécud nammucci do raind dam.* In this alternative, the second subject of the verb *fagabar* (“let there be found”) is the infinitive *lécud* (“the allowing”); the first subject is missing, and the sentence cannot be constructed unless *tairisem* (“staying/withstanding”) is written. Compare: “the withstanding of the contest still . . or the allowing me to divide the pig” in Chapter 11.

Chapter 11. *Messe tall in t-śúil aile as do chind* “I shall strike the other eye out of your head.” In manuscript *L*, the object is in the nominative case, while *H* correctly uses the accusative. Likewise: “for it was from me that your father received that name” in Chapter 12.

Chapter 20. *Beir būide etc.* O’Curry translates (*On the Manners and Customs III, p. 372*): “Ransom thy head, O Conchobar!” said he. “Thou...

### III. The Story of Mac Dáthó’s Pig

"Thou shalt have thy demand," said *Conchobar*. "It is not great, indeed," said *Ferloga*; "it is only that thou shalt take me along with you to *Emain Macha* <note>The royal capital of Ulster, also known as Emania</note>, and that the young women and girls of Ulster shall sing a *Cepóc* <note>A choral song or chant of praise</note> around me every evening, and each of them say: *Ferloga is my favourite*." — To these rather fanciful conditions King *Conchobar* was obliged to submit. The charioteer was brought to *Emania*; and in twelve months' time (the story tells us) he was conveyed over the river *Shannon* at *Athlone*, with a present to the king and queen of *Connacht* of King *Conchobar's* two favourite steeds with their golden bridles; but we are told nothing more of his relations with the maidens of *Ulster*.

Chapter 22. Regarding the pig of Mac Dáthó, the following information is found in the topographical work *Dinnseanchus* <note>The "Lore of Places," a collection of legends explaining Irish place-names</note> (according to O'Curry, *The Battle of Magh Lena*, p. 14—16, note):

From what is *Magh Léna* <note>The Plain of Léna</note> named? Not difficult. Léna, son of Mesroda, it was he who reared the pig of Mac Dáthó; he found it in the Oak-wood of the Piglet in the east of *Bladhma* <note>The Slieve Bloom Mountains</note>. It flourished with him until the end of seven years, so that there were nine inches of fat on its snout. The men of Ulster and the men of *Olnegmacht* <note>An ancient name for the province of Connacht</note> went to the feast of Mac Dáthó. His mother—that is, the wife of Mac Dáthó—came to him to ask for the pig to help protect his honor; for the five provinces of Ireland were at the feast, and she offered fifty chosen boars in its place, but he did not accept them from her. Sixty strippers <note>term: gamhnach; a cow that has not calved but is still giving milk</note> in a grass field were used to feed it, forty oxen were needed to move it, and the load of twenty-seven men was in its belly, until Conall Cearnach <note>A famous Ulster hero</note> consumed it while he was dividing it. Mesgéadhra and Mesroda were the two sons of Mac Dáthó. It was by Mesroda that the pig was reared. Then Léna, son of Mesroda, went with his pig to a dark trench on the night just before it was to be delivered. He slept near where it was, and the pig rooted up the trench over him without him noticing, so that it smothered him; however, he managed to strike out while sitting up so that the point of his sword reached the pig and it was killed. And Follscaidi, the swineherd of Mac Dáthó, went and brought the pig to the feast. So the burial mound of Léna was made there. From this, *Magh Léna* is named.

<summary>This page concludes the humorous ransom of King Conchobar by the charioteer Ferloga and provides a mythological origin story for the Plain of Léna, tied to the rearing and death of Mac Dáthó's legendary giant pig.</summary>
