### IV.
# The Courtship of Etain.
->(The Courtship of Etain.)<-

---

1. The text of the following narrative is published here for the first time—apart from the facsimile of the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre," a famous 12th-century Irish manuscript</note>. The following manuscripts have been utilized:
   $^1$) Egerton Manuscript 1782 in the British Museum, p. 205, a, designated as **Eg.**;
   $^2$) *Book of the Dun Cow* in the possession of the Royal Irish Academy in Dublin (written circa 1100), p. 129, designated as **LU.**
   Mr. O’Grady gave me an exact copy from the Egerton Manuscript as a gift; I was able to collate it with the original in 1874. I have used the *Book of the Dun Cow* in the facsimile published by the Royal Irish Academy (Dublin 1870).

2. Both texts are printed in full, as their versions are very different. The text of **LU.** is much shorter than that of **Eg.** up to Chapter 11. In general, it can often be observed that texts in younger manuscripts have been expanded and embellished. However, the question remains whether the first parts of this legend were always told in the rather bare form in which they appear in **LU.** during older times. For instance, the precise description of Étáin’s beauty (Chapters 3 and 4), which is missing in **LU.**, appears in almost the same words at the beginning of the saga titled *The Destruction of Da Derga's Hostel* <note>original: "Togail Bruidne Dá Derga," a central tale in the Ulster Cycle of Irish mythology</note>

<summary>This page introduces the editorial methods and manuscript sources (Egerton 1782 and Lebor na hUidre) used to reconstruct the Old Irish myth "The Courtship of Etain." The author notes the stylistic differences between the manuscripts, specifically how later versions often added descriptive flourishes regarding the heroine's beauty.</summary>

<keywords>The Courtship of Etain, Lebor na hUidre, Book of the Dun Cow, Egerton Manuscript 1782, O’Grady, Royal Irish Academy, Togail Bruidne Dá Derga, Étáin, Irish mythology, manuscript collation</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly analysis of the Irish saga "The Wooing of Etain" (Tochmarc Étaíne), focusing on the structural consistency of the narrative and the introduction of the plot.</meta>

# IV The Wooing of Etain.

One might indeed suspect that this piece was only added to our saga at a later date. However, if we consider that *Etain's* beauty had become proverbial <note>See Chapter 5 of this volume</note>, we will not be surprised that the description of her beauty had taken on a fixed form, which we encounter in various sagas where *Etain's* beauty plays a role.

The situation is different with the poems inserted in Chapters 9 and 10. While these undoubtedly belong only in this specific place, they certainly would not have formed an integral part of the narrative from the very beginning <note>Compare page 63</note>. In any case, the first poem (Chapter 9) deviates from the prose narrative in that verse 26 names not *Eochaid Airem*, but rather his brother *Eochaid Fedlech* <note>See Chapter 6</note> as the husband of *Etain*. This is a variation in tradition that we do encounter elsewhere <note>Compare O'Curry, *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume II, page 192 and Volume III, page 190</note>, but which we would not expect to find within one and the same text.

3. The content of the narrative is briefly as follows:
The King of Ireland <note>original: "Erinn"</note>, *Eochaid Airem*, invites the kings of the provinces to the Festival of *Tara* in the first year after ascending the throne. They refuse to appear, however, because the king is unmarried. *Eochaid* decides to choose a queen for himself, and his messengers soon find a woman worthy of the king. This is *Etain*, the daughter of *Etar*, from the *Side* <term>Side: the fairy folk or people of the mounds in Irish mythology</term>. *Eochaid* is led to her, and they quickly reach an understanding, as *Etain* has always loved him and has been waiting for him. He brings her home, and the Festival of *Tara* is held. At the festival, *Ailill*, a brother of *Eochaid*, conceives a violent passion for *Etain* which he cannot overcome. He falls ill and is brought to *Dun Fremain*, but no one understands his suffering until *Eochaid* sends his physician, who declares that only love or jealousy could be the cause of it. *Ailill*

---

<margin>\* See O'Curry's *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume III, page 189.</margin>

<summary>This page discusses the literary composition of the saga "The Wooing of Etain," noting discrepancies between its poetic and prose sections regarding Etain's husband, and begins a summary of the plot involving King Eochaid and his brother Ailill.</summary>

# IV The Wooing of Étáin. 115

is ashamed to confess what is consuming him. His death seems certain to everyone, and Eochaid, who as King must undertake a circuit through Ireland <note>original: "Erinn"</note>, asks Étáin to care for his brother as long as he lives, and to bury him worthily when he has died. Étáin comes daily into the house where he was staying to nurse him. One day she speaks earnestly with him about his illness and finally learns from him the reason for it. In the *Egerton* manuscript <note>original: "Eg." refers to Egerton 1782, a significant Irish manuscript</note> this dialogue is in metrical form; likewise, only in *Egerton* is the poem found in which Étáin promises Ailill the fulfillment of his desires. Ailill recovers quickly, and they arrange a tryst. Étáin arrives at the appointed time and soon beholds a man looking like Ailill, but weak and sickly. She waits in vain for the real Ailill. He had fallen into a deep sleep and only awoke after the time of the tryst had passed. Death was dearer to him than life. Étáin puts him off until another day, but her intention is thwarted on the second and third day in the same manner. At last, however, the man who appeared to Étáin in Ailill’s form reveals himself as Mider, King of the People of the Mounds <note>original: "Side" or Sidhe, the supernatural race of Irish mythology</note> of Brig ’Léith, and as her former husband to whom she belonged when she still dwelt in the realm of the Mounds. Étáin seems unable to recall this relationship, for she rejects Mider’s invitation to come with him, as she does not wish to give up the King of Ireland for a man whose lineage is unknown to her. Mider further reveals to her that it was he who stirred Ailill’s love for her, but also he who ultimately preserved her honor through his intervention. Ailill is cured of his passion, and Eochaid thanks Étáin upon his return for what she has done for his brother.

4. The specific title of this tale is *The Wasting Sickness of Ailill* <note>original: "Serglige Ailella"</note> (see Chapter 20); for the title *The Wooing of Étáin* <note>original: "Tochmarc Étáine"</note> does not belong to it alone, but also to several other legends, fragments of which are likewise found in the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre," the oldest surviving manuscript in Irish</note> (perhaps in part from the lost—

# IV The Wooing of Étain <note>original: "Tochmarc Etáine"</note>

transcribed from the lost *Book of Druim Snechta* <note>original: "Lebor Dromma Snechta," an ancient lost manuscript cited as a source in later medieval Irish texts</note>, see *Lebor na hUidre* <note>The Book of the Dun Cow, the oldest surviving manuscript in Irish</note> page 128a, 1 and 132a, 6). One legend refers to Mider’s earlier relationship with Étain (*LU* p. 129a), another to Étain’s abduction by Mider and to Eochaid’s expedition to recover her (*LU* p. 130b—132). The latter legend is recounted by O’Curry, *On the Manners and Customs II, p. 192—194* (cf. III, 190) according to a more complete source. O’Curry names in *Lectures on the Manuscript Materials p. 585* the *Yellow Book of Lecan* and the *Paper Manuscript H. 1. 13. in Trinity College Dublin* as the manuscripts which contain the legend *The Wooing of Étain*.

A short but complete version, in which Étain’s abduction by Mider is told differently than in the *Book of the Dun Cow*, is also found in the *Egerton Manuscript* <note>Egerton 1782 in the British Library</note>, and I share this one here. Mider says here that he dwells in the East, at the rising of the sun. The magic of the druid Dalán is also of interest.

5. We should perhaps speak here of myth rather than legend, for Mider and the realm of the *Side* <note>The People of the Mounds or Fairies; the supernatural race of Irish mythology</note> belong to Irish mythology. To deal with this more thoroughly is not the place here (see the indices to O’Curry’s Lectures, and my remarks in the *Contributions to Comparative Philology VIII, 246*). It is noteworthy that the dwellings of the *Side* were imagined to be located in or under hills.

According to Irish tradition, these events are said to have taken place at the end of the second or beginning of the first century before Christ. They therefore precede the time of the *Cattle Raid of Cooley* <note>original: "Táin Bó Cualgne," the central epic of the Ulster Cycle</note>. Eochaid Fedlech, the brother of Eochaid Airem, was the father of the famous Queen Medb of Connacht, the main heroine of the *Táin*. The statements in *Chapter 1* are to be judged accordingly. One must not take the chronology too strictly if Medb is supposed to have already been Queen of Connacht during Eochaid’s time. In other cases, too, a tendency can be observed to relate the old legends to the *Táin* in some way.

On the other hand, Eochaid Airem and Étain were the grandparents of King Conaire Mór (see *Chapter 20*), whose murder—

as is told in the already mentioned, highly interesting saga of the Destruction of Da Derga's Hostel <note>original: "Bruden Dá Derga"</note> (see O'Curry, Lectures on the Manuscript Materials, p. 258—260).

6. Finally, regarding the abbreviations in the manuscript, I have left them unmarked in the following cases: 1) in the nominative, accusative, and dative singular of the name Ailill, 2) in the conjunction *and* <note>original: "ocus"</note> (which is often written out in full in the Egerton manuscript), 3) in the noun *son* <note>original: "mac"</note> (the genitive *mic* is often written out in full in Egerton), 4) in the preposition *upon* <note>original: "for"</note>, 5) in the first syllable of *Ireland* <note>original: "hErend, hErind"</note> (abbreviated only in Egerton). — For the aspirated *s* <note>ṡ</note>, the spelling *hs* is found several times in Egerton, e.g., *ro hsirsit*, *do hsita*. — If the form of the open *a* (which can be very well distinguished from *u* in the Book of the Dun Cow <note>original: "LU." or "Lebor na hUidre"</note>) exists at all in Egerton, I have at least been unable to distinguish it from *u* in the pieces I have read in Egerton; I have therefore written *Temruch*, and even *argiallsut*. — The particle *then* <note>original: "dano"</note> is written out in full in Chapter 1 in Egerton.

The language of the Egerton Manuscript shows a more recent character in the phonetic composition of the words, but it is clearly based on an archaic text.

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

[Egerton 1782 Manuscript:]
The five provinces of Ireland then submitted to Eochaid Airem <note>The name "Airem" is often interpreted as "the Ploughman."</note>; that is, Conchobar mac Nessa, king of the province of Ulster, gave hostage to him, as did Messgegra, king of Leinster, and Cú Roí mac Dáire, king of the province of Munster, and Ailill and Medb, who possessed the province of Connacht.
<margin>5</margin>Now, Eochaid possessed two primary fortresses: the fortress of Frémainn in Meath and the fortress of Frémainn in Tethba <note>Modern Westmeath and Longford areas.</note>, and it was his fortress in Tethba that was the dearest to him of all his dwellings.

2. In the first year after Eochaid took the kingship, a proclamation was issued throughout Ireland to celebrate the Festival of Tara <note>The "Feis Temro," a great ritual assembly and feast confirming the king's authority.</note>, so that the men of Ireland might come to be inspected by him, and so that their tributes and <margin>10</margin>customs might be known by him.
The men of Ireland gave a single answer to Eochaid: they said they would not go to the Festival of Tara, whether he were to be King of Ireland for a long or a short time, while he lacked a wife worthy of him. For there was no nobleman among the men of Ireland without a wife worthy of him, and there was no king without a queen; for <margin>15</margin>no man without a wife would go to Tara for the festival, nor would a woman go without a husband.
Then Eochaid sent out his couriers and his entertainers <note>original: "obloire," likely jesters or performers.</note> and his road-scourers and his border-messengers throughout Ireland, and they searched all of Ireland seeking a woman...

[Lebor na hUidre Manuscript:]
The five provinces of Ireland submitted to him, that is, a king for every province. These were <margin>20</margin>the kings at that time: Conchobar mac Nessa, Messgegra, Tigernach Tétbannach, Cú Roí, and Ailill mac Máta Muirisci. The fortresses of Eochaid were the fortress of Frémainn in Meath and the fortress of Frémainn in Tethba. Frémainn of Tethba was the most beloved to him of the dwellings of Ireland. <margin>25</margin>

2. A proclamation was made by Eochaid to the men of Ireland to hold the Festival of Tara in the year after taking the kingship, to determine their customs and their tributes for a period of five years.
The men of Ireland had the same response for Eochaid: they would not assemble the Festival of Tara for a king who had no queen with him, for there was no queen <margin>30</margin>at the king's side when he took the sovereignty.
Eochaid sent messengers to every province from him throughout Ireland to...

# IV The Wooing of Étáin

<margin>[Egerton Manuscript:]</margin>
<note>The Egerton 1782 version provides a more detailed physical description of the encounter.</note>
...a match for him in form, beauty, appearance, and lineage. There was, however, another condition he held: that he would never take a woman if another man had known her before him.

3. Then his messengers, his scouts, his road-trackers, and his border-agents went out from him and searched all of Ireland, both south and north, until they found at the Estuary of Cíchmuine <note>Inbir Chichmuine: likely near Larne, Co. Antrim</note> a woman who was his equal: Étáin, daughter of Étar, King of Echraidhe. The messengers then returned to Eochaid and gave him a description of the girl’s form, beauty, and appearance.

Then Eochaid went to fetch the girl, and the path he took was across the Fair-ground of Breg Leth <note>A sidhe or fairy mound near Ardagh, Co. Longford</note>, where he saw the girl at the edge of a spring. She had a clear silver comb adorned with gold, and she was washing herself in a silver basin with four birds of gold perched upon it, and bright little gems of carbuncle <term>carbuncle: a deep red gemstone, often garnet</term> set in the rim of that basin. She wore a purple, fringed cloak of fine fleece, with silver tassels and a brooch of gold in the cloak over her breast. She wore a long, hooded tunic of stiff, smooth green silk with red-gold embroidery, and wonderful animal-shaped clasps of gold and silver at her breasts in the tunic; so that the glitter of the gold against the sun in the green silk was visible to the men. There were two golden-yellow tresses upon her head, each woven in four strands, with a ball of gold at the end of every strand.

4. At that moment, the girl was loosening her hair to wash it, and her two arms were out through the sleeve-openings of her tunic; each of her two arms was whiter than the snow of a single night, and each of her two cheeks was redder than the foxglove of the mountain. Her teeth were even and small in her head, like a brilliant shower of pearls. Her eyes were as blue...

---

<margin>[Lebor na hUidre:]</margin>
<note>The Book of the Dun Cow version offers a more concise summary of the search.</note>
...to seek for him the most beautiful woman to be found in Ireland. For he said that no woman should be in his company except one whom no man of the men of Ireland had known before.

3. She was found for him at the Estuary of Cíchmuine: namely, Étáin daughter of Étar. Eochaid took her then, for she was a match for him in beauty, form, lineage, splendor, youth, and fame.

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

[Egerton Manuscript:]
The luster of the hyacinth <note>original: "bughai"; likely the bluebell or wood hyacinth</note> was in each of her two eyes. She had thin, red lips. Her two shoulders were high and soft-white. Her forearms were soft and smooth-white; her fingers long and quite white; her nails beautiful and pink-hued. Her slender, long, and graceful side was as white as the snow or the foam of the wave.
<margin>5</margin>
Her thighs were smooth and sleek-white. Her two knees were round and firm-white. Her two calves were straight and red-gold. Her feet were thin and pale-skinned. Her two eyes <margin>or her two heels</margin> were of equal beauty; her two eyebrows were beetle-black and dark-blue over her eyes.

5. She was, then, the most fitting and the loveliest girl that the eyes of men had ever seen, and it seemed to them that she must be from the fairy mounds <note>original: "sídib"; the dwellings of the Tuatha Dé Danann</note>. It is of this maiden that it was said: "every shape is beautiful until compared to Étaín," and "everyone is lovely until compared to Étaín." Immediately, a great desire for her seized the King, and he sent a man of his people ahead to hold her there, and the
<margin>15</margin>
King arrived afterward to the maiden and asked news of her. "Where are you from, then, maiden?" said Eochaid, "and where have you come from?" "It is not difficult to say," she replied. "I am Étaín, daughter of the King of Eochraidhe from the fairy mounds." "Shall I have an hour of union with you?" asked Eochaid. "It is for your protection that we have come here," said the maiden. "I have been
<margin>20</margin>
here indeed for twenty years since I was born in the fairy mound, and the men of the mounds, both kings and noblemen, have been seeking me, and yet no marriage with any man of them was obtained from me, because I loved you and gave you my heart's affection since I was a child and able to speak, because of the tales of your excellence and your glory. I have never seen you
<margin>25</margin>
before this, yet I recognized you at once from your description, and it is you we have reached at last," she said. "That shall not be the invitation of a bad friend to you," said Eochaid, "and you shall have a welcome, and every other woman shall be left for you, and I shall be with you alone as long as it is your pleasure." "Give me my proper bride-price," <term>tinnscra: a gift given by a groom to a bride's father or the bride herself</term> said the maiden,
<margin>30</margin>
"and my requirements after that." "You shall have that," said Eochaid. Then, seven female slaves <term>cumala: a standard unit of value in early Ireland, literally 'female slaves'</term> were given to her as her bride-price, and he took her with him then to Tara <note>original: "Temraig"; the seat of the High Kings of Ireland</note>, and a truly fair welcome was made for her there.

---

20 in the fairy mound *Egerton.* 27 *The 'n' of 'sein' is uncertain.*

<summary>King Eochaid encounters the otherworldly Étaín, whose beauty is described in vivid detail, and she reveals she has traveled from the fairy mounds specifically to wed him. They agree on a bride-price, and he brings her to Tara as his sole queen.</summary>

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

<margin>From the Egerton 1782 manuscript:</margin>
6. Now, there were three brothers who were the three sons of Finn: namely, Eochaid Airem, and Eochaid <note>likely Eochaid Fedlech</note>, and Ailill Anglonnach, or "of the Single Blow," so called because the love for his brother’s wife was a single blow to him. It was then that the men of Ireland came to the Feast of <margin>5</margin> Tara, and they remained there for a fortnight before Samhain <note>The festival marking the beginning of winter and the New Year</note> and a fortnight after Samhain. During that time at the Feast of Tara, Ailill Anglonnach fell in love with Étaín, daughter of Étar. Ailill took to gazing constantly at the girl for as long as the Feast of Tara lasted. Then the wife of Ailill, the daughter of Luchta Red-Hand from the territory of Leinster, said: <margin>10</margin> "Well now, Ailill," said she, "why do you gaze into the distance away from you? For long-gazing is a sign of love." Ailill took that reproach to heart and did not look at the girl after that.

7. Then, however, the men of Ireland dispersed after consuming the feast of Tara. It was then that the pangs of jealousy and envy filled Ailill, and a heavy sickness settled <margin>15</margin> upon him, and he was carried afterwards to the fortress of Frémann in Tethba. Ailill remained there until the end of a year in long-sickness and long-sorrow, and he did not confess his sickness to anyone. Then Eochaid went to visit his brother and put his hand across his chest, and Ailill let out a groan. "Indeed," said <margin>20</margin> Eochaid, "this sickness is not a trifling matter," said Eochaid, "and how are you? Is it getting better for you?" "By my word," said he, "it is not; rather, it is worse and worse every day and every..."

<margin>From the Lebor na hUidre manuscript:</margin>
6. These are the three sons of Finn, son of Finnlug, son of the Queen: namely, Eochaid Fedlech and Eochaid Airem and Ailill Anguba. <margin>25</margin> Ailill Anguba then loved Étaín at the Feast of Tara, after she had slept with Eochaid. Because of this, he made a habit of long-gazing at her, and since long-gazing is a cause of love. Ailill’s mind reproached him for the deed he had done, but it was no help to him. Desire was stronger than nature. Ailill fell into a wasting sickness from it, because his honor would not allow him to speak of it <margin>30</margin> and he did not tell the woman herself.

<margin>5</margin>
8. Then Fachtna, the physician <term>liaig: a traditional healer or doctor</term> of Eochaid, was brought to them. He placed his hand over Ailill’s chest, and Ailill then let out a sigh. "Indeed," said Fachtna, "the deed is not a tragedy <note>original: "ni ba dirsann in gnim"</note>, for I know your sickness. Now, as for Eochaid, he went on his royal circuit throughout Ireland and left Étaín behind in the fortress. He said to her: "Well, my girl," said he, "let a gentle attendance be provided by you for Ailill as long as he remains alive; and if he dies," said he, "let his burial mound be dug by you, his pillar-stone and his headstone raised, and his name written in Ogham <note>The ancient Irish alphabet consisting of notches carved into stone</note>." Then the King went on his royal circuit throughout Ireland, and Ailill was left there in the fortress of Frémainn in Tethba <note>A territory in central Ireland, modern Westmeath/Longford</note> facing death and his end during that year.

8. Fachtna, the physician of Eochaid, was brought to examine him when he had resigned himself to death. The physician said to him: "Except for the two pangs that kill a person and which physicians cannot cure—namely, the pangs of love and the pangs of jealousy—these are what are within you." Ailill did not admit it to him, for he was ashamed.

Ailill was then left in Frémainn of Tethba to face death, and Eochaid went on a circuit of Ireland. Étaín was left in Ailill's company so that his last rites might be performed by her: that is, that his grave be dug, his lamentation performed, and his cattle slaughtered <note>A traditional funeral rite involving ritual sacrifice or a funeral feast</note>.

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

The maiden <note>Étaín</note> said to Ailill: "Come," said she, "tomorrow early in the morning to meet me at the house that is outside the fortress yonder, and there you shall have your request and your desire fulfilled."

12. However, Ailill remained without sleep that night until 
<margin>5</margin> morning came on the morrow. But when it was the proper time for him to go to his meeting, it was then that sleep fell heavily upon him, so that he remained sleeping until the hour of rising. Étaín went then to the meeting. She had not been waiting long when she saw a man coming toward her in the likeness of Ailill, though he appeared weary and exhausted. The 
<margin>10</margin> maiden perceived <note>literally: "brought recognition upon him"</note> that it was not Ailill. She remained there waiting for Ailill. Then the maiden returned from her meeting. "Come," said she, "to the 
<margin>15</margin> same place tomorrow."

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

<margin>[Egerton Manuscript:]</margin>
13. And it was just as it had been on the first day: the man came every day to meet her. Then the final day of her appointment arrived, and the same man appeared to her. "It was not with you," she said, "that I made this appointment at all. Why have you come to meet me? As for the one I agreed to meet here, it was not for any lust <margin>5</margin> or illicit reason that I arranged to meet him, but rather to heal him of the sickness he suffered because of his love for me." "It would be more fitting for you to come to meet me," said he, "for when you were Étaín Echraide <note>Echraide likely means 'Horse-rider'</note>, daughter of Ailill, it was I who was your first husband." "If that is so," she said, "what is your name at all, if I may ask?" "Not difficult; Mider of Brí Léith <note>A famous fairy mound or 'sidhe' in County Longford</note> is my name," he said. <margin>10</margin> "What was it that separated you from me, if we were once like that?" asked Étaín. "Not difficult," said Mider, "the sorcery of Fuamnach and the spells of Bresal Etarlam <insert>separated us</insert> <note>original: "ro duss sgar"</note>." Mider said to Étaín: "Will you go with me?" he asked. "No," she said, "I will not trade the King of Ireland for you—a man whose family or kin are unknown." <margin>15</margin> "It was I myself," said Mider, "who put the love for you into Ailill's mind."

<margin>[Lebor na hUidre Manuscript:]</margin>
13. At the time of the meeting, Étaín went to the tryst, and she saw the same man in the likeness of Ailill. Étaín went back to the house and found Ailill lamenting.
Étaín went out three times, and Ailill did not manage to reach the meeting, but she found the same man there. "It is not with you," she said, "that I made this tryst. Who <margin>20</margin> are you that has come to meet me? The man I agreed to meet—it was not for any secret or evil purpose I came to him, but to rescue the wife of the King of Ireland from the sickness that was upon him." "It would be more fitting for you to come to me," he said, "for when you were Étaín Echraide daughter of Ailill, I was your first husband. It was after paying a great price for you in the chief plains <margin>25</margin> of Ireland, and in waters and gold and silver, that I left you behind." "Tell me," she said, "what is your name?" "Not difficult; Mider of Brí Léith," he said. "Tell me," she said, "what separated us?" "Not difficult; the sorcery of Fuamnach and the spells of Bresal Etarlam." Mider said to Étaín: "Will you go with me?" <margin>30</margin> "No," she said, "I will not exchange the King of Ireland for a man whose family or lineage I do not know." "It was I indeed," said Mider, "who put the love for you into Ailill's mind for so long, until his blood and his [strength] failed..."

### IV The Wooing of Étaín

<margin>Egerton Manuscript</margin>
16. Now, Midir went westward to the Plain of Cruachan. "What good is it for us to visit this plain at all?" asked Croichen the Red-Skinned <note>Croichen was Midir's handmaid; the site is now known as Rathcroghan, the ancient capital of Connacht</note>. <margin>5</margin> Cruachan." They went after that to the fairy mound of Cruachan, because their kinship was close, and they remained there for the space of nine days eating and feasting. Then Croichen said to Midir: "Is this your dwelling?" Midir said to Croichen: "My dwelling is where the sun rises <note>meaning to the East</note>."
<margin>10</margin> Midir and Étaín then went to the Hill of Leth, son of Celtchar <note>Breg Leith, modern Ardagh Hill, Co. Longford</note>.

17. It was then, however, that Eochaid sent the couriers of Ireland and his entertainers and his road-scourers and his border-guards throughout Ireland to seek his wife. Eochaid himself then went throughout Ireland seeking Étaín; for a year
<margin>15</margin> from that day to the next he was seeking her and did not find her, and at the end of the year he reached the fortress of Frémainn <note>Frewin Hill, Co. Westmeath</note>.

18. Then Eochaid sent his druid to seek Étaín; Dalán was the druid's name. He traveled westward that day until he reached the mountain called the Mountain of Dalán, and he stayed there
<margin>20</margin> that night. The druid felt it a heavy burden that Étaín had been hidden from him for the space of a year; he then made four rods of yew and wrote <term>Ogham</term> <note>An early medieval alphabet of notches and lines used in Ireland</note> upon them, and it was revealed to him through his "keys of poetic wisdom" <note>original: "eochraib écsi" - a technical term for a druid's mantic or prophetic power</note> and through his Ogham, that Étaín was in the fairy mound of Breg Leith, having been carried there by Midir.

<margin>25</margin> 19. Then Dalán went eastward again to the fortress of Frémainn where Eochaid, King of Ireland, was. Eochaid asked his druid for news. It was then also that his couriers and his entertainers and his road-scourers and his border-guards returned to the King of Ireland, and he asked
<margin>30</margin> news of them, whether they had found news of Midir and Étaín. And they said they had not found it, until then his druid said to him: "Lamentation and great evil and trouble have found you by being in...

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

[Egerton Manuscript:]
“...the absence of your wife. Let the hosts of the men of Ireland be gathered by you, and proceed to Brí Léith <note>The hill of Bri Leith, modern Ardagh Hill, Co. Longford, associated with the fairy lord Midir</note> son of Celtchar, and let its destruction be carried out by you; it is there you shall find your wife, and bring her away from there either by consent or by force.”

<margin>5</margin> 20. Thereafter, Eochaid and the men of Ireland went to Brí Léith and they were destroying the fairy mound <note>original: "síd"</note> and demanding that Étaín be given out to them, but she was not given. And after that, the mound was breached, and Étaín was brought out, and she reached the fortress of Fremainn again. She remained in the affections <margin>10</margin> of the King of Ireland—that is, his own beloved and worthy dear companion, Eochaid Airem. And it was that Eochaid who was twelve years in the kingship of Ireland, until fire burned him in Fremainn. So that "The Love-Sickness of Ailill" is the name of that story, and "The Wooing of Étaín." And that Étaín bore no children to Eochaid Airem except for <margin>15</margin> one daughter, and her mother's name was given to her: Étaín, daughter of Eochaid Airem. And her daughter was Mess Búachalla <note>Literally "The Cowherd's Foster-child"</note>, the mother of Conaire the Great, son of Eterscél. It was for that reason that the fairy-folk <note>original: "sithchaire"</note> of the Plain of Breg and Midir of Brí Léith destroyed the taboos <term>geissi: sacred prohibitions or supernatural injunctions</term> of Conaire, <margin>20</margin> and they shortened his life and caused the plundering of the Plain of Breg, because of the destruction of the mound of Brí Léith and because Étaín had been taken from there by Eochaid Airem by force.

# Appendix

<margin>Regarding Chapter 13:</margin>
The earlier fates of *Étaín*, to which reference is made in this chapter, are known to me only from a fragment in the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre"</note> (Facsimile p. 129), which I share here:

...for a time, and her sun-bower <note>original: "gríanán," a sunny upper room or bower</note> with bright windows for going out of it; and a purple cloak was placed around her, and that sun-bower was carried by the Mac Óc <note>The Young Son, also known as Aengus, a god of love and youth</note> everywhere he went. And it was there she used to sleep every night, attending to her to restore her spirit until her joy and her form returned to her. <margin>25</margin> And that sun-bower was filled with wonderful, fragrant herbs, so that she thrived from them—from the scent and the bloom of those excellent, precious herbs. Fuamnach <note>The jealous first wife of Midir</note> was told of the love and the honor that was given to her by the Mac Óc. Fuamnach said to Midir: "Let your foster-son be summoned to you so that he may make peace between you both, and so that he may go in search of Étaín." A <margin>30</margin> messenger came to the Mac Óc from Midir, and she went to speak with him, and she went...

### IV The Wooing of Étaín

Fuamnach, meanwhile, went around until she was in the fairy mound and brought the same magical storm upon Étaín, which carried her out of her bower upon the wind, where she remained for seven years throughout Ireland, until a gust of wind drove her in misery and weakness so that it cast her upon the roof-tree of a house among the <term>Ulaid</term> <note>The people of Ulster</note> where they were drinking. She fell into the golden goblet that was in the hand of the wife of Étar, the champion from Inber Cichmaine in the province of Conchobar. The woman swallowed her with the sip she took from the vessel, so that she was conceived in her womb and was afterwards born as a girl. The name given to her was Étaín, daughter of Étar. It was one thousand and twelve years from the first birth of Étaín to Ailill until this final birth from Étar.

<margin>5</margin> Étaín was then reared at Inber Cichmaine by Étar, and fifty daughters of chieftains were around her, and it was he who used to feed and clothe them to be constantly in the company of Étaín, his daughter. One day, while all the girls were at the estuary bathing, they saw a horseman coming toward them across the plain to the water. He sat upon a brown, broad-backed horse with a curling mane and a wavy tail. A green mantle was folded about him, and a shirt with red embroidery was on him, and a gold brooch in his mantle reached to his shoulder on either side. A silver shield with a golden rim was upon his back. There was a silver shield-strap and a boss of gold upon it, and a five-pointed spear with a band of gold around it, and a bronze-colored <note>original: "cocró"</note> handle in his hand. Fair-yellow hair fell to his forehead. A fillet of gold was against his forehead so that his hair would not fall over his face. He stayed a while on the bank looking at the girls, and all the girls loved him, and then he recited this poem below:

<margin>25</margin> ->Étaín is here today indeed<-
->at the Mound of Fair Women after her wanderings<-
->among small children<-
->on the bank of Inber Cíchmuini.<-

->It is she who healed the eye of the king<-
->from the well of Loch Dá Líg,<-
<margin>30</margin> ->it is she who was swallowed in a drink<-
->by the wife of Étar in a heavy draught.<-

->It is because of her the king shall hunt<-
->the birds from Tethba,<-
->and he shall drown his two horses<-
<margin>35</margin> ->in the pool of Loch Dá Airbrech.<-

->There shall be many many wars<-
->because of you against the horseman of Meath,<-
->there shall be destruction upon the fairy mounds<-
->and battle against many thousands.<-

The young warrior <note>The god Midir</note> went away from them after that, and they did not know where he had come from or where he went then. "I think it is likely to be <margin>10</margin> true," said Mac Óc. "Étaín has been at my house in the mound <note>the *brú* or fairy dwelling</note> for some time in the shape in which she appeared to you, and perhaps it is toward her the woman has set out." Mac Óc went back to his house and found his glass bower <note>a magical sun-room</note> without Étaín in it. The Mac Óc turned upon Fuamnach’s track until he overtook her at a warlike assembly at the house of Bresal Etarlám the druid. The <margin>15</margin> Mac Óc attacked her and struck her head off, and he carried that head with him until it was on the brink of the mound. However, it is said in another version <note>original: "i slicht inninud aile"</note> that both of them, namely Fuamnach and Midir, were killed by Manannán <note>the sea god</note> in Brí Léith, of which it was said:

> Foolish Fuamnach was the wife of Midir, Sigmall and Brí with ancient trees, <margin>20</margin>

### Regarding Chapter 15:

*At this point, let at least an accurate printing of the poem from the Book of the Dun Cow <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre"</note> find a place here, in which Midir describes the beauties of his land and invites Étaín to come with him. In the Book of the Dun Cow, Midir wins Étaín from Eochaid in a game of chess; unfortunately, as already noted on page 115, the prose narrative in this manuscript is not preserved in its entirety. The poem following below, however, stands in only a loose connection with this narrative. The words "Will you come with me" are also found above in Chapter 13, and are perhaps the starting point of the poem. For Midir's invitation to Étaín to come with him has certainly belonged to the details of the story from the beginning.*

<columns>2</columns>

O fair woman, will you come with me,
the hair there is like the primrose flower,
There, there is neither "mine" nor "thine,"
the number of our host is a delight to the eye,
Every wealth is the purple of the plain,
though the plains of Ireland <note>original: "maige Fail"</note> are fair to see,

<column-break/>

to a wondrous land where stars are,
the shapely body is the color of snow.
white teeth there, black eyebrows,
every cheek is the color of the foxglove. <margin>4</margin>
the blackbird's eggs are a delight to the eye,
it is rare after knowing the Great Plain <note>Ireland</note>.

---

<margin>4</margin> of the host *Book of the Dun Cow*. Over *is dath sion* <note>color of foxglove</note> the gloss: *or it is speckled*.
<margin>5</margin> Over *maige* <note>plains</note> the gloss: *or herbs*; over *li sula* <note>delight to the eye</note>: *or it is the color*.

# IV The Wooing of Étaín

Though you find the ale of the Island of Destiny <note>original: "inse Fáil," a poetic name for Ireland</note> intoxicating, more intoxicating is the ale of the Great Land;
<margin>8</margin> a wondrous land is the land I speak of, where the young do not die before the old.

Warm, sweet streams flow through the land, a choice of mead and wine;
stately people without blemish, conception without sin or crime.

We see everyone on every side, and yet no one sees us;
<margin>12</margin> the darkness of Adam’s transgression <note>original: "temel imorbais A'daim"</note> conceals us from being counted.

O woman, if you come to my strong people, it is a crown of gold that will be upon your head;
fresh pork, ale, and new milk to drink you shall have with me there, O fair woman!

->O fair woman.<-

---

*The text and translation of this poem by Sullivan, in "On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish" Vol. III, p. 191, leave much to be desired. A translation by O’Curry, though based on a different manuscript, is found in the same work, Vol. II, p. 192.*

O’Kearney shares a very similar poem in the *Transactions of the Ossianic Society*, Vol. I, p. 23. For the same conceptions of a happy land return in the Finn Cycle <note>original: "Finnsage," the legends of Fionn mac Cumhaill</note>: to this land, the <term>Land of Youth</term> <note>original: "tír na n-óg"</note> or the <term>Land of the Living</term> <note>original: "tír na m-beo"</note> ("the Elysium of the pagan Irish"), Oisín is carried away by the daughter of the King himself.

<meta>This page introduces the fifth section of the manuscript, focusing on the mythological origins of the Irish hero Cuchulainn. It establishes the scholarly sources used for the translation.</meta>

# V.
## The Birth of Cuchulainn.

1. This strange tale is likewise found in the <term>Lebor na hUidre</term> <note>The "Book of the Dun Cow," the oldest surviving manuscript in the Irish language, dating to approximately 1100 AD.</note> and in the manuscript signed "Egerton 1782" in the British Museum; in the former on page 128 of the facsimile, in the latter on page 152. The scribe of the <term>Lebor na hUidre</term> cites as the source from which he copied this piece the <term>Libur Dromma Snechta</term> <note>The "Book of Druim Snechta," a now-lost 8th-century manuscript believed to contain very early versions of Irish myths.</note>, a regrettably lost older manuscript, which O'Curry discusses in *Lectures on the Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History*, page 13. Here too, regarding the Egerton manuscript, I possess a transcript by O'Grady, which I collated with the original in 1874.

2. The course of the narrative is as follows:
One day, a swarm of wondrous birds covers the land near <term>Emain Macha</term> <note>The ancient capital of the Ulaid (Ulster), located near modern-day Armagh.</note> and devours all the greenery. Conchobar <note>The legendary King of Ulster.</note> and his warriors set out to hunt them. They pursue the birds until night falls and it begins to snow. For shelter, they find a solitary house in which a man and a woman live. The woman gives birth to a son, and Dechtire, Conchobar’s sister, who had accompanied him as a charioteer, takes charge of the infant. Having returned to <term>Emain Macha</term>, she raises him until, to her great sorrow, a fatal illness carries him away. Following a wondrous vision by day, a man appears to her by night, identifying himself as <term>Lug mac Ethnend</term> <note>Also known as Lugh, a major deity of the Irish pantheon associated with light and skill.</note>, a supernatural being belonging to the <term>Tuatha Dé Danann</term> <note>The "People of the Goddess Danu," a race of divine beings in Irish mythology who inhabited Ireland before the arrival of humans.</note>, to—

<sig>2</sig>

<summary>This page introduces the mythological birth of the hero Cuchulainn, citing the historical manuscripts used as sources and summarizing the initial hunt that leads to his first, supernatural birth. It establishes the connection between the royal house of Ulster and the divine Lug mac Ethnend.</summary>
<keywords>Cuchulainn, Lebor na hUidre, Egerton 1782, Libur Dromma Snechta, Emain Macha, Conchobar, Dechtire, Lug mac Ethnend, Tuatha Dé Danann, Irish mythology, manuscript studies</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of the "Compert Conculaind" (The Conception of Cuchulainn), focusing on the transition between different manuscript versions and the naming of the hero's mother.</meta>

# V The Conception of Cuchulainn.
135

makes himself known. He tells her that she will become pregnant by him. It was he who arranged everything as it happened; he was the boy she had cared for, and this boy is now within her womb. Deichtire becomes pregnant, and no one knows from where. Conchobar betroths her to Sualdaim. She is ashamed of her condition. Having become a virgin again <note>This refers to a miraculous restoration of virginity common in Celtic hagiography and myth</note>, she becomes pregnant anew and gives birth to Setanta. This was Cuchulainn's name before he killed Culand's hound <note>The name Cuchulainn literally means "Culand's Hound," earned after the hero killed the smith Culand's guard dog and offered to take its place</note>.

3. Here the narrative ends in the Egerton manuscript <note>original: "Eg." refers to Egerton 1782, a 16th-century Irish manuscript</note>. In the Book of the Dun Cow <note>original: "LU." for Lebor na hUidre, the oldest surviving manuscript in Irish</note>, another narrative follows\*, of which unfortunately only the beginning—and even that in a mutilated state—is preserved. However, the same narrative is found in its entirety in a second version of the saga, which is shared in Egerton immediately after the first (Eg. 1782, p. 153).

Some details in this narrative are difficult to understand; the relevant passages are marked with asterisks. Evidently, mythological concepts lie at the foundation here as well, but these can only be traced with certainty once more similar stories are known. Sullivan refers to one of these, which likewise concerns a miraculous birth and in which birds also play a significant role, in *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Vol. I, p. ccclxx.

4. The name of Cuchulainn's mother is Dechtire. The facsimile of the Book of the Dun Cow always provides "Deichtine" in this narrative. The same confusion of *r* and *n* occurs in Egerton, where Chapter 1 offers "Briccni" for "Briccriu."

The abbreviated spelling has been left unmarked in the words for "and," "upon," and "son" <note>original: "ocus, for, mac"</note>. In Egerton, "and" is written out in full several times. The Book of the Dun Cow offers the form "dno" <note>An abbreviation for the Latin 'dano' or 'didiu,' meaning 'then' or 'moreover'</note> in Chapter 2.

---

\* The content of this section concerns the dispute over the upbringing of Cuchulainn.

<summary>This page discusses the miraculous conception of the Irish hero Cuchulainn (Setanta) and compares the textual variations between the Egerton 1782 and Lebor na hUidre manuscripts. It highlights the mythological elements of the story, such as the role of birds and the hero's mother, Dechtire.</summary>

<meta>This page begins the narrative of the Conception of Cú Chulainn, the central hero of the Ulster Cycle. It presents two versions of the opening: one from the Book of the Dun Cow (LU) citing the lost Book of Druim Snechta, and a second from the Egerton 1782 manuscript (Eg).</meta>

# V. The Conception of Cú Chulainn

<margin>[LU:]</margin>
->**The Conception of Cú Chulainn follows here, from the Book of Druim Snechta.**<-

1. One day, when the nobles of Ulster were with Conchobar <note>The King of Ulster</note> in Emain Macha <note>The royal seat of Ulster, modern Navan Fort</note>, a flock of birds used to frequent the plain before Emain. They grazed so thoroughly that they did not leave even the roots of the grasses or herbs in the ground. It was a cause of vexation to the Ulstermen to see them destroying their lands. They prepared nine chariots to hunt them one day, for it was a custom among them <margin>5</margin> to hunt birds. Conchobar, then, was in his chariot, and his sister Deichtire, who was a young woman of marriageable age. She was the charioteer for her brother. The heroes of Ulster were likewise in their chariots: namely Conall and Loegaire and all the rest. Bricriu <note>The "poison-tongued" satirist of the court</note> was also with them.

2. The birds flew before them swiftly across Sliab Fuait <note>The Fews Mountains, Co. Armagh</note>, across <margin>10</margin> Edmuind, and across Brega <note>The plain of Meath</note>. There was no ditch, nor fence, nor stone wall around land in Ireland in that time of old—until the time of the sons of Aed Slane—but only level plains; because of the many households in their time, it was then that boundaries were first partitioned in Ireland. Beautiful indeed and fair was the bird-track and the bird-song they had. There were nine score birds <margin>15</margin>

---

<margin>[Eg:]</margin>
->**The Conception of Cú Chulainn here.**<-

1. Conchobar and the nobles of Ulster were around him in Emain Macha once upon a time. A flock of birds used to frequent the plain before Emain; they grazed so that they did not leave even a blade of herb in the ground there. That was a grievance to the Ulstermen. They harnessed <gloss>so it is written</gloss> nine chariots to go to hunt them, <margin>20</margin> for it was always a custom of theirs to hunt birds. Conchobar was there first in his chariot, and Deichtire his sister was before him for the purpose, she being a young woman; and the heroes of Ulster besides in their chariots: namely Conall Cernach, and Fergus mac Róich, and Loegaire the Triumphant, and Celtchar son of Uthechar. Bricriu was there also with them like <margin>25</margin> every other warrior.

2. The birds then go before them across Sliab Fuait, across Muirtheimne, across Edmann, across the plain of Brega <gloss>so it is written</gloss>. Beautiful then was the bird-track and the bird-song they sang while in flight.

[Egerton version:]
Nine score birds was their number, and a chain of white bronze <note>original: "findruine," a precious alloy often used in Celtic art</note> between every two birds. Each *twenty* of the multi-colored birds went before them, and a chain of red gold between <margin>15</margin> those. Three of the birds were struck down before night. They went on then toward the edge of the Brug. Night fell upon them there. "Unyoke now," said Conchobar, "and go on a search for us to see if we may find a house for us in some place."

3. Conall Cernach and Bricriu went for that purpose. A <margin>20</margin> single new house was found, and a married couple within. They received a great welcome. They go back to their people and relate their travels to them, and Bricriu said it would not be worth going to the house, for it would be narrow, and it was without cloaks or food. They went out then to the house in their full number, <margin>25</margin> and they did not *just* fit into the house with their chariots and their horses and their weapons; and every good thing reached them in the house, both food and drink, both common and uncommon, so that there was never a better night for them. They saw something: a fair youth at the door of the cellar. And he said to them: <margin>30</margin> "When would you like your portion?" said he. "We have thought it time for a long while," [they replied].

# V. The Conception of Cú Chulainn

[From the Book of the Dun Cow:]
...before her lips, a small creature leaped from the liquid toward her mouth; when it was taken from her lips, no one saw where it vanished <note>original: "scéinti lea hanail," suggesting it disappeared or was scattered by her breath</note>. She slept then that night and saw a vision: a man came toward her and spoke to her. He said to her that she would be pregnant by him, and that it was he who had brought them to the fairy mound <note>The "Brugh" or "Bruig" refers to the Otherworld dwellings, likely Newgrange</note>. He stayed with her then. The young boy was there, and it was he who came into her womb, and his name would be Setanta, and he was Lug <note>original: "Lug mac Ethnend," the solar deity and master of all arts</note> son of Ethniu, and the foals were to be reared alongside the boy.

6. The girl was then pregnant. It was a great concern for the men of Ulster <term>Ulaid: the people of the northern province of Ireland</term>, for they did not know a husband who possessed her; they feared that it was Conchobar <note>The King of Ulster</note> who had done it in his drunkenness, for his sister used to sleep near him. Conchobar then betrothed his sister to Sualdaim mac Róig. She felt great shame going to the man in the marriage bed while she was pregnant. When she reached the bedpost, she crushed the fetus against the wood <note>original: "brúchtis... co m-bu óglán," implying she pressed her womb against the frame until the pregnancy vanished or was neutralized</note> until she was empty. She went to the man then. She became pregnant again immediately. She bore a son, and the name Setanta was given to him.

<column-break/>

[From the Egerton 1782 manuscript:]
...the small creature leaped from the liquid toward her mouth; when the creature was taken from her lips, no one saw the creature after that. The creature vanished suddenly from her breath at last. She slept then that night, and saw a vision: a man came to her, and said to her: "You shall be pregnant by me, woman," said he, "it is I who brought you all to the fairy mound," said he. "It is with me you stayed in the form of a bird. I am the boy you reared, it is he who has come into your womb, and Setanta shall be his name. I am Lug son of Ethniu."

6. The girl was then pregnant. That was a cause of worry for the men of Ulster then, for no husband was known for her at that time. The Ulstermen thought that Conchobar had done the deed in a drunken stupor, for his sister used to sleep with him always. The girl was then betrothed to Sualdaim. She was ashamed to go, being pregnant. She then prayed against the child that was in her womb; when she went to the bedpost and pressed her body against it, she became empty. She went to the man and was pregnant [again]...

# V. The Conception of Cuchulainn

[From the Egerton 1782 manuscript:]
"I am excellent in wealth and abundance. I am fierce in valor and arms. I am a warrior against my enemies. I am capable of caring for my foster-son. I am a shelter against every hardship. I bring misfortune to every strong man, and I bring benefit to every weakling." "Who is it that listens to me?" said Amergin <note>Amorgeni; the chief poet and father of Conall Cernach</note>, "I am enduring. <margin>5</margin>I am capable of fostering my foster-son for a king. I am praised for every excellence, for my valor and my arms, for my wisdom, for my luck, for my age, for my eloquence, for the brilliance and courage of my lineage (?) though I be a warrior, I am a poet, I am worthy of a king's favor. I overcome every chariot-warrior, and I owe thanks to no one except to Conchobar <note>The King of Ulster</note>. <margin>10</margin>I do not join with anyone except with the king alone."

"That will not be the end of it," said Sencha <note>Senco; the great judge and mediator of Ulster</note>. "Let Finnchóem <note>Conchobar's sister</note> take the boy until we reach Emain <note>Emain Macha, the royal capital of Ulster</note>, and Morann <note>Morunn; a legendary judge known for his infallible neck-ring</note> shall give a judgment concerning him once we have arrived." They went then to Emain, and the boy was with Finnchóem. Morann then gave his judgment after they arrived, and he said: <margin>15</margin>"Let Conchobar keep him indeed," said Morann, "for Finnchóem is his next of kin. Let Sencha teach him the ways of eloquence. Let Blai the Hospitaller <note>Blai Briugu; a wealthy landowner bound by a geis (taboo) to host any guest</note> feed him. Let him be brought to the knee of Fergus <note>Fergus mac Róich, the former king</note>. Let Amergin be his tutor. Let him be a foster-brother to Conall Cernach. Let him be nursed at the breasts of his mother Finnchóem. It is in this way that all shall protect him, <margin>20</margin>both commoner and chariot-warrior, both king and master-poet, for this boy shall be the friend of many. This boy shall equally avenge all your insulted honors; he will fight all your battles and your combats." This is what was done then: Amergin and Finnchóem took him, and he was reared in the fortress of Brith in the Plain of Muirthemne. The End.

# V. The Conception of Cú Chulainn

They appear. Dechtire, the sister of Conchobar <note>King of Ulster</note>, and fifty young maidens with her, secretly depart and vanish. For three years, they are sought in vain. One day, they transform themselves into birds and settle upon the plain of Emain <note>Emain Macha, the royal seat of Ulster</note>, consuming everything green. Conchobar, Fergus, Bricriu, and others set out on a hunt and pursue the birds for a long distance, until the birds completely disappear as night falls. Fergus searches for a shelter and finds a small house with a man and a woman inside. Everyone goes there. Bricriu steps out of the house and hears a mournful sound. He follows it and soon stands before a large, beautiful house. He learns that this is the dwelling place of Dechtire and her fifty maidens, and that they had transformed themselves into the birds and settled on the plain of Emain in order to lure the men of Ulster to this place. Bricriu returns to his companions and tells them what he has seen, though he conceals the fact that he discovered Dechtire in the mistress of the house. Conchobar sends for the woman; Fergus brings her forth, but she keeps away from Conchobar by pretending to be ill. In the morning, a boy is born. This is followed by the dispute over who should raise him.

### **The Conception of Cú Chulainn <term>Coimpert Conculaind</term> related below according to others, namely: The Feast of the House of Becfoltach.**

Dechtire, the sister of Conchobar, went away with fifty<gloss>1</gloss> maidens <note>original Old Irish: "coecait ingen"</note> on a secret elopement, escaping the men of Ulster and Conchobar; no trace or track of them was known, and they remained sought after until the end of three years. They used to frequent the plain before Emain in the form of a flock of birds, so that they grazed upon the plain until they left not even a blade of grass in the earth <margin>5</margin> there. This was a great affliction for the men of Ulster. The men of Ulster then harnessed nine chariots to hunt the birds, for hunting birds was a custom of theirs; namely, Conchobar was there, and Fergus, Amergin, and Blai Briuguig, Sencha, and Bricriu. The birds go...

...they traveled southward over **Sliab Fuait** <note>The Fews Mountains in County Armagh</note>, over **Ath Lethun** <note>The Broad Ford</note>, over **Ath n-Garuch**, and over **Mag n-Gossa** between the **Men of Ross** and the **Men of Arda**. "The fifty maidens are here in this house.
<margin>25</margin>Their leader is here beside me. **Deichtine** <note>King Conchobar’s sister</note> is her name, and it was they who went in the form of birds to **Emain Macha** to lure the **Ulstermen** so that they would come here." The woman gave a fringed purple cloak to **Bricriu**. And he went back to his people then. **Bricriu** meditated in his mind while
<margin>30</margin>returning to his people. "As for these fifty maidens," he said, "who are missing from **Conchobar** <note>King of Ulster</note>, he will give a wealth of gifts for finding them... I will hide it then," he said, "that it is his sister and her maidens."

# V. The Conception of Cú Chulainn

I found it. But however, I saw a house and a delightful company of women there besides. Then Conchobar <note>King of Ulster</note> asked news of Bricriu <note>The "poison-tongued" satirist of the Ulster Cycle</note>. "What is the nature of that place?" said Bricriu. "I reached a splendid, well-appointed house," said Bricriu, "and a noble, gracious, queenly princess <margin>5</margin> with beautiful curly hair is there. A company of fair, bright women, and a generous, most illustrious household are in the house." "That is a small matter," said Conchobar. "That man is a tenant of mine," he said, "he is in my land." "Let his wife come to me tonight to sleep with me," said Conchobar. No one then dared <margin>10</margin> to go against that command except Fergus <note>Fergus mac Róich, the former king and legendary hero</note>. He went then and delivered his message. And he was greeted with a welcome, and the woman came with him. She complained to Fergus that the pains of childbirth <term>galar noited</term> <note>Literally "the sickness of the nine days," referring to labor</note> were upon her. He spoke to Conchobar, and a delay was granted to her. Each of them left the other and they slept then. When they awoke, they saw something: a small male child in the fold of Conchobar's cloak.

## VI.
### Three Poems from the Finn Cycle.

1. In contrast to the large number of texts relating to <term>Conchobar</term> <note>Conchobar mac Nessa, the legendary King of Ulster in the first major cycle of Irish mythology</note> and his famous contemporaries, the oldest Irish manuscripts contain relatively few texts belonging to the second major cycle of legends, in which <term>Finn mac Cumaill</term> and <term>Ossín</term>* are the best-known names. For our knowledge of ancient Celtic culture, these legends are not all of equal value; most lack that realistic, detailed depiction of ancient living conditions which lends such high value to the sagas of the first category. However, the remarkable political circumstances that form the background of the second cycle of legends—which bear anything but a mythological character—deserve careful attention. According to tradition, <term>Finn</term> was supposedly slain in the year 283 AD <note>original: "p. Chr." (post Christum)</note>. As is the case elsewhere, mythical elements are interwoven with the legend here as well.

2. As is well known, the <term>Finn Cycle</term> <note>original: "Finnsage"</note> is found not only in Ireland but also in Scotland. Ireland possesses the oldest sources of the Finn legend, for here it can be traced in manuscript form—

---

<note>
* <term>Ossín</term>, the Old Irish form of the name, is a diminutive of *oss*, deer, and is therefore translated by <term>O'Curry</term> (*On the Manuscript Materials*, p. 304) as "little fawn." This etymology is consistent with the fact that in the Irish legend, <term>Finn's</term> primary occupation is hunting. <term>Oisin</term> is a later Irish form. In the *Book of the Dean of Lismore*, the name is written as Ossin, Ossein, Osseane, Ossan, Ossane. In the Scottish Highlands, it is pronounced "Oshen," with a short final syllable and the stress on the first syllable (Clerk, *The Poems of Ossian*, Edinburgh and London, 1870, I, p. 229). In <term>Macpherson</term> <note>James Macpherson, the 18th-century poet who claimed to have "discovered" the poems of Ossian</note>, the Nominative is Oisian, and the Genitive and Vocative are Oisein.
</note>

# VI The Legend of Finn. 147

...traceable back to the beginning of the 12th century and beyond, while in Scotland only back to the beginning of the 16th century.

As the oldest Irish sources, the following are known to me thus far:

1) The **Lebor na hUidre** <note>The Book of the Dun Cow, the oldest surviving manuscript in Irish</note> (end of the 11th or beginning of the 12th century) with the sober narrative of Finn’s birth titled "**Fotha Catha Cnucha**" ("The Cause of the Battle of Cnucha"), published and translated by Hennessy in the *Revue Celtique*, II, pages 86—93.

2) The **Book of Leinster** (middle of the 12th century) with five individual poems attributed to Finn himself, and three attributed to his son **Ossín** <note>Usually spelled Oisín in modern English; the legendary warrior-poet</note>. O’Curry, in *On the Manuscript Materials*, pages 302 and 304, overlooked one of the latter.

3) The **Book of Ballymote**, written around 1391, and the **Book of Lecan**, from the year 1416, with four other individual poems, of which two are attributed to Finn, the third to **Fergus** (a second son of Finn), and the fourth to **Cailte** (a cousin of Finn). The first two poems are found only in the Book of Lecan*, the other two in both manuscripts. According to O’Curry, in the previously cited work <note>original: "a. a. O." (am angegebenen Orte)</note>, pages 303 and 306.

4) The **Psalter of Cashel**, a Bodleian manuscript from the year 1453, with the narrative titled "**Macgnimartha Finn**" ("The Boyish Exploits of Finn"), published and translated by O’Donovan in the *Transactions of the Ossianic Society*, IV, pages 281—304.

5) The **Book of Lismore**, from the second half of the 15th century (O’Curry, in the previously cited work, page 199), with the "**Agallamh na Seanórach**" ("Dialogue of the Ancient Men"), a conversation between Oisín, Cailte, and St. Patrick (in verse), of which O’Curry (cited work, page 309) has shared a portion in translation, and on page 594 the Irish text for it. Compare *On the Manners and Customs*, III, page 703.

6) The manuscripts containing **Cormac’s Glossary**, edited by Stokes (*Three Irish Glossaries*, London 1862; *Cormac’s Glossary translated*, Calcutta 1868). A fragment of the same,

The <term>Leabar Breac</term> <note>The "Speckled Book," a medieval Irish manuscript</note> from the end of the 14. century offers the text in its entirety. However, internal evidence suggests that this glossary "was written, if not in the time of Cormac, at least within a century or so after his death" (Stokes, Three Irish Glossaries Preface p. xviii). <term>Cormac</term>, King and later Bishop of Caisel (that is, <note>original: "Castellum"</note> Castle), lived 831—903 AD. In this glossary, under the entry <term>orc tréith</term> <note>literally "the prince's steed," referring here to a legendary boar</note>, one of the many adventures is told that <term>Finn</term> experiences on hunting expeditions (Stokes, as cited above p. 34, Pref. p. xlvi).

3. Only then does the oldest Scottish source begin: the well-known book of the <term>Dean of Lismore</term> from the beginning of the 16. century, for the most part edited and translated by Th. MacLauchlan, with an introduction by Will. F. Skene, Edinburgh 1862. From the contents of this manuscript, twenty-three poems belong to the <term>Legend of Finn</term>. Of these, nine are attributed by specific headings to <term>Ossin</term> <note>Oisín, the son of Finn and legendary poet</note>, two to <term>Fergus</term>, and one to <term>Keilt mac Ronane</term> (in Irish <term>Cailte mac Ronáin</term>). In some cases, the once-present name of the author is no longer legible; however, for example, the poem provided on page 58 of the Gaelic text (page 80 of the English translation) is also likely to originate from <term>Ossin</term>, because the poet names <term>Fynn mac Cowil</term> <note>Finn mac Cumhaill</note> as his father. The remaining poems are anonymous. <term>Finn</term> himself does not appear as a poet in this manuscript. Philologically, this manuscript is also of great value because it represents the pronunciation of that time:

> "the language is not written in the orthography used in writing Irish, and now universally employed in writing Scotch Gaelic, but in a peculiar kind of phonetic orthography, which aims at presenting the words in English orthography as they are pronounced" (Skene, Introduction p. vii).

4. Whether these Scottish poems all originated on Scottish soil, or whether some perhaps go back to Irish originals, we must leave undecided for now. However, we may consider the latter possibility—

We must consider this because the Gaelic poem which Skene <note>William Forbes Skene (1809–1892), a Scottish historian and antiquary</note> shares in his Introduction (p. LXXXIV) as the oldest on Scottish soil from a manuscript "written prior to the year 1500" (?) is nothing more than a faithful copy—whether direct or indirect—of one of the three Irish poems attributed to Ossín <note>The legendary bard Oisín, son of Finn Mac Cumhaill</note> in the *Book of Leinster* <note>A 12th-century Irish manuscript</note>, with the exception of the glosses. Furthermore, the poems in the *Book of the Dean of Lismore* <note>A 16th-century Scottish collection of Gaelic poetry</note> could not, at least not all of them, have originated in the form in which they are presented to us there. This suspicion is prompted specifically by the irregularity of the metrical form. Even MacLauchlan <note>Thomas MacLauchlan, who translated the Dean of Lismore's book</note> admits at the end of his book (p. 130) that many of the poems must be read with Irish accentuation if the metrical form is to be preserved: "*Many of these pieces will not read as poetry at all, unless read in accordance with the Irish method of accentuation.*" I would also like to draw particular attention to the irregularity of the syllable count in the half-verses. Finally, Skene himself adds in the *Additional Notes* (e.g., p. 147) that several of the poems exist in Irish versions.

I felt it necessary to address the difference in age between the Irish and Scottish sources because one could easily get the erroneous impression from Skene’s Introduction that the Irish sources for the Legend of Finn are not of much consequence. Skene writes dispassionately and is willing to make concessions, but he is not impartial for the simple reason that—at least at the time he wrote the aforementioned Introduction—he did not know the sources for Irish antiquity well enough. He mentions on p. LXII that eleven poems have been identified in Ireland in sources older than the 15th century, but he focuses much more on the fact that almost all the texts published in the *Transactions of the Ossianic Society* (6 volumes, Dublin 1853–1858)

In doing so, however, on page LXI, in the summary of the first volume, he overlooked one of the poems from the *Book of Leinster* (12th century), and on page LXII, in the summary of the fourth volume, he overlooked the narrative from the *Psalter of Cashel* from the year 1453 mentioned above on page 147.

5. Regarding the various types of texts belonging to the Legend of Finn <term>Finnsage: the cycle of stories surrounding the mythical hero Finn MacCumhaill</term>, I refer to O'Curry, *On the Manuscript Materials* page 301 ff. We emphasize that in this cycle of legends, the poetic texts are more prominent. Curiously, the heroes of this cycle are simultaneously the singers of their own deeds. The oldest layer of poems are those in which Finn, Ossín <note>Also known as Ossian</note>, Fergus, or Cailte appear alone, either narrating or lamenting. Originally, Ossín shared the poetic honor with the other heroes, and only gradually did he become the sole singer of his era. It is characteristic in this regard that in the oldest Irish sources, most poems (seven out of twelve) are attributed to Finn and only three to Ossín; whereas in the *Book of the Dean of Lismore*, not a single one is attributed to Finn, and almost all to Ossín. A second layer of poems consists of the dialogues between Ossín and Saint Patrick, in which the Christian and pagan eras are contrasted with one another. According to tradition, Ossín is said to have lived to see the new era beginning with St. Patrick as an old, broken man. In the "*Colloquy of the Ancients*" <note>original: "Agallamh na Seanórach"</note> (see above page 147), which is the oldest text of this kind to date, Ossín still has a companion from the old times in Cailte. The *Book of the Dean of Lismore* contains several such poems, and in the *Transactions of the Ossianic Society*, most "Fenian Poems"—which unfortunately do not originate from ancient sources—appear in this form.

6. Regarding the difference in age between the two main legendary cycles, it is very interesting that a far greater gap is felt in the legend between St. Patrick and Cuchulainn <note>The hero of the Ulster Cycle, which predates the Fenian Cycle</note>. For at St. Patrick's request, God allows Cuchulainn to rise up out of hell—

"But Patrick's power was great, namely to awaken Cuchulainn after the same *had been in the earth for nine times fifty years*." <note>original: "neun mal fünfzig Jahre in der Erde gewesen war"</note> So it is told in the saga titled *The Demoniac Chariot of Cuchulainn* <note>original: "Siabar-charpat Conculaind"</note>, which O'Beirne Crowe published and translated from the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre," the oldest surviving manuscript in Irish literature</note> in the *Journal of the Royal Historical and Archaeological Association of Ireland*, 1871, p. 371 ff. On this occasion, it should be noted that the account mentioned above on p. 147—edited by O'Donovan from the *Psalter of Cashel*—concerning Finn's feats of strength which he is said to have performed as a boy, is merely an imitation of what Fergus tells of the boy Cuchulainn in the *Cattle Raid of Cooley* <note>original: "Táin Bó Cualgne," the central epic of the Ulster Cycle</note> (see *Book of the Dun Cow* p. 59, "The Boyhood Deeds here below" <note>original: "Na macgnimrada inso sís"</note>). The dependence of the "Fenian Tales" on the first primary cycle of sagas will be demonstrable even more frequently upon closer investigation. Furthermore, the same mythological elements that we encounter in the first saga cycle reappear in the Finn saga.* From this, it does not follow, of course, that Finn was not a historical person at all and never lived. O'Curry has argued very energetically for his historical character in *On the Manuscript Materials*, p. 303: "...that he existed about the time at which his appearance is recorded in the annals, is as certain as that Julius Caesar lived and ruled at the time stated on the authority of the Roman historians." That is saying too much.

7. May we consider the poems attributed to Finn, Ossín <note>Oisín, the son of Finn and legendary poet</note>, Fergus, and Cailte to be authentic—that is, are they truly the poetic productions of these men? When, in the *Book of the Dean of Lismore*, even the dialogues between Ossín and St. Patrick are attributed to Ossín himself, no one will seriously believe in this authorship of Ossín. But when the Reverend Clerk, the most recent editor of the *Poems of Ossian* (Edinburgh 1870), p. xxxviii, advocates for the view that

# VI The Legend of Finn.

Even if these poems—which first became known through *Macpherson* <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), who claimed to have discovered and translated ancient epic poems by the bard Ossian; these were later revealed to be largely his own compositions based on fragments.</note>—originated neither in modern times nor in the Middle Ages, but in an even older era, one could at least hold it possible with much more justification *a priori* that among the oldest Irish poems attributed to *Finn* and *Ossín* <note>Also known as Ossian, the son of Finn Mac Cumhaill.</note>, one or another might date back to such an ancient time. We must grant some indulgence to *O'Curry* <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a foundational scholar of Irish history, though criticized here for lacking formal linguistic training.</note>, who performed great service for Irish antiquity but possessed no linguistic knowledge, if he seems to have almost held this view (see *On the Manuscript Materials p. 302, cf. however p. 301*). Only a linguistically trained man can feel the full weight of the argument that we must not attribute the grammatical forms of the 11th or 12th century to the third century. That the poems preserved in the *Book of Leinster* <note>A 12th-century Irish manuscript containing a vast collection of genealogies, tales, and poems.</note> were originally composed in an older linguistic form is not to be assumed. For only in the form in which they lie before us are they regularly constructed metrical works of art. As soon as we attempted to introduce a more archaic form, the metrical structure would be destroyed. On the other hand, these poems are certainly older than the 12th century. I would not like to place too much weight on the circumstance that one of them is glossed <gloss>annotated with explanations</gloss> throughout. The glosses were necessary not because of old forms, but because of rare words; and the fact that rare words are used can also depend on the whim of the poet.

How I imagine that *Finn* and *Ossín* became singers has already been suggested above on page 63. My hypothesis is supported by the fact that the headings of the old poems read: *Ossín* or *Finn cecinit* <note>Latin: "Ossín or Finn sang [this]."</note>. This corresponds exactly to the "and so it was that Conchobur sang this" <note>original: "conid and ro chachain Conchobur inso"</note> (p. 140) and similar phrases by which poems incorporated into or appended to prose narratives are so often introduced. From this *cecinit* <note>Latin: "he sang"</note> it is only a small step to the "The author of this is Ossín" <note>original: "Auctor hujus Ossín"</note>, as we read in the *Book of the Dean of Lismore*. It is very interesting that according to *Keating's* <note>Seathrún Céitinn (Geoffrey Keating), a 17th-century Irish historian.</note> report on the *Fianna* <note>The small, semi-independent warrior bands of Irish mythology.</note>, which *O'Curry* shares in *On the Manners and Customs Vol. II p. 381*, no one could be admitted into this corps who was not experienced
