# VI The Finn Cycle. 153

was in the art of poetry. Whether this trait of the legend has Ossín’s <note>Also known as Ossian, the legendary Gaelic bard.</note> poetic fame as a prerequisite or a consequence is difficult to decide.

8. It must be conceded that the Scots possessed the Finn Cycle in the 16. century, that it still lives today in the mouths of the people, that it is localized in the mountains and valleys of Scotland, and that Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), who claimed to have discovered and translated ancient epic poems by Ossian.</note> did not need to go to Ireland to fetch the "Ossianic poems" or the materials for them. A different question, however, is whether the Finn Cycle was originally at home in Ireland or in Scotland. Naturally, Finn could only have been either an Irishman or a Scotsman. In the Irish legend, which we can trace back in manuscript form to the 11. century, Finn is an Irishman. More important in this question, however, is that even in the poems found in the *Book of the Dean of Lismore* <note>A 16th-century Scottish manuscript containing a large collection of Gaelic poetry.</note>, Finn does not appear to us as a Scotsman, but as an Irishman, and that there, not Scotland, but Ireland is designated as the scene of his deeds. The legendary material is therefore undoubtedly of Irish origin. The dependence of the Scottish legend on the Irish is also expressed in external matters: here as there, alongside Ossín, Fergus and Cailte also appear as singers or authors of songs; here as there, Ossín is brought together with St. Patrick.

9. Skene <note>William Forbes Skene (1809–1892), a Scottish historian and antiquary.</note> is of a different opinion. He believes that the Scots possess the Finn Cycle independently of the Irish. Instead of placing Finn in the foreground, he starts from those warrior bands (<term>fiann</term>, <term>fianna</term> <note>Old Irish terms for small, semi-independent warrior groups.</note>), of whom Finn appears as the supreme commander. By rejecting the Irish tradition as illusory and uncertain—going much too far in this—he considers the <term>fiann</term> or <term>fianna</term> to be the name of a race which immediately preceded the <term>Scoti</term> <note>Latin term for the Gaels/Scots.</note> in Ireland and Scotland:

> „they were of the population who immediately preceded the Scots in Erin <note>Ireland</note> and in Alban <note>Scotland</note>, . . they belong to that period in the history of both countries, before a political separation had taken place

<summary>This page examines the competing claims of Ireland and Scotland regarding the origins of the Finn Cycle, concluding that while the legends are deeply rooted in Scottish folklore, the earliest manuscript evidence points to an Irish origin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Finn Cycle (Finnsage) and the debate over its origins in Ireland versus Scotland, following the critique of previous scholarly claims.</meta>

# VI The Legend of Finn.

between them” (p. LXXVIII). The only positive evidence for these very bold assertions—which, in my view, are not suited to be preferred over Irish tradition—consists of a few passages from a later Irish poem in which the “Warriors <note>original: *Fians*</note> of Scotland <note>original: *Alban*</note>,” “Warriors of Britain <note>original: *Breatan*</note>,” and “Warriors of Scandinavia <note>original: *Lochlin*</note>” are mentioned. But *warrior-band* <note>original: *fiann*</note> cannot possibly be a tribal name, for it is a feminine noun and is very often used in the singular. For example, in Cormac’s Glossary under the entry <term>orc tréith</term> <note>literally "the prince's boar"; a technical term in Old Irish law and literature</note> (where, as noted above, a legend is recounted), we find the Nominative Singular *the warrior-band* <note>original: *in fiann*</note>, Dative *with his warrior-band* <note>original: *cona féinn*</note>, and Accusative *with the warrior-band* <note>original: *la feinn*</note>; alongside this, the Genitive Plural *to support the warrior-bands* <note>original: *do fulang na fiann*</note>. In the same source, the derivative term <term>fénnid</term> <note>a member of a fian; a landless hunter-warrior</note> occurs in the sense of *champion* or warrior. The Irish understand *fiann* in the sense of a *national militia* or *standing army*. This is not the place to investigate the interesting political conditions that come into consideration here.

10. The Irish legend certainly reached Scotland early. Communication between Scotland and Ireland has existed from time immemorial, as the oldest Irish legends testify. However, it must have taken a long time before the legend became so at home in Scotland that the Scottish people believed in Finn as the hero of their own ancient past. In the poems contained in the book of the <term>Dean of Lismore</term> <note>A 16th-century Scottish manuscript containing a large collection of Gaelic poetry</note>, I cannot find this belief clearly expressed. We cannot discuss here the transformation that the Irish legend underwent in Macpherson’s poems <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), who claimed to have translated the works of the ancient bard Ossian</note>. We have already highlighted one point above on page 65: the Finn legend is mingled with older Irish legendary cycles that had likewise found their way to Scotland. Only a precise factual analysis of Macpherson’s poems, with proper consideration of language and metrical form, can help us reach an objective judgment on this unique literary phenomenon. One must not deny these poems a significant historical value, for in the last century they exerted a powerful influence on great minds. Their factual value, however, will consist in the fact that they represent a final phase

<summary>The author argues that the term 'fiann' refers to a type of military organization rather than a specific tribe, and critiques the historical authenticity of Macpherson's Ossianic poems while acknowledging their cultural impact.</summary>

# VI. Three Ossianic Poems.

represent Celtic legend, language, and poetry. What exactly should be attributed to the editor Macpherson <note>James Macpherson (1736–1796), who claimed to have discovered and translated ancient Gaelic epics by the bard Ossian.</note> in this regard has still not been definitively proven. It is unjustified to brand every deviation from the older, Irish form of the legend as a "forgery" by Macpherson. Likewise, one must not reproach Macpherson's poems simply because they offer a later form of the legend rather than the oldest, or because the core of the legend is of Irish origin. If we were to consider such points as the sole deciding factors here, then, for example, our judgment of Wolfram von Eschenbach’s *Parzival* <note>A famous Middle High German epic; the author adapted French sources, much as Macpherson adapted Gaelic ones.</note> would also have to be significantly lowered.

The name "Ossianic Poems" has become a technical term <note>original: "Terminus technicus"</note> for the poems belonging to the Finn Cycle <note>The legends surrounding the mythical hero Finn MacCumhaill.</note>. We retain this name, although, as we have seen, only a few of the oldest poems are attributed to Ossín <note>Oisín, the son of Finn and the traditional narrator of the cycle.</note> himself.

11. The three Ossianic poems that I present here from the *Book of Leinster* <note>A 12th-century Irish manuscript containing a vast collection of lore and literature.</note> are distinguished from other poems found in this volume by the complicated but easily recognizable regularity of their metrical form. All three belong to the meter called "Great Rannaigheacht" <note>original: "Rannaigheacht mor"</note> in O’Donovan’s *Irish Grammar*, page 422. The stanza (*rann*) consists of two long lines or four half-verses, each of which contains seven syllables. Each long line ends with a monosyllabic word. These monosyllabic words form the end-rhyme of the stanza. To this is added the internal rhyme, through which the two half-verses of a long line are connected. The most elegant form of this rhyme consists in—

---

* The essential factor in Irish rhyme is the vowel. There are verses whose rhyme consists only in the agreement of the vowels. However, the perfect type of rhyme also requires the agreement of consonants. The consonants do not necessarily need to be identical; their similarity is sufficient. Which consonants are considered similar to one another is shown by the following classification of consonants: s; p, c, t; b, g, d; f, ch, th; ll, m, nn (nd), ng, rr; bh, dh, gh, mh, l, n, r. Cf. O’Donovan, *Irish Grammar*, page 415.

<summary>The author defends the literary value of Macpherson's Ossianic poems despite their deviations from older sources and introduces the technical metrical rules of the "Great Rannaigheacht" found in the Book of Leinster.</summary>
<keywords>Ossian, James Macpherson, Finn Cycle, Book of Leinster, Irish prosody, Rannaigheacht mor, Celtic philology, Wolfram von Eschenbach</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical analysis of Irish poetic structures, specifically focusing on the metrical rules found in the three Ossianic poems introduced in the previous section.</meta>

# VI Three Ossianic Poems.

that the last word of the first <term>half-verse</term> <note>German: Halbvers; the first half of a poetic line</note> rhymes with the penultimate word of the second <term>half-verse</term>, although articles or particles preceding the final word of the second <term>half-verse</term> are not taken into account. In I 12, II 5, 7, 8, and III 4, the internal rhyme appears twice; in some verses, it is absent. Finally, <term>alliteration</term> <note>the repetition of initial consonant sounds</note> in these poems rarely serves to connect the two halves of a <term>long-line</term> <note>German: Langzeile; a full line of verse consisting of two halves</note>, as it does in Germanic poetry; instead, it occurs predominantly within the <term>half-verse</term>, most often on the last two words, where again articles or particles are not considered.

The <term>alliterative rhyme</term> <note>German: Stabreim</note> connects two half-lines in I 7, and two <term>long-lines</term> in I 9 and 10. An <term>aspirated s</term> <note>a softened 's' sound, often pronounced like 'h' in Irish</note> is to be regarded as non-existent for rhyming purposes (I 12, II 5, 6, 8). In formulas such as <term>of the living</term> <note>original: inna m-beo</note>, <term>if they were</term> <note>original: dia m-betis</note>, and <term>their death</term> <note>original: a m-bás</note>, it is not the <term>eclipsed</term> <note>a linguistic feature where a preceding letter suppresses the sound of the following one</note> 'b' that counts as the <term>rhyme-stem</term> <note>German: Reimstab</note>, but the 'm'. Conversely, the 'n' placed before a vocalic onset does not seem to hinder vocalic <term>alliteration</term> (I 5). In the manuscripts, the beginning of the poem is usually repeated after its conclusion; however, it is not uncommon for the last word of the poem itself to correspond to its beginning: as seen here in I <term>ogom</term> (or <term>og</term> in verse 12? see the note there), in II <term>to the custom of the stone-throwers</term> <note>original: do gres teilcind lia</note>, and in III <term>thou</term> <note>original: tú</note>.

In general, I refer—as previously on page 4—to the chapter "Irish Poetic Construction" <note>original Latin: Constructio Poetica Hibernica</note> in the *Celtic Grammar* <note>original Latin: Grammatica Celtica</note> page 936, and to the chapter "Of Versification" in O'Donovan's *Grammar of the Irish Language* page 412. However, when Zeuss says on page 937:

> "it is not necessary, however, for the number of syllables to be the same in each half-line, nor in the individual verses of the same poem," <note>original Latin: nec tamen necesse est idem numerus syllabarum sit in utroque hemistichio nec in singulis versibus ejusdem carminis</note>

this observation is not correct in such a general sense. To be sure, there are poems with a freer and changing <term>meter</term> <note>German: Versmass</note> (cf. e.g., Patrick's Hymn, p. 52), but in most Irish poems I have encountered, the number of syllables is regulated by rule (cf. the citation in the note to p. 4). The nature of <term>stressed</term> <note>German: Hebungen</note> and <term>unstressed</term> <note>German: Senkungen</note> syllables in Old Irish verse has not yet been investigated in detail. If one assumes that the <term>stress</term> coincides with the <term>word accent</term>, and considers that polysyllabic

# VI. Three Ossianic Poems.

Words can have a secondary stress in addition to the primary stress (cf. O’Donovan’s *Irish Grammar* p. 403). If one finally assumes that the syllables where the rhyme or alliteration appears must also be in the stressed position, it soon becomes clear that every half-verse has a specific number of stresses, although a regular alternation between stressed and unstressed syllables does not take place.

Alliteration and rhyme are marked in the following three poems through the typography.

### I.

This poem is found in the *Book of Leinster* folio 109, b, and has already been printed, albeit not without inaccuracies, in the *Transactions of the Ossianic Society*, Vol. I p. 49, with a translation by O’Curry. The latter is at least more correct than Sullivan’s translation in the *Introduction to O’Curry’s Lectures on the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish* (Vol. I) p. CCCXLI. My text is based on a new transcription.

The subject of the poem is the Battle of Gabair Aicle <note>Genitive: Gabra, Dative: Gabair</note>*, in which Oscar, son of Ossian, and Cairbre Lifechair, the King of Ireland, gave each other their deaths. This battle is said to have taken place in the year 284 AD <note>original: "p. Chr." (post Christum)</note>, one year after Finn’s death. The Fenians <term>Fennier: members of the Fianna, the legendary bands of warriors led by Finn Mac Cumhaill</term> were defeated in the struggle against the King of Ireland, against whom they had rebelled. This decisive battle is treated in more detail in a dialogue between Ossian and St. Patrick, a later poem published in the 1st volume of the *Transactions of the Ossianic Society* (Dublin 1854) by Nicholas O’Kearney, with an English translation and a noteworthy introduction. In the *Book of the Dean of Lismore*, they refer—

---

<margin>* In English, it is customary to say "The Battle of Gabhra," although the genitive form Gabhra is not justified in this translation. According to O’Curry, Gabair is "now the hill of Skreen, near Tara"; according to O’Kearney, it is "Garristown in the County of Dublin."</margin>

# VI. Three Ossianic Poems.

There are two poems concerning this battle (the Battle of Gabra <note>original: "cath Gabra, Gabrych"</note>), one of which is attributed to Fergus.

Regarding the prehistory of the battle, O'Curry treats it briefly in *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Volume II, page 387.

The mention of the Ogham inscription and the mention of riding are particularly interesting. In the oldest sagas, the heroes travel in chariots, not on horseback.

---

->**Ossian sang.**<-

->In the battle of Gabra, Oscar and Cairpre Lifechair were slain.<-

**Ogham** on a **stone**, a **stone** above a **grave**, in the **place** where **men used to go in a troop**;
the **son of the King of Ireland** was **wounded there** by a **slender spear** atop a **white horse**.

**Cairpre cast a shot of notice** from the **back of his good son, it was a struggle**;
<margin>4</margin>
**before their weariness could meet**, **Oscar** was **struck in his right hand**.

**Oscar cast a great shot** **angrily, fiercely like a lion**,
so that he **killed Cairpre, grandson of Conn**, **before whom deeds of battle had submitted**.

**Great feats of the youths** met their **death from the fight**;
<margin>8</margin>
**before their weapons could meet**, **their dead were more numerous than their living**.

**I myself was in the struggle** on the **south side of green Gabra**;
I **killed fifty heroes twice over**, **it is I who struck them with my hand**.

**A chariot-player would play for a reward** **at the time when my choice was a rush**;
<margin>12</margin>
I **used to kill a boar in a holy wood**, or I **would outstrip a swift bird for its egg**.

**That Ogham which is in the stone** **around which the doomed men fell**;
**if Finn of the twenty deeds remained**, **long would the Ogham be remembered**.

<summary>This page presents a Middle Irish poem attributed to the legendary bard Ossian, describing the tragic Battle of Gabra where his son Oscar and the High King Cairpre fell. The translator highlights the historical significance of Ogham inscriptions and the transition from chariots to cavalry in Irish lore.</summary>

# Appendix.

**Verse 4.** Regarding *gairsiu condristais* <note>original: "shortly before they should meet"</note> compare Verse 8; regarding *ro bi* <note>original: "he was slain"</note> see Verse 10. Does *sciss* rhyme with *bi*? The alliteration appears to be missing.

**Verse 7.** *O’Curry* <note>Eugene O'Curry (1794–1862), a prominent Irish social historian and scholar.</note> translates *amansi* as "dexterous," taking *na maic* <note>the sons</note> (supplementing it thus) as the subject and the feminine form *mora* <note>great</note> as the predicate. Could *mansi* be the nominative plural of *manais*, which *O’Curry* (in *On the Manners and Customs of the Ancient Irish*, Vol. II, p. 255) explains as a "broad trowel-shaped spearhead for thrusting"? Compare the same work, p. 262, where this word is connected with *mór* <note>great</note>. Or does *amansi* belong to the phrase "from the wiles of this world" <note>original: "ar amainsib in chentair"</note> (see *Lorica of Gildas*, edited by Stokes, Glossary 147)?

**Verse 8.** *Stokes* (in *Beiträge*, Vol. VII, p. 54) translates *gairsiu condristais a n-airm* as: "shortly before their weapons met," by breaking down *gairsiu* into *gair-résiu* <note>literally "short-before"</note>. The subjunctive form should probably be given even more consideration. The alliteration, if present, is very well hidden. The second half-verse has one syllable too many, unless one is permitted to suppress or elide the *a* after *lia* (compare *O’Donovan’s* remark on the verse "A youth who was with great Mary" <note>original: "Oglach do bhí ag Muire mhóir"</note>, *Irish Grammar*, p. 420).

**Verse 11.** *Arpetend etc.* Sullivan translates: "*I used to handle the Corbach with skill, when my courage was high!*"

**Verses 13, 14.** In this stanza, the half-verses rhyme with each other, rather than the full lines as seen previously. Perhaps it was added later? In both Verse 14 and Verse 12, the final word was certainly chosen with regard to the opening word of the poem <note>A common Irish poetic device called 'dúnadh' or 'closing', where the end of the poem echoes the beginning.</note>. In the first half of Verse 13, there is one syllable too many. Should *sin* be read for *isin* <note>the</note>, or *ogum* <note>Ogham script</note> used without the article? Since *ogum* was originally neuter, in Old Irish it would be called *a n-ogum*. This final stanza is cited by *O’Donovan* (*Irish Grammar*, Introduction, p. XLV), but with the incorrect reading of *mór* <note>great</note> instead of *na troich* <note>of the doomed/short-lived one</note>.

---

### O’Curry’s Translation.

*The question marks are my own.*

1. 2. *An Ogham in a stone, a stone over a grave, in the place where men were wont to pass; the son of the king of Ireland <note>original: "Eire"</note> was there slain, by a mighty spear on a white horse’s back.*

3. 4. *Cairpre threw a sudden cast, from the back of his horse good in battle; shortly before he (?) met his own death (?), Oscar was slain by (?) his right arm.*

5. 6. *Oscar threw a mighty throw, angrily, vehemently, like a lion; and killed Cairpre the grandson of Conn, before they raised their battle cries.* <note>Oscar was the son of Oisín and grandson of Finn; Cairpre Lifechair was the High King of Ireland who fought the Fianna at the Battle of Gabhair.</note>

7. 8. *Dexterous, great, were the youths, who received their deaths from the fight; shortly before their weapons met, their dead were more than their living.*

11. 12. *Music, boating, rewarding, the prey most difficult I chose <note>original: "der ganze Vers unsicher" — the entire verse is uncertain</note>, I would kill a boar in the hard wood, I would rob a vengeful bird of its egg.*

13. 14. *That Ogham <note>An early Medieval alphabet used primarily to write the Archaic Irish language on stone monuments.</note> which is in the stone, around which fell the slain; were Finn the fighter of battles living, long would he remember the Ogham.*

## II.

The second poem, found in the *Book of Leinster* <note>A significant 12th-century Irish manuscript</note> folio 153, b (Facsimile page 192), is one of those attributed to Finn. I am sharing it here according to a transcript by Mr. Hennessy, which I compared with the original in 1871. Regarding the situation to which the poem refers, I have no further specific details. O’Curry, in *On the Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History* page 302, describes it as "a short poem, of only five quatrains <note>four-line stanzas</note>, on the origin of the name of *Magh-da-Gheisi*, or the Plain of the Two Swans (in Leinster)." This name clearly relates to the two maidens whose loss Finn mourns in verse 3.

## Finn son of Cumhall sang this:

The stone that I used to throw always across the Plain of the Two Swans to the Ridge of Sleep;
long was my cast with that stone, but today it does not reach its mark from me.

<margin>4</margin>
There come no more to meet me here the two gifted daughters of lasting womanly-strife;
with bright forms and dark cheeks, great would be the deed were they still alive.

If my life has reached its end, every danger and every deed has come upon me,
therefore they come not to meet me, unless it be a desire to avoid me in death.

<summary>This page concludes a poem about the Battle of Gabhair and introduces a second poem from the Book of Leinster concerning the "Plain of the Two Swans." The verses reflect on the fading strength of the hero Finn mac Cumaill and his mourning for two lost maidens.</summary>

# VI Three Ossianic Poems.

**The Plain of the Two Swans** <note>original: "Mag Da gési"</note> shall be until Judgment Day in their wake, every hour though it be sad; it shall be a place of parting without affection for the seed that has reaped and slept. <margin>8</margin>

Many a warrior was slain every day in that **good** plain with its many mists; across the **Plain of the Two Swans** in every place, from the place where stones were always cast.
->The End.<-

### III.

Attention has already been drawn above on page 149 to the dual source of this poem. In the Book of Leinster <note>A 12th-century Irish manuscript, one of the most important sources of medieval Irish literature</note>, it is found on folio 161, b. Gaidoz discovered the original of the version shared by Skene in the manuscript marked "XXXVIII, 2" in the Advocates’ Library (p. 154), a manuscript originating from the collection of the Highland Society of London. Regarding this manuscript, Gaidoz remarks in the *Celtic Review* <note>original: "Rev. Celt."</note> Vol. II p. 470: "It is a quarto manuscript on paper, of 94 leaves, which we have judged to be from the 17th century." <note>original: "C’est un ms. in-4 en papier, de 94 feuilles, que nous avons jugé être du XVII^e siècle"</note>. I am having the text derived from there (designated by Ed.) printed together with the glosses <note>interlinear or marginal explanations</note>, just as it appears in Skene’s Introduction to "The Dean of Lismore’s Book" p. lxxxiv. I owe the text from the Book of Leinster to the kindness of Professor Atkinson in Dublin, who, at my request during the printing of this book, sent me an exact copy (with line divisions and with the glosses between the lines, as in the original).

That the Edinburgh text must go back, directly or indirectly, to the Dublin text—or with it to a common older source—is evident particularly from the almost complete identity of the glosses and the signature. In the signature, this poem is attributed to Oisin <note>The legendary bard and warrior of the Fenian Cycle, son of Fionn mac Cumhaill</note>. However, O’Curry seems to have overlooked it on p. 304 of *On the Manuscript Materials* <note>original: "Ms. Mat."</note> during his enumeration of the oldest Ossianic poems, so that the number of these would be twelve rather than eleven.

It is not without interest that citations from this poem are found in O’Davoren’s Glossary (15th century), one of the *Three Irish Glossaries* edited by Stokes. To this fact—

# Appendix.

*Verse 2 cited in O'Davoren's Glossary (edited by Stokes) p. 96: <term>Genam</term> [means] a sword. As in: "my sword in my hand" (i.e., in my hand). <term>Duais</term> means hand in that context.*

*Verse 3 cited in the same work p. 71: <term>Cinnes</term> [means] he leaped. As in: "vast the vision... over which I leaped swiftly, my hound" (i.e., my dog). <term>Cuib</term> means dog in that context.*

*Verses 7 and 8 translated by Hennessy, Academy Aug. 1, 1871 p. 367:*
> "As big as a caldron her eye, as big as a hill her good lair; my sword severed her neck, and my hound [hanging] from her ear."

# VI. Three Ossianic Poems.

Verse 9 is cited in O’Davoren’s Glossary, page 71: *Cribuis*, that is, a pig; as in: "the sow of the sea of eastern Tallann." <note>original: "Cribuis i. muc. ut est cribus mara talla tair." This is an Old Irish gloss explaining the rare word 'cribuis'.</note>

->Skene’s Translation.<-

*The parentheses are my own additions.* <note>The author is referring to William Forbes Skene, a 19th-century historian and Celtic scholar.</note>

1. 2. *My eyes slumbered in sleep, my spear was with my shield, my sword was in my hand, and my hand under my ear. (In 2b a syllable is missing.)*

3. 4. *A strange dream (?) happened to me, I set swiftly my dogs (Singular) on a sow in the plain upon flesh. She was fat to the tusk in her jaw. (Dacheird, focheird is a verbal form and belongs to the preceding text.)* <note>The author is correcting Skene's grammatical interpretation of the Old Irish verbs.</note>

5. 6. *Thirty feet for me with my shoes, in her side to the beard of her snout; thirty inches for Finn in her tusk, fat above on her under her hide (?).* <note>Finn refers to Finn MacCumhaill (Finn McCool), the legendary Irish hunter-warrior.</note>

7. 8. *Large as a caldron was each eye, large as a vessel the hollow beneath (?). My sword hewed in her neck, and my dogs (Singular) fixed on her ear.*

9. 10. *Sow of the sea of eastern Tallann, which strikes the rock where the wave touches. My limbs were to me a protection to me strong, as thyself not weak like thee.*

# VII.

## The Vision of Adamnán.

---

1. This text is presented here from two manuscripts:
1) from the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre"; a famous 12th-century Irish manuscript</note> (written circa 1100, see above p. 114), in the facsimile pp. 27–31;
2) from the *Speckled Book* <note>original: "Lebor Brec"</note>, a manuscript from the end of the 14th century, which is likewise in the possession of the Royal Irish Academy in Dublin, and has also been published in facsimile*, in the facsimile pp. 253–256.

The text of the *Book of the Dun Cow* has already been edited by Stokes, with an English translation and valuable notes, Simla 1870 (see *Grammar Celtica*² p. XL). Since this edition is very rare — "Fifty copies privately printed" is stated on the title page — a new printing will not be unnecessary. According to Stokes, the same text exists in a manuscript in the Paris Library, Old Collection, No. 8175.

2. The *Vision of Adamnán* <note>original: "Fís Adamnáin"</note> belongs to the most remarkable products of Old Irish Christian literature. The starting point is formed by the words of the Bible (commented upon more extensively in the *Speckled Book*):
> Great is our Lord, and great is his power, and of his wisdom there is no number. The Lord lifting up the gentle, but bringing the sinners low even to the ground <note>original Latin: "Magnus Dominus noster, et magna virtus ejus, et sapientiae ejus non est numerus. Suscipiens mansuetos Dominus humiliat autem peccatores usque ad terram"</note> (Psalm 147, Verses 5 and 6).

# VII The Vision of Adamnán.

<note>original: "Fís Adamnáin"</note>

is illustrated by what Adamnán once saw. For the secrets of Heaven and Hell were revealed to the Apostles and other saints, but most recently to Adamnán. On the Feast of John the Baptist, his soul left its body and, guided by its guardian angel, beheld first the Kingdom of Heaven (Chapter 4) and then Hell (Chapter 21). Both realms are described in detail, for before the soul returned to its body, Adamnán received the command to preach to both monks and laypeople what awaits the good and the evil in the afterlife. Adamnán preached of this in the great assembly when the Law of Adamnán <note>The "Lex Innocentium," an 8th-century legal code protecting non-combatants</note> was introduced; Patrick preached of it earlier, as did Peter, Paul, and the other Apostles; so too did Pope Sylvester in the great assembly in which he received Rome from Constantine, and Fabian, who converted Emperor Philip, and Elijah under the Tree of Life in Paradise (Chapter 31 ff.). Stokes has already pointed out here and there the manifold relationships between this tract and Dante's *Divine Comedy*.

3. When Stokes draws attention to the grammatical peculiarities which characterize the text before us as Middle Irish*, and concludes from this character of the language that "The piece cannot therefore be older than the eleventh century," this conclusion is not correct. Since we know that Irish texts were updated by every new scribe—

---

* As such phenomena, Stokes mentions: "confusion of *e* and *i* in endings; putting *ae* for *oe*, *nd* for *nn*, *ur* for *air*, *er*, and *ll* for *ld*, *ln*; addition of an initial *f*, metathesis <note>the transposition of sounds</note> of *cs* and *ts*; final *a* for *ae*, *ai*, *e*; in the article, disuse of the neuter forms of the nominative and accusative singular and use of *na* for the nominative plural masculine, the dual, and the dative plural; in the adjectives, use of the feminine form in the nominative plural masculine, and in the case of verbs compounded with prepositions, use of absolute instead of subjoined forms." These remarks refer primarily to the text in the *Book of the Dun Cow* <note>original: "Lebor na hUidre," a 12th-century manuscript</note>, but also apply to the text in the *Speckled Book* <note>original: "Lebor Brec," a 15th-century manuscript</note>. It should be noted, however, that in the latter, the old diphthong *oe* is very often preserved.

# VII The Vision of Adamnán <note>original: "Fís Adamnáin"</note>

Since prose texts were rewritten more or less consistently into the language of their own time, the actual age of a text cannot be determined solely from its linguistic form. <term>Adamnán's</term> name offers a certain point of reference for the age of our text. <term>Adamnán</term>* was the tenth abbot of <term>Iona</term> <note>A small island off the coast of Scotland, a major center of Gaelic monasticism</note> after <term>Colum Cille</term> <note>Also known as Saint Columba</note>, and died in the year 703 according to the <term>Annals of the Four Masters</term> (<term>O'Curry</term>, <term>On the Manuscript Materials p. 342</term>). However, our text does not present itself as the direct work of <term>Adamnán</term>; rather, it contains at its core a narrative of what <term>Adamnán</term> used to preach based on the revelation granted to him. That the author lived further from the time of <term>Adamnán</term> is evident from the fact that he (Chapter 32) describes King <term>Finnachta Fledach</term> as being involved in the so-called Law of <term>Adamnán</term>, whereas this law is said to have been introduced only after <term>Finnachta's</term> death.**

Similarly, the mention of the fable (Chapter 32) that Rome had already been gifted to the Pope by Constantine speaks against a very early date, at least for the final parts of our text. This fable first emerged in the 8th century.*** On the other hand, the mention of the tithe (Chapter 25) would not necessarily argue against the 8th century, even if it was only introduced in Ireland after 703, as <term>Stokes</term> (following <term>Reeves</term>) points out. All these points have already been discussed by <term>Stokes</term>, partly in the Preface and partly in notes on the relevant passages. Nevertheless, these things do not prove that our text originated as late as the 11th century. Why not in the 10th or 9th century?

Not to be confused with this <term>Vision of Adamnán</term> is another <term>Vision which Adamnán, a man full of the Holy Spirit, saw</term> <note>original Latin: "Visio quae vidit Adamnanus vir spiritu sancto plenus"</note>, which—

---

\* <term>Adamnán ua Tinne</term> (i.e., <term>Adamnán</term>, grandson of <term>Tinne</term>) is the full name. <term>O'Curry</term>, <term>On the Manuscript Materials p. 608</term> erroneously has "Adamnán o Atínní" ("the grandson of Atinni").

\** The dates do admittedly fluctuate in the tradition. According to the <term>Annals of the Four Masters</term>, which <term>Stokes</term> follows, the following dates result: 695 King <term>Finnachta</term> dies; 697 <term>Adamnán's</term> Law; 703 <term>Adamnán</term> dies. According to the <term>Chronicon Scotorum</term>: 691 King <term>Fínsnachta</term> dies; 693 <term>Adamnán's</term> Law; 700 <term>Adamnán</term> dies.

\*** See <term>Döllinger</term>, <term>Fables respecting the Popes p. 61 ff.</term> <note>original: "Papst-Fabeln"</note>

<summary>This page discusses the historical dating of the "Vision of Adamnán," arguing that while the text claims a connection to the 7th-century Abbot of Iona, internal anachronisms suggest a later composition between the 8th and 10th centuries.</summary>

# VII The Vision of Adamnán

A prophecy of a terrible plague, which O'Curry discusses in *On the Manuscript Materials of Ancient Irish History*, page 424. The Latin text with the Irish commentary is found in the *Lebor Brecc* <note>The "Speckled Book," a 15th-century Irish manuscript</note>, page 258b to page 259b.

4. My expansion of the manuscript abbreviations is not indicated by the typeface except in the following cases (cf. above, p. 66): 1) in *ocus* <note>Irish for "and"</note>, which is written out in full only a few times in both manuscripts; 2) in *for* <note>Irish preposition "on/upon"</note>, usually indicated by an *f* with a stroke above it; 3) in *acht* <note>Irish for "but" or "except"</note>, which in the *Lebor Brecc* is very often expressed by an *s* with a stroke above it, whether it be the conjunction *acht* (equivalent to the Latin *sed* <note>original: "sed"</note>) or simply the letter combination *acht* within a word (e.g., *cumachta* <note>power</note>). On the other hand, italics are used to indicate when the syllable *et* within a word (e.g., *forcetul* <note>teaching/instruction</note>) is expressed in the *Lebor Brecc* by the symbol for the Latin conjunction *et* <note>the ampersand or Tironian et</note>, because this symbol is also used on page 254b, line 24 of the *Lebor Brecc* for the syllable *ed* in *medon* <note>middle</note>.

Regarding graphic peculiarities in the *Lebor Brecc*, the following should also be mentioned: 1) the abbreviation for the Latin *autem* <note>original: "autem"; meaning "however"</note> (an *h* with a hook or stroke) is often found where the *Lebor na hUidre* <note>The "Book of the Dun Cow," an 12th-century manuscript</note> has *im.* (that is, *immorro* <note>Irish for "moreover/however"</note>); 2) the length mark is placed over syllables that are long by position (e.g., *múintir* <note>people/household</note>); 3) an *f* with a dot <note>the punctum delens</note> or the aspiration mark above it indicates not only the loss of this sound occurring after vowels, but also its voiced pronunciation <note>eclipsis</note> caused by the original nasal ending of the preceding word. To prevent misunderstandings, I list the individual cases here and leave them unmarked in the text: *co ḟlescaib*, *co ḟleisc* <note>with rods</note> p. 254b lines 52 and 84 (Chapters 15, 18); *i ḟilet* <note>in which are</note> p. 254a line 31 (Chapter 4), *i ḟiadnaise* <note>in the presence of</note> p. 254a lines 50 and 64, p. 254b line 58 (Chapters 6, 7, 16), *i ḟhocus* <note>nearby</note> p. 255a line 54 (Chapter 25), *tresan ḟhial n-gloinide* <note>through the crystalline veil</note> p. 255b line 85 (Chapter 32); *na ḟial sin* (Genitive Plural) p. 254b line 36 (Chapter 14), *a ḟulang* (Possessive Pronoun Plural) p. 255a line 70 (Chapter 26); *i toltnaigi ḟhognuma* <note>in the pleasure of service</note> p. 255a line 45 (Chapter 23). Likewise, *docum talman* <note>towards the earth</note> is found in isolation on p. 253b line 51. In modern orthography, one would write: *co bh-flescaib*, *dochum d-talman*.

## The Vision of Adamnán follows here.

1. Noble and wonderful is the Lord of the elements, and great and amazing is His strength and His power. He is gentle and kind, merciful and loving. For He summons to Him toward heaven the people of charity and mercy, the...

---

## The Vision of Adamnán begins.

<margin>[From the Leabhar Breac:]</margin> "Great is our Lord and great is His power, <margin>5</margin> and of His wisdom there is no number." <note>original Latin: "Magnus dominus noster et magna virtus ejus et sapientiae ejus non est numerus." A quote from Psalm 147:5.</note> Noble and wonderful is the Lord, great and amazing is His strength and His power; there is neither limit nor number to the abundance of His wisdom or His great knowledge. "The Lord lifting up the meek, but bringing the sinners down even to the earth." <note>original Latin: "Suscipiens mansuetos dominus humiliat hautem peccatores usque ad terram." From Psalm 147:6.</note> The <margin>10</margin> Lord, then, summons toward Him to heaven the people of charity and mercy and gentleness and kindness. He casts down and prostrates to the earth and to hell the unprofitable assembly of the sons of malediction. The Holy Spirit, the Spirit from whom comes every consolation and every counsel to both churches—the Old <margin>15</margin> Testament and the New Testament—it is that Spirit who enlightened the gifts and the secrets of the divine laws for the Church throughout the whole world; <term>cre</term> <note>The scribe likely intended "cé," meaning "here" or "this side of the world."</note> it is that Spirit who breathed these words through the royal prophet, namely David son of Jesse, saying: "Great is our Lord." Great and vast is our Lord, the Lord Jesus Christ, that is: "no one <margin>20</margin> can offer Him worthy praise." <note>original Latin: "nemo potest ei dignam laudem afferre."</note> This is the same as saying that it is not possible for anyone to perform praise that is sufficient for Him. "Greater than all praise is the power by which He created the nature of things and sustains the heavy mass of the earth." <note>original Latin: "Major est omni laude virtus qua condidit naturam rerum et sustinet molem gravem terram."</note> For He surpasses every praise in His eternal strength and power, by which He brought forth the <margin>25</margin> nature of the elements and is sustaining the size and weight of the earth without any movement upon them. "And of His wisdom there is no number," that is: "the Father knows the quality of all things." <note>original Latin: "Et sapientiae ejus non est numerus i. e. pater cognoscit qualitatem omnium rerum."</note> Many are the insights and the knowledge of the Lord; this is the same as saying that though the elements are many and though they are diverse, there is nothing in them that would be <margin>30</margin> in obscurity to Him. "The Lord lifting up the meek," that is...

---

<note>Line 31: "obscurity" or "unclear" in the Leabhar Breac manuscript.</note>

# VII The Vision of Adamnán

<margin>[Lebor na hUidre:]</margin>
Of the high scholar of the western world <note>Adamnán (c. 624–704 AD), Abbot of Iona and author of the Life of Saint Columba.</note>, it is spoken here: of how his soul departed from his body on the feast day of John the Baptist, and how it was borne toward the Kingdom of Heaven with the angels of heaven, and to Hell with its rabble-host. There are diverse and wonderful assemblies there, with chasubles <note>term: chasuble (casla); a liturgical vestment, here representing the white robes of the elect.</note> of white linen around them, and pure white hoods above their heads.
<margin>10</margin> The saints of the East of the world are in a separate assembly in the East of the Land of the Saints. The saints of the West of the world are in the West of the same land. The saints of the North of the world and of its South are in two vast assemblies to the South and the North. Everyone, then, who is in the Land of the Saints is near enough to hear the music
<margin>15</margin> and to contemplate the vessel <note>original: "luinge"; likely referring to the "ship" or "station" of the celestial hierarchy.</note> in which are the nine ranks of heaven according to their steps and according to their order.

### VII The Vision of Adamnán

->[From the Book of the Dun Cow:]<-

5. At one time, then, the saints sing wonderful music praising God; at another time, they listen to the music of the people of heaven. For the saints have no need of anything else but listening to the music to which they hearken, and contemplating the light they behold, and being satisfied by the fragrance that is in the land. There is, moreover, a wonderful kingdom <note>original: "flaith"</note> face to face with them to the southeast, and a glass veil <note>original: "fial glainide"</note> between them and a golden porch to the south. It is through this that they perceive the form and shadow of the people of heaven. There is, however, no veil or darkness between the people of heaven and the saints, but they are in light and in presence toward them always. A fiery circle, moreover, is around that land all about, and everyone goes in and out and it does not harm them.

6. The twelve apostles, moreover, and the Virgin Mary in a separate assembly around the powerful Lord. Patriarchs <note>original: "Úasalathraig"</note> and prophets and disciples of Jesus are in close proximity to the apostles. There are also certain holy virgins to the right of Mary and no great distance between them.

<note>Note on line 6: The manuscript LU reads "dogníus" (face) here.</note>

---

->[From the Speckled Book:]<-

5. At one time, then, the saints sing wonderful music praising God; at another time, they listen to the music of the people of heaven. For the saints do not require anything else but listening to the music to which they hearken, and contemplating the light they see, and being satisfied by the fragrance that is in that land. There is, then, a wonderful kingdom before their faces to the southeast. A glass veil is between them and a golden porch to the south, and through this they perceive the shapes and shadows of the people of heaven. There is, however, no darkness at all between the people of heaven and the saints, but they are in light and in presence toward them always. A fiery circle, then, was around <gloss>as written</gloss> that people all about; everyone goes in and out and it does not harm any of them.

6. The twelve apostles and the Virgin Mary are in a separate assembly around the powerful Lord. Patriarchs and prophets and disciples of Jesus Christ are in close proximity to the apostles. There are also holy virgins to the right of Mary and a small space between them. Infants, moreover, and youths are around them on every side, and the music of the birds of the people of heaven <insert>continues on next page</insert>.

# VII. The Vision of Adamnán

<margin>From the Book of the Dun Cow</margin>
Infants and youths are around them on every side, and the music of the birds of the heavenly host making melody for them. Splendid and swift companies of guardian angels of the souls are in humble service and attendance between those assemblies in the presence of the King forever. No one in this present world is able <margin>5</margin> to give a description *or* an account of those assemblies, *as* they truly are. The companies and the assemblies that are in the land of the saints, *as* we have said, will remain in that great glory until the Great Assembly of Judgment, until the Righteous Judge shall station them on the Day of Doom in the stations and in the places where they will be <margin>10</margin> beholding the face of God without veil or shadow between them through the world of worlds <note>original: "tria bithu na m-betha" — a common liturgical formula for "forever and ever".</note>.

7. That royal throne is like a fashioned chair with four columns of precious stone beneath it. Although...

# VII. The Vision of Adamnán

<margin>[Book of the Dun Cow:]</margin>
...towards the ranks of heaven. For no one could describe His glow and His power, His redness <note>original: "derge"; likely referring to the brilliant, fiery radiance of the divine</note> and His great brightness, His delight and His sweetness, His elegance and His stability, and the multitude of His angels and archangels singing music to Him. His messengers are very numerous, coming to Him and going from Him with very brief messages for each group in turn; His gentleness and great kindness toward some, and His harshness and great severity toward others.

10. If anyone were to be constantly gazing at Him from the east and the west, from the south and the north, they would find on every side of Him a noble face, seven times brighter than the sun. You would not see the form of a human upon Him, neither head nor feet, but rather a fiery mass flaming throughout the world, with everyone trembling and in fear before Him. Heaven and earth are full of His light, and a radiance like a royal star surrounds Him. Three thousand different songs are performed by every single choir <term>classchétol: choral singing or harmony</term> that is around Him. Each individual song of those is itself as sweet as all the various musics of the world.

---

<margin>[The Speckled Book:]</margin>
...towards the ranks of heaven. For no other person could describe His glow, His power, His charity <note>original: "dérc"; the scribe here uses a word for 'charity' or 'love' where the other version uses 'redness'</note>, His great brightness, His delight, His sweetness, His elegance, His stability, and the multitude of His angels and archangels singing music to Him.
<margin>20</margin> His very numerous servants coming to Him and going from Him with brief messages for each group in turn; His gentleness and great kindness toward some, and His harshness and severity toward others.

10. If anyone were to be looking at Him from the east and the west, from the south and <note>(page 254b:)</note> the north, they would find on every side His noble face, seven times brighter than the sun. You would not see a human shape upon Him, of head or feet, but as a red fiery mass flaming throughout the world, and everyone trembling and in fear before Him. Heaven and earth are full of His brightness, and a radiance like a royal star is in His great surroundings. Three thousand different songs are performed by every single choir that is singing around Him, and each song of those is as sweet as the many musics of the world.

# VII. The Vision of Adamnán

<margin>From the Book of the Dun Cow (LU)</margin>
11. The city, then, in which that royal seat <note>original: *rígsudi*; the throne of God</note> stands, is fashioned thus: there are **seven walls of glass** of diverse colors surrounding it, each wall higher than the one before it. Moreover, the floor and the lowest foundation of the city are made of white glass with the appearance of sunlight upon it, variegated with blue, purple, green, and every other color besides.

12. **A gentle, very mild, and very kind people**, lacking no good thing, are they who inhabit that city. For none reach it and none inhabit it forever except holy virgins <insert>or</insert> devoted pilgrims of God. Their arrangement and their order, however, it is difficult to know how it was brought about; for the back of none of them <insert>or</insert> their side is turned toward another. Rather, it is (p. 28b:) thus that the unspeakable power of the Lord has arranged and preserved them: face to face in rows and in equal circles <note>original: *corónib*, literally "crowns" or "tonsures," here referring to circular ranks</note> all around the royal seat, in delight and in beauty, with all their faces toward God.

# VII The Vision of Adomnán

<margin>[LU:] <note>Lebor na hUidre (The Book of the Dun Cow), c. 1100 AD</note></margin>
...exquisite, of red gold and silver upon it, with beautiful rows of precious stones and a variety of sparkling gems, and with seats and crowns of carbuncle <note>a deep red gemstone, often associated with divine light</note> upon those chancel-rails. There are three precious stones moreover, with a smooth sound and with the sweetness of music <margin>5</margin> between every two main sections, and their upper halves are like lamps aflame. Seven thousand angels in the forms of chief-candles are illuminating and enlightening the city all around; seven thousand others in its very center are flaming forever around the royal city. If all the men of the world were in one place, numerous though they are, <margin>10</margin> the fragrance of the head of just one of those candles would satisfy them as food.

14. Now, as for those people of the world who do not reach that city while they are alive, but for whom it is destined to be their dwelling after the verdict of Doom <note>The Last Judgment</note>, they dwell unsteadily and restlessly in heights and on hills, in marshes and in moors <margin>15</margin> until Judgment comes to them.
This is how those hosts and assemblies are, and—
